    Common mistakes: expecting the domain of convergence to be a polydisc
    (true only in one variable, where it is a disc); confusing it with the
    largest domain where the sum is holomorphic. Now you try: domain of
    ∑ₙ zⁿw²ⁿ. ({|z||w|² < 1})

D4. (a) Let f be holomorphic near 0 ∈ C², f(0) = 0, f ≢ 0. Prove that 0
    is NOT an isolated zero. (b) Describe the zeros of f = w² − z and
    g = z² + w² near 0. (c) Find max |z + w + zw| over the closed unit
    bidisc and say where it is attained.
    Answer: (a) argument principle in w, uniformly in z. (b) w = ±√z (two
    for each z ≠ 0); two lines w = ±iz. (c) 3, at (1, 1) only.
    Teacher explains: (a) After a linear change of coordinates f(0, w) ≢
    0 (some complex line through 0 is not inside {f = 0}). Its zeros are
    isolated, so choose r > 0 with f(0, w) ≠ 0 for 0 < |w| ≤ r. By
    continuity and compactness of {|w| = r}, f(z, w) ≠ 0 for |z| < δ,
    |w| = r. For such z let
       N(z) = (2πi)⁻¹ ∮_{|w|=r} (∂f/∂w)(z, w)/f(z, w) dw,
    the number of zeros of f(z, ·) in |w| < r. N is continuous and
    integer-valued, hence constant, and N(0) ≥ 1. So EVERY z with |z| < δ
    has a zero (z, w(z)): a whole family of zeros through 0. (b) w² − z = 0
    gives two roots ±√z for each z ≠ 0, one (double) at z = 0: N ≡ 2.
    z² + w² = (w − iz)(w + iz): the union of two complex lines, crossing
    at 0. (c) For fixed w, z ↦ f is holomorphic, so its maximum on |z| ≤ 1
    lies on |z| = 1; the same in w: the max is on the TORUS. There |z + w
    + zw| ≤ 3, with equality iff z, w and zw point the same way, i.e.
    z = w = 1. Common mistakes: "the maximum principle puts the max on the
    whole topological boundary" (true but weak: the torus suffices); in
    (a) forgetting to make f(0, w) ≢ 0 (f = z vanishes identically on the
    w-axis). Now you try: zeros of z³ − w² near 0? (the cusp w = ±z^(3/2),
    two points over each z ≠ 0)

D5. HARTOGS FIGURE. Let 0 < a, b < 1 and H = {(z, w) ∈ D² : |z| < a or
    |w| > b}. Prove that every f holomorphic on H extends holomorphically
    to the bidisc D², in two ways: (i) Cauchy integral in w, (ii) Laurent
    series in w.
    Answer: (i) F(z, w) = (2πi)⁻¹∮_{|η|=r} f(z, η)/(η − w) dη, b < r < 1;
    (ii) negative Laurent coefficients vanish for |z| < a, hence always.
    Teacher explains: Draw H in the (|z|, |w|) square [0, 1)²: an "L"
    made of the vertical strip |z| < a and the top strip |w| > b.
    (i) Fix r with b < r < 1. For every |z| < 1 the circle {z} × {|η| = r}
    lies in H (because r > b), so
       F(z, w) = (2πi)⁻¹ ∮_{|η|=r} f(z, η)/(η − w) dη
    is defined for |z| < 1, |w| < r; it is continuous and holomorphic in z
    and in w separately (differentiate under the integral), hence
    holomorphic on D × D_r. For |z| < a the whole closed disc {z} ×
    {|η| ≤ r} lies in H, so the one-variable Cauchy formula gives F = f.
    The set H ∩ (D × D_r) = {|z| < a, |w| < r} ∪ {|z| < 1, b < |w| < r} is
    connected, F = f on an open subset, so F = f on all of it (identity
    theorem). Hence "f on H, F on D × D_r" is a single holomorphic
    function on H ∪ (D × D_r) = D². (ii) On {|z| < 1, b < |w| < 1} write
    f(z, w) = ∑_{k∈Z} aₖ(z)wᵏ, aₖ(z) = (2πi)⁻¹∮_{|w|=r} f(z, w)w^(−k−1) dw,
    holomorphic on |z| < 1. For |z| < a, f(z, ·) is holomorphic on the
    whole disc, so aₖ(z) = 0 for k < 0; by the identity theorem aₖ ≡ 0 for
    k < 0, and ∑_{k≥0} aₖ(z)wᵏ is the extension. Common mistakes: "f
    extends because D² ∖ H is small" (it has interior! the point is the
    SHAPE); forgetting to check that the circles of integration stay in
    H. Now you try: why is f = 1/(w − c), |c| < b, not a counterexample?
    (it is not holomorphic on H: the points (z, c) with |z| < a lie in H)

D6. State Hartogs' extension theorem and prove it assuming the lemma:
    (n ≥ 2) every compactly supported smooth ∂̄-closed (0,1)-form g on Cⁿ
    equals ∂̄v for some compactly supported smooth v. Deduce: (a) C² ∖ {0}
    is not a domain of holomorphy; (b) a holomorphic function of n ≥ 2
    variables has no isolated singularities and no isolated zeros.
    Answer: cut off near K with χ, correct by v; (a) every f extends to
    C²; (b) apply it to f and to 1/f.
    Teacher explains: THEOREM: Ω ⊆ Cⁿ (n ≥ 2) a domain, K ⊂ Ω compact,
    Ω ∖ K connected; then every f ∈ 𝒪(Ω ∖ K) extends to F ∈ 𝒪(Ω). PROOF:
    pick χ ∈ C_c^∞(Ω) with χ = 1 near K (and Ω ∖ supp χ connected). The
    function f₀ = (1 − χ)f (set f₀ = 0 on K) is smooth on Ω, and
       ∂̄f₀ = −f ∂̄χ =: g,
    which is smooth, ∂̄-closed (∂̄g = ∂̄∂̄f₀ = 0) and supported in the
    compact set supp ∂̄χ ⊂ Ω ∖ K; extend it by 0 to Cⁿ. Take v with ∂̄v =
    g, v compactly supported, and put F = f₀ − v. Then ∂̄F = g − g = 0: F
    is holomorphic on Ω. Outside supp χ, v is holomorphic (∂̄v = 0) and
    vanishes for |z| large; the unbounded component of Cⁿ ∖ supp χ is
    connected, so v = 0 there, in particular on a non-empty open part of
    Ω ∖ supp χ near ∂Ω. There F = f₀ = f, and by connectedness F = f on
    Ω ∖ K. (a) Take Ω = C², K = {0}: every f ∈ 𝒪(C² ∖ {0}) is the
    restriction of an entire function, so no function "blows up" at 0.
    (b) If f is holomorphic on a ball minus its centre, it extends; if f
    had an isolated zero at a, 1/f would be holomorphic on a punctured
    ball, extend, and then f·(1/f) = 1 near a contradicts f(a) = 0. E.g.
    the singular set of 1/(z² + w²) is the pair of lines w = ±iz, never a
    point. Common mistakes: forgetting the connectedness of Ω ∖ K (for K =
    a sphere ∂B ⊂ C² the complement B ∪ (C² ∖ B̄) is disconnected);
    thinking the theorem holds for n = 1 (1/z). Now you try: is C² minus a
    closed ball a domain of holomorphy? (no: every f on it extends to an
    entire function)

D7. (a) Prove that every convex domain Ω ⊆ Cⁿ is a domain of holomorphy.
    (b) Compute the holomorphic hull (with respect to C²) of the circles
    K₁ = {(λ/2, λ/2) : |λ| = 1} and K₂ = {(λ/2, λ̄/2) : |λ| = 1}, and of
    the torus K₃ = {|z| = |w| = ½}. (c) State the Cartan-Thullen theorem.
    Answer: (a) f_p = 1/(ℓ(z) − ℓ(p)) blows up at each p ∈ ∂Ω. (b) K̂₁ =
    the disc {(ζ, ζ) : |ζ| ≤ ½}; K̂₂ = K₂; K̂₃ = the closed bidisc of radius
    ½. (c) domain of holomorphy ⇔ holomorphically convex.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let p ∈ ∂Ω. A real supporting hyperplane at p
    has the form Re ℓ(z) = Re ℓ(p) with ℓ complex linear (every real
    linear functional is Re of a complex linear one), and Re ℓ(z) <
    Re ℓ(p) on Ω. So f_p(z) = 1/(ℓ(z) − ℓ(p)) is holomorphic on Ω and
    |f_p| → ∞ as z → p: f_p cannot be continued to any neighbourhood of p.
    (Via Cartan-Thullen, or by combining countably many f_p, no function
    family extends anywhere: Ω is a domain of holomorphy.) (b) K₁ lies on
    the complex line w = z. For a polynomial P, λ ↦ P(λ/2, λ/2) is
    holomorphic in λ, so by the maximum principle |P| on the disc
    {(ζ, ζ) : |ζ| ≤ ½} is ≤ max_K₁ |P|: that disc is in K̂₁. Conversely
    the functions z − w and z bound K̂₁ inside it (|z − w| ≤ 0, |z| ≤ ½).
    K₂ lies on the curve zw = ¼; using |zw − ¼| ≤ 0, |z| ≤ ½, |w| ≤ ½ we
    get |z| = |w| = ½ and w = 1/(4z) = z̄: K̂₂ = K₂. The same circle shape,
    different position, completely different hull! K₃: the maximum
    principle on polydiscs gives the closed bidisc of radius ½ ⊆ K̂₃, and
    |z|, |w| ≤ ½ give equality. (c) CARTAN-THULLEN: for Ω ⊆ Cⁿ TFAE: Ω is
    a domain of holomorphy; Ω is holomorphically convex (K ⊂⊂ Ω ⇒ K̂_Ω ⊂⊂
    Ω); there is one f ∈ 𝒪(Ω) that extends past no boundary point. Key
    step: dist(K̂, ∂Ω) = dist(K, ∂Ω) on a domain of holomorphy (Cauchy
    estimates on polydiscs). Common mistakes: using the polynomial hull
    when Ω ≠ Cⁿ (the hull depends on Ω: take all f ∈ 𝒪(Ω)); believing
    "hull = convex hull" (K̂₂ is not convex). Now you try: hull of the
    sphere {|z|² + |w|² = ¼} with respect to Ω = C² ∖ {0}. ({0 < |z|² +
    |w|² ≤ ¼}, not compact in Ω, so Ω is not holomorphically convex)

D8. (a) Show that a C² function φ is plurisubharmonic iff its complex
    Hessian (φ_jk̄) = (∂²φ/∂zⱼ∂z̄ₖ) is positive semidefinite. (b) For which
    real c is φ = |z|² + |w|² + c Re(zw̄) psh on C²? strictly psh? (c) Show
    that log|f| is psh for f holomorphic, and that ψ = |z|² + t Re(z²) is
    psh for every t but convex only for |t| ≤ 1.
    Answer: (a) Δ_λ φ(a + λb) = 4∑φ_jk̄ bⱼb̄ₖ. (b) psh ⇔ |c| ≤ 2, strictly
    ⇔ |c| < 2. (c) restriction to lines; ψ = (1 + t)x² + (1 − t)y².
    Teacher explains: (a) Put u(λ) = φ(a + λb). By the chain rule
    ∂u/∂λ̄ = ∑ₖ φ_k̄ b̄ₖ and ∂²u/∂λ∂λ̄ = ∑ⱼₖ φ_jk̄ bⱼb̄ₖ, and Δ_λ = 4∂²/∂λ∂λ̄.
    A C² function of one variable is subharmonic iff Δu ≥ 0. So φ is psh
    ⇔ ∑φ_jk̄(a)bⱼb̄ₖ ≥ 0 for all a, b ⇔ the Hermitian matrix is psd.
    (b) φ = zz̄ + ww̄ + (c/2)(zw̄ + z̄w). Then φ_zz̄ = 1, φ_ww̄ = 1,
    ∂φ/∂w̄ = w + (c/2)z, so φ_zw̄ = ∂/∂z(w + (c/2)z) = c/2 = φ_wz̄.
    Matrix [[1, c/2], [c/2, 1]], eigenvalues 1 ± c/2:
    psd ⇔ |c| ≤ 2, pd ⇔ |c| < 2. At c = 2, φ = |z + w|², psh but
    degenerate in the direction (1, −1). (c) On a line, log|f(a + λb)| is
    log|h(λ)| with h holomorphic in λ, which is subharmonic (harmonic
    where h ≠ 0, −∞ at zeros; the sub-mean value inequality holds there
    trivially). ψ = x² + y² + t(x² − y²) = (1 + t)x² + (1 − t)y²:
    ψ_zz̄ = ¼Δψ = 1 > 0 always (Re z² is pluriharmonic), but the real
    Hessian diag(2 + 2t, 2 − 2t) is psd only for |t| ≤ 1. Since
    biholomorphisms preserve psh (φ ∘ F is psh: the complex Hessian
    transforms as J*HJ) but not convexity, psh is the right notion of
    convexity for complex analysis. Common mistakes: using the real
    Hessian; forgetting that the complex Hessian is Hermitian (φ_wz̄ =
    conj(φ_zw̄)). Now you try: is (Re z)(Re w) psh? (no: Hessian
    [[0, ¼], [¼, 0]] is indefinite)

D9. Let Ω = {ρ < 0}, ρ = |z|² + |w|⁴ − 1 (the "egg"). (a) Define the Levi
    form and compute it at p₁ = (0, 1), p₂ = (1, 0), p₃ = (1/√2, 2^(−1/4)).
    Is Ω strictly pseudoconvex? (b) Show that the sign of the Levi form on
    complex tangent vectors does not depend on the defining function.
    (c) Show that a convex domain with C² boundary is Levi pseudoconvex.
    Answer: (a) L(p₁) = 1·|t|², L(p₂) = 0, L(p₃)(t) = 2√2 for t = (2^(1/4),
    −1/√2); pseudoconvex, strictly except on the circle {w = 0, |z| = 1}.
    Teacher explains: (a) At p ∈ ∂Ω the COMPLEX TANGENT space is T_p =
    {t ∈ C² : ∑ ρ_zⱼ(p)tⱼ = 0}, and the LEVI FORM is L_p(t) = ∑ ρ_jk̄(p)tⱼt̄ₖ
    on T_p. Here ρ_z = z̄, ρ_w = 2|w|²w̄ (since |w|⁴ = w²w̄²), ρ_zz̄ = 1,
    ρ_ww̄ = 4|w|², ρ_zw̄ = 0. At p₁: ∂ρ = (0, 2), T = {t₂ = 0}, L = |t₁|² >
    0. At p₂: ∂ρ = (1, 0), T = {t₁ = 0}, L = 4|w|²|t₂|² = 0: weakly (not
    strictly) pseudoconvex. At p₃: |z|² = ½, |w|⁴ = ½, |w|² = 2^(−1/2);
    ∂ρ = (2^(−1/2), 2·2^(−1/2)·2^(−1/4)) = (2^(−1/2), 2^(1/4)); t = (ρ_w,
    −ρ_z) = (2^(1/4), −2^(−1/2)) is tangent and
       L = 1·2^(1/2) + 4·2^(−1/2)·½ = √2 + √2 = 2√2 ≈ 2.828 > 0.
    In general L = |t₁|² + 4|w|²|t₂|² ≥ 0, = 0 for t ≠ 0 only when w = 0.
    (b) If ρ̃ = hρ with h > 0, then at p (ρ = 0)
       ρ̃_jk̄ = hρ_jk̄ + h_j ρ_k̄ + ρ_j h_k̄,
    and for t ∈ T_p: ∑ ρ_j tⱼ = 0 and ∑ ρ_k̄ t̄ₖ = conj(∑ ρ_k tₖ) = 0 (ρ
    real), so L̃ = hL: same sign. (c) Write t = u ∈ R⁴ in real terms; T_p
    is invariant under multiplication by i, so u and iu are both real
    tangent vectors, and one checks L_p(t) = ¼(D²ρ(u, u) + D²ρ(iu, iu)) ≥
    0, because a convex defining function has D²ρ ≥ 0 on the tangent
    space. Common mistakes: computing the Levi form on ALL vectors (the
    egg's ρ has psd Hessian everywhere, but in general only T_p counts);
    confusing weak pseudoconvexity with non-pseudoconvexity. Now you try:
    Levi form of ρ = |z|² − Im w (the Siegel domain) at 0. (|t₁|², on
    T = {t₂ = 0}: strictly pseudoconvex)

D10. (a) State the Levi problem and its solution. (b) Let U = (D × D_¼) ∪
     (D_¼ × D) ⊂ C² (D_r = disc of radius r). Show U is not a domain of
     holomorphy and find its envelope of holomorphy. (c) For which φ is
     the Hartogs domain Ω_φ = {z ∈ D, |w| < e^(−φ(z))} pseudoconvex?
     Decide for φ = |z|² and φ = −|z|².
     Answer: (a) pseudoconvex ⇔ domain of holomorphy. (b) E(U) = {|z| <
     1, |w| < 1, |zw| < ¼}. (c) φ subharmonic: yes for |z|², no for
     −|z|².
     Teacher explains: (a) Every domain of holomorphy is pseudoconvex
     (−log dist(z, ∂Ω) is psh, by Cartan-Thullen). LEVI PROBLEM: is every
     pseudoconvex domain a domain of holomorphy? YES (Oka for n = 2 in
     1942; general n by Oka 1953, Bremermann and Norguet 1954; Hörmander's
     L² ∂̄-method, 1965, gives a modern proof). (b) U is a complete
     Reinhardt domain; its log image is the L-shaped region {x < 0, y <
     log ¼} ∪ {x < log ¼, y < 0}, not convex. The Taylor series at 0 of any
     f ∈ 𝒪(U) converges on U (it converges on each polydisc in U), so by
     D3 it converges on the smallest log-convex complete Reinhardt domain
     containing U. The convex hull of the L-shape is {x < 0, y < 0, x + y
     < log ¼}: the segment joining the corners (0, log ¼) and (log ¼, 0)
     lies on x + y = log ¼. So every f extends to
        E = {|z| < 1, |w| < 1, |zw| < ¼},
     and E is itself a domain of holomorphy (log-convex), e.g. 1/(1 −
     4zw) does not extend past {|zw| = ¼}. Check: (0.7, 0.3) ∉ U (0.7 >
     ¼ and 0.3 > ¼) but |zw| = 0.21 < 0.25, so it is in E. (c) Ω_φ =
     {(z, w) : z ∈ D, log|w| + φ(z) < 0}. The function log|w| + φ(z) is psh
     iff φ is subharmonic; then (φ continuous) −log(−log|w| − φ(z)) −
     log(1 − |z|²) is a psh exhaustion, so Ω_φ is pseudoconvex;
     conversely pseudoconvexity forces φ subharmonic.
     φ = |z|² (Δφ = 4 ≥ 0): pseudoconvex. φ = −|z|² is superharmonic:
     Ω = {|w| < e^(|z|²)} is not pseudoconvex. Common mistakes: taking the
     convex hull of U itself instead of its log image; thinking the union
     of two domains of holomorphy is one. Now you try: envelope of (D ×
     D) ∪ (D₂ × D_½)? ({|z| < 2, |w| < 1, |z||w| < 1}; log hull corners
     (0, 0), (log 2, −log 2))

D11. State the Weierstrass preparation theorem and sketch its proof. For
     f(z, w) = w³ + w² + z find the order k, the Weierstrass polynomial P
     and the unit u (series up to z³). State the division theorem and find
     the remainder of e^w on division by w² − z.
     Answer: k = 2; P = w² + (−z + 2z² − 7z³ + ...)w + (z − z² + 3z³ +
     ...), u = w + 1 + z − 2z² + 7z³ + ...; remainder cosh√z +
     w·sinh(√z)/√z.
     Teacher explains: THEOREM: if f is holomorphic near 0 ∈ C^(n−1) × C
     and f(0′, w) = wᵏh(w), h(0) ≠ 0, then near 0, f = u·P with u(0) ≠ 0
     and P = wᵏ + a_(k−1)(z′)w^(k−1) + ... + a₀(z′), aⱼ(0′) = 0; u, P are
     unique. PROOF SKETCH: as in D4 choose r, δ with f ≠ 0 on {|z′| < δ,
     |w| = r}; then f(z′, ·) has exactly k zeros b₁(z′), ..., bₖ(z′) in
     |w| < r. The power sums
        sₘ(z′) = ∑ⱼ bⱼ(z′)ᵐ = (2πi)⁻¹ ∮_{|w|=r} wᵐ (∂f/∂w)/f dw
     are holomorphic in z′; by Newton's identities so are the elementary
     symmetric functions, i.e. the coefficients of P = ∏(w − bⱼ); at z′ = 0
     all bⱼ = 0, so aⱼ(0) = 0. Finally u = f/P has only removable
     singularities in w, and u(z′, w) = (2πi)⁻¹∮ f/(P·(η − w)) dη shows it
     is holomorphic jointly; it never vanishes. EXAMPLE: f(0, w) = w²(1 +
     w): k = 2. Since f is a cubic in w, factor off the root near −1: r₃
     = −1 + ε with ε − 2ε² + ε³ = −z, so ε = −z + 2z² − 7z³ + ... . Then
     w³ + w² + z = (w − r₃)(w² + a₁w + a₀) with a₁ = 1 + r₃ = ε and a₀ =
     r₃a₁; hence a₁ = −z + 2z² − 7z³, a₀ = z − z² + 3z³ (check: −r₃a₀ = z),
     u = w − r₃ = w + 1 + z − 2z² + 7z³. For fixed small z ≠ 0 the two
     small roots of P are the zeros of f near 0 (≈ ±√(−z)). DIVISION: for
     P of degree k, every g = qP + r, deg_w r < k, uniquely. With P = w² −
     z and g = e^w: r = A(z) + B(z)w must agree with e^w at w = ±√z:
     A ± B√z = e^(±√z), so A = cosh√z = ∑zᵐ/(2m)!, B = sinh(√z)/√z =
     ∑zᵐ/(2m + 1)! — both holomorphic, as the theorem promises.
     Consequence: 𝒪ₙ is Noetherian and a UFD (reduce to C{z′}[w] and use
     Hilbert's basis theorem and Gauss's lemma, by induction on n).
     Common mistakes: applying the theorem when f(0′, w) ≡ 0 (f = zw: first
     change coordinates z = z″ + w); forgetting that aⱼ(0) = 0. Now you
     try: prepare f = e^w − 1 − z. (k = 1, P = w − log(1 + z), since the
     zero is w = log(1 + z))

D12. THE ∂̄-PROBLEM. (a) In C solve ∂u/∂z̄ = 𝟙_D (D the unit disc) with
     the Cauchy-Green formula u(z) = (1/π)∫ f(ζ)/(z − ζ) dA(ζ). (b) On C²,
     decide whether f = z̄₂ dz̄₁ + z̄₁ dz̄₂ and g = z̄₂ dz̄₁ are ∂̄-exact.
     (c) Prove: if n = 2 and f = f₁dz̄₁ + f₂dz̄₂ is smooth, compactly
     supported and ∂̄-closed, then ∂̄u = f has a compactly supported
     solution. Why does this fail in C?
     Answer: (a) u = z̄ for |z| ≤ 1, u = 1/z for |z| ≥ 1. (b) f = ∂̄(z̄₁z̄₂);
     g is not ∂̄-closed, so no solution. (c) u = (1/π)∫ f₁(ζ, z₂)/(z₁ − ζ)
     dA(ζ); in C the solution in (a) is 1/z at ∞.
     Teacher explains: (a) E(z) = 1/(πz) is a fundamental solution of
     ∂/∂z̄ (∂E/∂z̄ = δ₀), so u = E * f solves ∂u/∂z̄ = f. For |z| > 1,
     ζ ↦ 1/(z − ζ) is holomorphic on D̄, and the area mean value property
     gives (1/π)∫_D dA/(z − ζ) = (1/π)·π·(1/z) = 1/z. The kernel is
     locally integrable, so u is continuous on C. On D, ∂(u − z̄)/∂z̄ = 1 −
     1 = 0: u − z̄ is holomorphic in D, continuous on D̄, and on |z| = 1
     it equals 1/z − z̄ = 0. By the maximum principle u = z̄ on D. So
     u(½) = ½, u(2) = ½, u(i/2) = −i/2. (b) The compatibility
     condition is ∂f₁/∂z̄₂ = ∂f₂/∂z̄₁ (because ∂̄∂̄u = 0). For f: 1 = 1 ✓,
     and u = z̄₁z̄₂ works (unique up to adding a holomorphic function).
     For g: ∂g₁/∂z̄₂ = 1 ≠ 0 = ∂g₂/∂z̄₁: no solution. (c) Define
        u(z₁, z₂) = (1/π)∫_C f₁(ζ, z₂)/(z₁ − ζ) dA(ζ)
                  = (1/π)∫_C f₁(z₁ − ζ, z₂)/ζ dA(ζ),
     smooth (differentiate the second form under the integral). By (a),
     ∂u/∂z̄₁ = f₁. Using the closedness,
        ∂u/∂z̄₂ = (1/π)∫ (∂f₂/∂ζ̄)(ζ, z₂)/(z₁ − ζ) dA(ζ) = f₂(z₁, z₂),
     by Cauchy-Pompeiu for the compactly supported f₂(·, z₂). If |z₂| is
     large, f₁(·, z₂) ≡ 0, so u = 0 there. Outside a large ball u is
     holomorphic (∂̄u = f = 0) and the complement of the ball is CONNECTED
     (n ≥ 2) and contains points with |z₂| large, so u ≡ 0 outside the
     ball: supp u is compact. In C the analogous u from (a) equals 1/z near
     ∞, and ∫ f ζᵐ dA = 0 for all m ≥ 0 is needed for compact support
     (here ∫_D 1 dA = π ≠ 0). HÖRMANDER'S THEOREM: on a pseudoconvex Ω and
     psh weight φ, every ∂̄-closed f ∈ L²(e^(−φ)) has u with ∫|u|²e^(−φ)(1
     + |z|²)⁻² ≤ ∫|f|²e^(−φ); this yields the solution of the Levi problem.
     Common mistakes: forgetting the compatibility ∂̄f = 0; a sign error in
     the kernel (it is 1/(z − ζ), with the factor 1/π). Now you try: solve
     ∂u/∂z̄₁ = z₂, ∂u/∂z̄₂ = z₁ on C². (u = z̄₁z₂ + z₁z̄₂ = 2Re(z₁z̄₂))

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Wirtinger calculus. Compute what is asked; H = holomorphic, N = not.
    a) z₁z₂: H or N?                   b) z₁z̄₂: ∂/∂z̄₂; H or N?
    c) |z₁|² + z₂: ∂/∂z̄₁               d) e^(z₁z₂) sin z₂: H or N?
    e) Re(z₁)z₂: ∂/∂z̄₁                 f) z₁/(1 − z₂) on {z₂ ≠ 1}: H or N?
    g) x₁ − iy₁ + z₂: H or N?          h) ∂/∂z̄₁ of |z₁|⁴
    i) ∂²/∂z₁∂z̄₁ of log(1 + |z₁|²)     j) ∂/∂z̄₂ of g(z₁ + z̄₂), g entire
    k) a holomorphic f with Re f = x₁² − y₁² + x₂
    l) is x₁x₂ − y₁y₂ the real part of a holomorphic function? of which?
    m) ∂²/∂z₁∂z̄₂ of x₁y₂               n) is |z₁|² − |z₂|² pluriharmonic?
    o) complex Jacobian determinant of F = (z₁², z₂³) at (1, 1)
    p) inverse of the biholomorphism F(z, w) = (z + w², w) of C²
    q) det of the complex Jacobian of (e^z, we^(−z)); is F injective?
    Answers:
      a) H        b) z₁; N     c) z₁      d) H       e) z₂/2
      f) H        g) N (it is z̄₁ + z₂)    h) 2z₁²z̄₁ = 2|z₁|²z₁
      i) 1/(1 + |z₁|²)²        j) g′(z₁ + z̄₂)          k) z₁² + z₂
      l) yes, Re(z₁z₂)         m) i/4 (so x₁y₂ is not pluriharmonic)
      n) no (∂²/∂z₁∂z̄₁ = 1)    o) 6       p) (z, w) ↦ (z − w², w)
      q) 1 everywhere; not injective ((z + 2πi, w) has the same image)

M2. Cauchy formula on the torus T = {|ζ| = 1, |η| = 1} (positively
    oriented) and Taylor coefficients c_jk of zʲwᵏ.
    a) (2πi)⁻² ∫_T e^(ζ+η)/(ζη) dζdη
    b) (2πi)⁻² ∫_T (ζ² + η²)/((ζ − ½)(η − ⅓)) dζdη
    c) (2πi)⁻² ∫_T e^(ζη)/(ζ³η³) dζdη   d) (2πi)⁻² ∫_T cos(ζ + η)/(ζ²η²) dζdη
    e) (2πi)⁻² ∫_T dζdη/((ζ − 2)(η − ½))
    f) c₂₃ for 1/((1 − z)(1 − 2w))      g) c₂₂ for 1/(1 − z − w)
    h) ∂³f/∂z∂w²(0) for f = e^(z+2w)    i) c₃₃ and c₃₂ for 1/(1 − zw)
    j) (4π²)⁻¹ ∫∫ f(e^(iθ), e^(iφ)) dθdφ for f = (3 + z + w)²
    k) (4π²)⁻¹ ∫∫ |1 + 2e^(iθ) + 3e^(iφ)|² dθdφ
    l) (4π²)⁻¹ ∫∫ |e^(iθ) + e^(iφ)|⁴ dθdφ
    m) (2πi)⁻² ∫_T e^(ζ+η)/((ζ − ½)²η³) dζdη
    n) c₁₁ for f = sin(z + w)           o) c₄₀, c₂₂ for (z + w)⁴
    Answers:
      a) 1        b) 1/4 + 1/9 = 13/36       c) 1/2     d) −1
      e) 0 (no pole of ζ inside)         f) 8       g) C(4, 2) = 6
      h) 4        i) 1 and 0     j) 9 (mean value = f(0, 0))
      k) 14 (Parseval: 1 + 4 + 9)        l) 6 (|z² + 2zw + w²|²: 1+4+1)
      m) √e/2 ≈ 0.82436          n) 0 (sin has only odd degree terms)
      o) 1, 6

M3. Cauchy estimates, Liouville, maximum principle.
    a) |f| ≤ 1 on the unit bidisc: bound for |∂f/∂z(0)|
    b) |f| ≤ 3 on Δ(0; (2, 3)): bound for |∂²f/∂z∂w(0)|
    c) same f: bound for |∂⁴f/∂z²∂w²(0)|
    d) same f: bound for |c₃₂|
    e) f entire on C², |f| ≤ 1 + |z| + |w|: what is f?
    f) f entire, |f| ≤ C(1 + |z|² + |w|²)^(3/2): what is f?
    g) f entire, |f(z, w)| ≤ e^(Re z): what is f?
    h) max |zw| on the closed unit bidisc; where attained?
    i) max |z + w| on the closed unit ball {|z|² + |w|² ≤ 1}
    j) max |zw| on the closed unit ball
    k) max |z² + w| on the closed unit bidisc
    l) max |1 + z + w| on the closed unit ball
    m) f : D² → D holomorphic, f(0) = 0: best bound for |f_z(0)| + |f_w(0)|
    n) f : B² → D, f(0) = 0: best bound for |f_z(0)|² + |f_w(0)|²
    Answers:
      a) 1        b) 1!1!·3/(2·3) = 1/2       c) 2!2!·3/(2²·3²) = 1/3
      d) 3/(2³·3²) = 1/24        e) affine: a + bz + cw
      f) a polynomial of degree ≤ 3       g) ce^z (fe^(−z) is bounded)
      h) 1, exactly on the torus |z| = |w| = 1      i) √2
      j) 1/2 (at |z| = |w| = 1/√2)        k) 2       l) 1 + √2
      m) 1 (restrict to λ ↦ (λe^(iα), λe^(iβ)); equality for (z + w)/2)
      n) 1 (restrict to the disc in the direction (f̄_z, f̄_w))

M4. Domains of convergence of power series (describe the open domain;
    for Reinhardt domains also give the log image, x = log|z|, y =
    log|w|).
    a) ∑_{j,k} zʲwᵏ                    b) ∑ₙ (zw)ⁿ
    c) ∑ₙ zⁿw²ⁿ                        d) ∑ₙ zⁿ (as a series in z, w)
    e) ∑ₙ n! zⁿwⁿ                      f) ∑_{j,k} C(j + k, j) zʲwᵏ
    g) ∑_{j,k} zʲwᵏ/(2ʲ3ᵏ)             h) ∑ₙ (zw)ⁿ/n²
    i) ∑_{j,k} C(j + k, j) z²ʲwᵏ        j) ∑ₙ zⁿ w^(n²)
    k) ∑_{j,k} 2^(jk) zʲwᵏ
    l) is {|z|² + |w|² < 1} log-convex?  m) is {|z| < 1, |zw| < 1}?
    n) smallest domain of convergence containing (D × D_½) ∪ (D_½ × D)
    o) smallest one containing (D × D) ∪ (D₂ × D_½)
    Answers:
      a) D², {x < 0, y < 0}             b) {|zw| < 1}, {x + y < 0}
      c) {|z||w|² < 1}, {x + 2y < 0}     d) D × C, {x < 0}
      e) empty (converges only on {zw = 0})
      f) {|z| + |w| < 1}, {eˣ + eʸ < 1}  g) D₂ × D₃
      h) {|zw| < 1} (absolutely convergent also on |zw| = 1)
      i) {|z|² + |w| < 1}               j) C × D, {y < 0}
      k) empty (the terms 2^(n²)rⁿsⁿ are unbounded unless zw = 0)
      l) yes (eˣ + eʸ convex ⇒ sublevel set convex)
      m) yes ({x < 0, x + y < 0})       n) {|z|, |w| < 1, |zw| < ¼}
      o) {|z| < 2, |w| < 1, |zw| < 1}

M5. Hartogs phenomenon: does EVERY f holomorphic on the set extend
    holomorphically to the larger set? (Y/N, with reason or example)
    a) C² ∖ {0} → C²                   b) D ∖ {0} → D (in C)
    c) B ∖ B̄(0, ½) → B (B the unit ball of C²)
    d) D² ∖ ({|z| ≤ ½} × {0}) → D²       e) C² ∖ {z = 0} → C²
    f) Hartogs figure H (a = b = ½) → D²
    g) {(z, w) ∈ D² : |w| > ½} → D²
    h) D² ∖ {z = 0}, f bounded → D²
    i) D³ ∖ {z₁ = z₂ = 0} → D³ (a complex line removed)
    j) C² minus the closed unit ball → C²
    k) the annulus {½ < |z| < 1} in C → D
    l) {½ < |z|² + |w|² < 1} → B
    m) D² ∖ K, K = the closed real disc {(x, 0) : −½ ≤ x ≤ ½} → D²
    n) singular set of 1/(z² + w²): isolated point?
    o) zero set of z² − w³ near 0: isolated point?
    Answers:
      a) Y (Hartogs)          b) N (1/z)          c) Y (K compact, B ∖ K
         connected)           d) Y (compact K)    e) N (1/z)
      f) Y                    g) N (1/w)          h) Y (Riemann extension
         theorem across a hypersurface)           i) Y (codimension 2:
         Hartogs-Riemann; D³ ∖ line is not a domain of holomorphy)
      j) Y (to an entire function)              k) N (1/z; n = 1)
      l) Y                    m) Y (compact, connected complement)
      n) N: two lines w = ±iz         o) N: a cusp curve w = z^(2/3)

M6. Domains of holomorphy (Y/N) and holomorphic hulls.
    a) any domain in C                 b) the unit ball of C²
    c) the bidisc D²                   d) C² ∖ {0}
    e) C² ∖ {z = 0}                    f) {½ < |z|² + |w|² < 1}
    g) Ω₁ × Ω₂, Ωⱼ ⊆ C domains          h) a Hartogs figure
    i) {|zw| < 1}                      j) {|z|² + |w|⁴ < 1}
    k) D × (D ∖ {0})                   l) C² minus the closed unit ball
    m) Ω ∖ {g = 0}, Ω a domain of holomorphy, g ∈ 𝒪(Ω), g ≢ 0
    n) (D × D_¼) ∪ (D_¼ × D)            o) {|z| < 1, |w| < 1, |zw| < ¼}
    p) the hull in C² of the torus {|z| = |w| = ½}
    q) the hull in C² of {(λ/2, 0) : |λ| = 1}
    r) the hull in C² of {(λ/2, λ̄/2) : |λ| = 1}
    s) the hull in C² ∖ {0} of the sphere |z|² + |w|² = ¼
    Answers:
      a) Y        b) Y (convex)      c) Y       d) N       e) Y (C* × C)
      f) N (extends to the ball)     g) Y       h) N       i) Y (log-convex)
      j) Y (convex)                  k) Y (product)       l) N (Hartogs)
      m) Y        n) N (envelope = o))          o) Y
      p) the closed bidisc of radius ½   q) {|z| ≤ ½, w = 0}
      r) the circle itself (it lies on zw = ¼)
      s) {0 < |z|² + |w|² ≤ ¼}: not compact in Ω

M7. Plurisubharmonic or not? Give the complex Hessian (φ_jk̄) and decide
    psh / strictly psh / not psh on C² (or on the stated domain).
    a) |z|² + |w|²                      b) |z|² − |w|²
    c) log(1 + |z|² + |w|²): det of the Hessian
    d) Re(z²w)                          e) |z|² + |w|² + c Re(zw̄), c real
    f) |z + w̄|²                         g) |zw|²
    h) log|z² + w³|                     i) (Re z)²
    j) −log(1 − |z|² − |w|²) on the ball: Hessian at 0
    k) max(|z|, |w|)                    l) |z|² + t Re(z²): psh? convex?
    m) −|z|²                            n) log(|z|² + |w|²): det of Hessian
    o) u(z) with u subharmonic on C     p) (Re z)(Re w)
    Answers:
      a) I; strictly psh               b) diag(1, −1); not psh
      c) det = 1/(1 + |z|² + |w|²)³ > 0 (and φ_zz̄ > 0); strictly psh
      d) 0 (pluriharmonic); psh, not strictly
      e) [[1, c/2], [c/2, 1]]: psh ⇔ |c| ≤ 2, strictly ⇔ |c| < 2
      f) I (|z|² + |w|² + 2Re(zw), the last term pluriharmonic); strictly
      g) [[|w|², z̄w], [zw̄, |z|²]], det 0, trace ≥ 0: psh (= |f|², f = zw)
      h) psh (log|f|, f holomorphic)   i) [[½, 0], [0, 0]]: psh
      j) I at 0; strictly psh (the Bergman-type potential)
      k) psh (max of psh)              l) Hessian diag(1, 0) in (z, w):
         psh for every t; convex iff |t| ≤ 1
      m) not psh                       n) 0: psh but degenerate (rank 1)
      o) psh                           p) [[0, ¼], [¼, 0]]: not psh

M8. Levi form. Ω = {ρ < 0}; decide strictly pseudoconvex (SPC), weakly
    pseudoconvex (WPC, pseudoconvex but not strictly), Levi-flat (LF), or
    not pseudoconvex (NPC) near the given point or everywhere.
    a) ρ = |z|² + |w|² − 1             b) ρ = 1 − |z|² − |w|² (near |z|² +
                                          |w|² = 1, Ω the outside)
    c) ρ = |z|² + |w|⁴ − 1 at (1, 0)    d) the same ρ at (0, 1)
    e) ρ = |z|² − Im w (at 0)          f) ρ = Im w − |z|² (at 0)
    g) ρ = Re w                        h) ρ = |zw|² − 1 at (1, 1)
    i) ρ = Re w + |z|⁴ at 0            j) ρ = Re w + (Re z)²
    k) ρ = Re w − (Re z)²              l) ρ = Re w + y² − x² (z = x + iy)
    m) ρ = Re w + |z|² + t Re(z²)
    n) ρ = |z|² + |w|² − 1 at (1/√2, 1/√2): L(t) for t = (1, −1)
    o) {|w| < e^(−|z|²)}               p) {|w| < e^(|z|²)}
    q) {|w| < 1/(1 + |z|²)}            r) the bidisc D² near (1, 0)
    Answers:
      a) SPC      b) NPC (Levi form −|t|²)     c) WPC (L = 4|w|²|t₂|² = 0)
      d) SPC      e) SPC (Siegel domain; L = |t₁|²)
      f) NPC (L = −|t₁|²)                      g) LF (a half-space)
      h) LF (∂ρ = (1, 1), t = (1, −1), L = 0; ∂Ω is foliated by the
         curves zw = e^(iθ))
      i) WPC at 0 (L = 4|z|²|t₁|² = 0), SPC elsewhere
      j) SPC (L = ½|t₁|²; also convex) k) NPC (L = −½|t₁|²)
      l) LF (y² − x² = −Re z², pluriharmonic; not convex!)
      m) SPC for every t (L = |t₁|²); convex only for |t| ≤ 1
      n) 2 (> 0)  o) pseudoconvex (φ = |z|² subharmonic)
      p) NPC (φ = −|z|² superharmonic)
      q) pseudoconvex (φ = log(1 + |z|²) subharmonic)
      r) LF near (1, 0): the boundary piece {|z| = 1} × D is foliated by
         discs {e^(iθ)} × D

M9. Weierstrass preparation (w is the distinguished variable). Give the
    order k, the Weierstrass polynomial P (series to the stated order) and
    the unit u, or explain why the theorem does not apply directly.
    a) w² − z                          b) e^w − 1 − z
    c) w³ + w² + z (to z³)             d) we^z
    e) zw                              f) sin w − z
    g) w⁴ + w² + z² (to z⁸)            h) w³ − z²w + z⁵
    i) z + w                           j) z
    k) z³ + w⁵e^z                      l) w − w² + z (to z⁴)
    m) w² + z₁z₂ (in C³)               n) w sin(w²) + z⁴
    o) number of zeros of z + w² + w³ in |w| < δ for small fixed z ≠ 0
    Answers:
      a) k = 2, P = w² − z, u = 1
      b) k = 1, P = w − log(1 + z) = w − z + z²/2 − z³/3 + ...
      c) k = 2, P = w² + (−z + 2z² − 7z³)w + (z − z² + 3z³) + ...,
         u = w + 1 + z − 2z² + 7z³ + ...
      d) k = 1, P = w, u = e^z
      e) f(0, w) ≡ 0: not w-regular; with z = z″ + w it becomes w² + z″w
         (k = 2)
      f) k = 1, P = w − arcsin z = w − z − z³/6 − 3z⁵/40 − ...
      g) k = 2, P = w² + (1 − √(1 − 4z²))/2 = w² + z² + z⁴ + 2z⁶ + 5z⁸ +
         ... (Catalan numbers), u = w² + (1 + √(1 − 4z²))/2
      h) k = 3, P = f, u = 1           i) k = 1, P = w + z
      j) not regular; after z = z″ + w: k = 1, P = w + z″
      k) k = 5, P = w⁵ + z³e^(−z), u = e^z
      l) k = 1, P = w + z − z² + 2z³ − 5z⁴ + ..., u = (1 + √(1 + 4z))/2 −
         w
      m) k = 2, P = f                  n) k = 3 (f(0, w) = w³ − w⁷/6 +
         ...); P is a cubic in w, u(0) = 1
      o) 2

M10. Weierstrass division g = qP + r (deg_w r < deg_w P) and the local
     ring 𝒪₂ = C{z, w}. Give r, or answer the question.
     a) g = w³, P = w² − z              b) g = w⁴, P = w² − z
     c) g = e^w, P = w² − z             d) g = w⁵, P = w − z
     e) g arbitrary, P = w − h(z)       f) g = w², P = w² + zw + z²
     g) g = sin w, P = w²               h) g = cos w, P = w² − z
     i) g = w³, P = w² + zw + z²
     j) is z² − w² irreducible in 𝒪₂?   k) is w² − z³?
     l) is w² − z² − z³?
     m) dim_C 𝒪₂/(w² − z, z)            n) dim_C 𝒪₂/(z², w³)
     o) dim_C 𝒪₂/(z², w² − z³)          p) dim_C 𝒪₂/(zw, z + w)
     q) dim_C 𝒪₂/(w² − z³, w)           r) is the maximal ideal (z, w)
        principal?
     Answers:
       a) zw       b) z²       c) cosh√z + w·sinh(√z)/√z       d) z⁵
       e) g(z, h(z))           f) −zw − z²          g) w
       h) cos√z = ∑(−z)ᵐ/(2m)! (no w term: cos is even)
       i) z³ (since (w − z)(w² + zw + z²) = w³ − z³)
       j) no: (z − w)(z + w)   k) yes (z³ is not a square in C{z})
       l) no: (w − z√(1 + z))(w + z√(1 + z)), √(1 + z) a unit
       m) 2 (C{w}/(w²))        n) 6 (basis zⁱwʲ, i < 2, j < 3)
       o) 4 (w² ≡ z³ ≡ 0: basis 1, z, w, zw)
       p) 2 (w = −z, then z² = 0)          q) 3 (C{z}/(z³))
       r) no: 𝒪₂ is a UFD but not a PID (z, w have no common factor)

M11. The ∂̄-equation. In C solve ∂u/∂z̄ = f; in C² check the compatibility
     ∂f₁/∂z̄₂ = ∂f₂/∂z̄₁ for f = (f₁, f₂) and solve ∂u/∂z̄ⱼ = fⱼ if possible.
     a) f = 1                           b) f = z
     c) f = z̄                           d) f = z²z̄³
     e) f = e^(z̄)                       f) f = x = Re z
     g) (z̄₂, z̄₁)                        h) (z̄₂, 0)
     i) (z₁, z₂)                        j) (z₂, z₁)
     k) (2z̄₁z̄₂, z̄₁²)                    l) (z̄₁z₂, z₁z̄₂)
     m) u = Cauchy-Green solution for f = 𝟙_D: u(½), u(2), u(i/2)
     n) does ∂u/∂z̄ = 𝟙_D have a compactly supported solution in C?
     o) ∂u/∂z̄ = f, f ∈ C_c(C): condition for a compactly supported u
     Answers (u is unique up to adding a holomorphic function):
       a) z̄        b) |z|² = zz̄      c) z̄²/2       d) z²z̄⁴/4
       e) e^(z̄)    f) |z|²/2 + z̄²/4 (since x = (z + z̄)/2)
       g) closed; u = z̄₁z̄₂             h) not closed (1 ≠ 0): no solution
       i) closed; u = |z₁|² + |z₂|²     j) closed; u = z₂z̄₁ + z₁z̄₂
       k) closed (2z̄₁ = 2z̄₁); u = z̄₁²z̄₂
       l) closed; u = (z₂z̄₁² + z₁z̄₂²)/2
       m) ½, ½, −i/2 (u = z̄ on D̄, 1/z outside)
       n) no: u ~ 1/z at ∞ since ∫_D 1 dA = π ≠ 0
       o) ∫ f(ζ)ζᵐ dA(ζ) = 0 for all m ≥ 0 (expand 1/(z − ζ) at ∞)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) A function on C² holomorphic in each variable separately is
        holomorphic (no continuity assumed).
     b) The zeros of a holomorphic f ≢ 0 on C² are isolated.
     c) Every domain in C² is a domain of holomorphy.
     d) Every convex domain in Cⁿ is a domain of holomorphy.
     e) Every domain of holomorphy in C² is convex.
     f) The unit ball and the bidisc in C² are biholomorphic.
     g) Every power series in C² converges on a polydisc, and its domain
        of convergence is a polydisc.
     h) The domain of convergence of a power series is logarithmically
        convex.
     i) The maximum and the sum of two psh functions are psh.
     j) The minimum of two psh functions is psh.
     k) Every harmonic function on R⁴ = C² is psh.
     l) Every C² psh function is convex.
     m) Hartogs' extension theorem holds in C.
     n) The union of two domains of holomorphy is a domain of holomorphy.
     o) Each component of the intersection of two domains of holomorphy
        is a domain of holomorphy.
     p) Pseudoconvex domains in Cⁿ are exactly the domains of holomorphy.
     q) Every f ∈ 𝒪₂ with f(0) = 0, f ≢ 0 is w-regular after a linear
        change of coordinates.
     r) 𝒪₂ is a principal ideal domain.
     s) ∂̄u = f is solvable on C² for every smooth (0,1)-form f.
     t) Strict pseudoconvexity is preserved by biholomorphisms, convexity
        is not.
     Answers:
       a) T (Hartogs' theorem on separate analyticity)
       b) F: f = z (zero set the line {z = 0})
       c) F: C² ∖ {0}           d) T
       e) F: D × {½ < |w| < 1}, or {|zw| < 1}
       f) F: Poincaré 1907 (their automorphism groups differ)
       g) F: ∑(zw)ⁿ converges on {|zw| < 1}, not a polydisc
       h) T        i) T         j) F: min(x, −x) = −|Re z|
       k) F: |z|² − |w|²        l) F: |z|² + 2Re(z²) = 3x² − y²
       m) F: 1/z on D ∖ {0}     n) F: the Hartogs figure (union of
          two polydiscs)        o) T (holomorphic convexity passes to
          intersections)        p) T (Levi problem: Oka, Bremermann,
          Norguet)              q) T         r) F: (z, w) is not principal
       s) F: z̄₂ dz̄₁ (not ∂̄-closed)
       t) T (the Levi form transforms by the Jacobian; (z, w) ↦ (z, w +
          z²) maps the half-space {Re w < 0} onto the non-convex
          {Re w < Re z²})

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw everything in the (|z|, |w|) quadrant or the log plane
  (x, y) = (log|z|, log|w|): polydiscs are rectangles, the ball is a
  quarter circle, {|zw| < 1} is a half-plane, the Hartogs figure is an L,
  and "domain of convergence" / "domain of holomorphy" for Reinhardt
  domains means "log image convex" — the child can SEE the envelope of
  holomorphy as a convex hull. Always check holomorphy by writing f in z
  and z̄ and differentiating in z̄; compute complex Hessians as matrices
  and test psd with trace and determinant; for Levi forms first find the
  complex tangent vector t = (ρ_w, −ρ_z), then evaluate the Hermitian form
  on t only. For Weierstrass preparation, first compute f(0, w) to get
  the order k, then find the k small roots as series in z. Harder: prove
  Hartogs' theorem on separate analyticity (via Baire and Hartogs'
  lemma on psh functions); prove that −log dist(z, ∂Ω) is psh for a
  domain of holomorphy; prove Poincaré's theorem that B² and D² are not
  biholomorphic by comparing automorphism groups at 0; prove the Oka
  lemma and Hörmander's L² estimate for ∂̄ on pseudoconvex domains and
  deduce the Levi problem; show via Weierstrass division that 𝒪ₙ is
  Noetherian and a UFD, and prove Riemann's extension theorem across an
  analytic hypersurface and across analytic sets of codimension ≥ 2.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.5 — ABSTRACT ALGEBRA (GRADUATE): SOLVABLE AND NILPOTENT GROUPS,
MODULES, TENSOR PRODUCTS, EXACT SEQUENCES, CATEGORY THEORY BASICS,
INTRODUCTION TO HOMOLOGICAL ALGEBRA
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - TOOLS FROM GROUP THEORY: group actions, orbit-stabiliser, the CLASS
    EQUATION |G| = |Z(G)| + ∑[G : C(xᵢ)], SYLOW THEOREMS (Sylow p-subgroups
    exist, are conjugate, and n_p ≡ 1 (mod p), n_p | |G|/pᵃ). A finite
    p-group has non-trivial centre. Standard use: counting elements to
    show a group of order 30, 56, pq, ... has a normal Sylow subgroup.
  - COMPOSITION SERIES 1 = G_n ◁ ... ◁ G_1 ◁ G_0 = G with SIMPLE factors
    Gᵢ/Gᵢ₊₁. JORDAN-HÖLDER: any two composition series of a finite group
    have the same factors up to order and isomorphism. The factors do NOT
    determine G (Z/4 and Z/2 × Z/2; Z/6 and S₃). Z has none.
  - SOLVABLE GROUPS: DERIVED SERIES G⁽⁰⁾ = G, G⁽ⁱ⁺¹⁾ = [G⁽ⁱ⁾, G⁽ⁱ⁾]; G is
    solvable iff G⁽ⁿ⁾ = 1 for some n iff G has a subnormal series with
    ABELIAN factors (finite G: iff all composition factors are Z/p). Closed
    under subgroups, quotients and EXTENSIONS. G/N abelian ⇔ G′ ≤ N.
    Examples: abelian groups, p-groups, S₃, S₄ (S₄ ▷ A₄ ▷ V₄ ▷ 1), GL₂(F₃),
    upper triangular matrices. S_n (n ≥ 5), A₅, SL₂(F₅), free groups of
    rank ≥ 2 are NOT solvable. Burnside: order pᵃqᵇ ⇒ solvable;
    Feit-Thompson: odd order ⇒ solvable (statements only).
  - NILPOTENT GROUPS: LOWER CENTRAL SERIES γ₁ = G, γᵢ₊₁ = [γᵢ, G]; UPPER
    CENTRAL SERIES Z₀ = 1, Zᵢ₊₁/Zᵢ = Z(G/Zᵢ). G is nilpotent of class c iff
    γ_{c+1} = 1 iff Z_c = G. Nilpotent ⇒ solvable (S₃ shows the converse
    fails). Finite p-groups are nilpotent; D₈, Q₈ have class 2, D_{2^(k+1)}
    has class k, the Heisenberg group UT₃(F_p) has class 2, UTₙ class n − 1.
  - FINITE NILPOTENT GROUPS: TFAE (i) nilpotent, (ii) H < G ⇒ H < N_G(H)
    ("normalisers grow"), (iii) every Sylow subgroup is normal, (iv) G is
    the direct product of its Sylow subgroups, (v) every maximal subgroup
    is normal. Subgroups and quotients of nilpotent groups are nilpotent,
    but an extension of nilpotent by nilpotent need not be (S₃).
  - SEMIDIRECT PRODUCTS N ⋊_φ H for φ : H → Aut(N); G ≅ N ⋊ H when N ◁ G,
    H ≤ G, N ∩ H = 1, NH = G. Classification: groups of order pq (p < q)
    are Z/pq, plus exactly one non-abelian group when p | q − 1. Small
    counts: order 8: 5, order 12: 5 (Z/12, Z/2 × Z/6, A₄, D₁₂, Z/3 ⋊ Z/4),
    order 16: 14, order p²: 2, order p³: 5.
  - MODULES over a ring R: submodules, quotients, isomorphism theorems,
    direct sums and products, FREE modules (bases, rank over commutative
    R), torsion, annihilators. Abelian groups = Z-modules; a k[x]-module
    = a k-vector space with one linear map T; a Z[G]-module = a
    representation of G. Submodules of f.g. modules need not be f.g.
    unless R is Noetherian.
  - STRUCTURE THEOREM (f.g. modules over a PID R): M ≅ Rʳ ⊕ R/(d₁) ⊕ ... ⊕
    R/(d_k) with d₁ | d₂ | ... | d_k non-units (INVARIANT FACTORS), or
    Rʳ ⊕ ⊕R/(pᵢ^eᵢ) (ELEMENTARY DIVISORS), unique. Computed by SMITH NORMAL
    FORM: dᵢ = Δᵢ/Δᵢ₋₁, Δᵢ = gcd of i × i minors. Consequences: f.g.
    abelian groups; number of abelian groups of order ∏pᵢ^aᵢ is ∏p(aᵢ);
    RATIONAL CANONICAL FORM (invariant factors of V_T; last one = minimal
    polynomial, product = characteristic polynomial) and JORDAN FORM
    (elementary divisors (x − λ)ᵉ).
  - Hom AND TENSOR: Hom_R(M, N); M ⊗_R N defined by the UNIVERSAL PROPERTY
    for R-bilinear maps, built as the free module on M × N modulo the
    bilinearity relations. Key computations: R/I ⊗ M ≅ M/IM, Z/m ⊗ Z/n ≅
    Z/gcd(m, n), Q ⊗ Z/n = 0, Q ⊗_Z Q ≅ Q, Rᵐ ⊗ Rⁿ ≅ Rᵐⁿ, ⊗ distributes
    over ⊕, EXTENSION OF SCALARS S ⊗_R M, C ⊗_R C ≅ C × C, k[x] ⊗ k[y] ≅
    k[x, y]. Not every element is a pure tensor m ⊗ n.
  - EXACT SEQUENCES: exact at B means im = ker; SHORT exact 0 → A → B → C
    → 0. SPLITTING LEMMA: retraction ⇔ section ⇔ B ≅ A ⊕ C compatibly.
    Non-split: 0 → Z/2 → Z/4 → Z/2 → 0, 0 → Z → Z → Z/n → 0; sequences
    ending in a free (projective) module split. FIVE LEMMA, SNAKE LEMMA
    (ker α → ker β → ker γ → coker α → coker β → coker γ).
  - EXACTNESS OF FUNCTORS: Hom_R(M, −) and Hom_R(−, N) are LEFT exact;
    − ⊗_R N is RIGHT exact (it is a left adjoint: Hom(M ⊗ N, P) ≅ Hom(M,
    Hom(N, P))). Failures: Hom(Z/2, −) and − ⊗ Z/2 on 0 → Z → Z → Z/2 → 0.
  - PROJECTIVE (Hom(P, −) exact ⇔ direct summand of a free module),
    INJECTIVE (Hom(−, I) exact; BAER'S CRITERION; over a PID ⇔ DIVISIBLE)
    and FLAT (− ⊗ M exact; over a PID ⇔ torsion-free) modules. Free ⇒
    projective ⇒ flat. Examples: Z/2 over Z/6 projective, not free; Q over
    Z flat and injective, not projective; Q/Z injective, not flat; over a
    PID (or local ring, f.g.) projective ⇔ free.
  - CATEGORIES: objects, morphisms, composition, identities; Set, Grp,
    Ab, R-Mod, Ring, Top, a poset, a group as a one-object category.
    FUNCTORS (covariant/contravariant: forgetful, free, Hom(A, −), dual
    V ↦ V*, abelianisation, π₁), NATURAL TRANSFORMATIONS (V → V** natural;
    V ≅ V* not natural), equivalences, faithful/full.
  - UNIVERSAL PROPERTIES: initial/terminal objects, products and
    coproducts (Set: × and ⊔; Grp: × and free product *; Ab: ⊕; CRing: ×
    and ⊗_Z; poset: inf and sup), kernels, pullbacks, limits and colimits
    (Z_p = lim Z/pⁿ, Z[1/2] = colim(Z →2 Z →2 ...)). YONEDA LEMMA: Nat(
    Hom(A, −), F) ≅ F(A). ADJUNCTIONS F ⊣ G: free ⊣ forgetful,
    abelianisation ⊣ inclusion, − ⊗ N ⊣ Hom(N, −); LEFT ADJOINTS PRESERVE
    COLIMITS, right adjoints preserve limits.
  - HOMOLOGICAL ALGEBRA: chain complexes (d² = 0), HOMOLOGY Hₙ = ker dₙ /
    im dₙ₊₁, chain maps and chain homotopies (homotopic maps induce the
    same map on homology); a SHORT EXACT SEQUENCE OF COMPLEXES gives a LONG
    EXACT SEQUENCE with connecting maps (snake lemma). PROJECTIVE
    RESOLUTIONS (unique up to homotopy); Torₙ(A, B) = Hₙ(P_• ⊗ B),
    Extⁿ(A, B) = Hⁿ(Hom(P_•, B)). Over Z: Tor₁(Z/m, Z/n) ≅ Ext¹(Z/m, Z/n)
    ≅ Z/gcd(m, n), Tor₁(A, Z/n) ≅ A[n], Ext¹(Z/n, Z) ≅ Z/n, and Torᵢ =
    Extⁱ = 0 for i ≥ 2. Ext¹(C, A) classifies extensions 0 → A → B → C → 0.
    Over k[x]/(x²), Torₙ(k, k) = k for every n (infinite resolution).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Show that G is solvable (G⁽ⁿ⁾ = 1 for some n) iff G has a series
    G = G₀ ▷ G₁ ▷ ... ▷ G_n = 1 with every Gᵢ/Gᵢ₊₁ abelian. (b) Prove that
    subgroups, quotients and extensions of solvable groups are solvable.
    (c) Find the derived series of S₄ and show S_n is not solvable for
    n ≥ 5.
    Answer: (a) induction G⁽ⁱ⁾ ≤ Gᵢ; (b) H⁽ⁱ⁾ ≤ G⁽ⁱ⁾, (G/N)⁽ⁱ⁾ = G⁽ⁱ⁾N/N;
    (c) S₄ ▷ A₄ ▷ V₄ ▷ 1 (derived length 3); for n ≥ 5 the series
    stops at A_n ≠ 1.
    Teacher explains: The key lemma: for N ◁ G, G/N is abelian iff every
    commutator [x, y] = x⁻¹y⁻¹xy lies in N, i.e. iff G′ ≤ N. (a) If the
    series exists, Gᵢ/Gᵢ₊₁ abelian gives Gᵢ′ ≤ Gᵢ₊₁; by induction G⁽ⁱ⁾ ≤
    Gᵢ (if G⁽ⁱ⁾ ≤ Gᵢ then G⁽ⁱ⁺¹⁾ = [G⁽ⁱ⁾, G⁽ⁱ⁾] ≤ Gᵢ′ ≤ Gᵢ₊₁), so G⁽ⁿ⁾ ≤ G_n
    = 1. Conversely the derived series itself is such a series (each G⁽ⁱ⁾
    is even characteristic in G). (b) Subgroup: H⁽ⁱ⁾ ≤ G⁽ⁱ⁾ by induction.
    Quotient: commutators of cosets are cosets of commutators, so (G/N)⁽ⁱ⁾
    = G⁽ⁱ⁾N/N. Extension: if N and G/N are solvable, (G/N)⁽ᵐ⁾ = 1 means
    G⁽ᵐ⁾ ≤ N, hence G⁽ᵐ⁺ᵏ⁾ ≤ N⁽ᵏ⁾ = 1 for large k. (c) Every commutator is
    an even permutation and S₄/A₄ ≅ Z/2 is abelian, so S₄′ ≤ A₄; the
    commutator of (1 2) and (1 3) is a 3-cycle, and the 3-cycles generate
    A₄, so S₄′ = A₄ (conjugating one 3-cycle in S₄ gives them all). A₄/V₄
    ≅ Z/3 gives A₄′ ≤ V₄; two non-commuting 3-cycles have a non-trivial
    commutator, which is then a double transposition, and conjugation
    in A₄ moves it to all three, so A₄′ = V₄. V₄ is abelian: V₄′ = 1. For
    n ≥ 5, A_n is simple and non-abelian, so A_n′ = A_n (a non-trivial
    normal subgroup); the derived series S_n ▷ A_n = A_n = ... never
    reaches 1. Common mistakes: "solvable means abelian"; claiming a
    subgroup of a solvable group is normal in the series; forgetting that
    S_n′ = A_n needs the 3-cycles. Now you try: the derived series of
    GL₂(F₃). (GL₂(F₃) ▷ SL₂(F₃) ▷ Q₈ ▷ {±I} ▷ 1, orders 48, 24, 8, 2, 1;
    derived length 4)

D2. (a) Prove that a finite p-group has non-trivial centre and deduce that
    it is nilpotent. (b) Compute the upper and lower central series of the
    dihedral group D₈ = ⟨r, s | r⁴ = s² = 1, srs = r⁻¹⟩ and of Q₈, and find
    the class of D₁₆. (c) Show that S₃ is solvable but not nilpotent.
    Answer: (a) class equation; (b) D₈, Q₈: 1 < Z = γ₂ (order 2) < G,
    class 2; D₁₆ has class 3; (c) Z(S₃) = 1 and γ₂ = γ₃ = A₃.
    Teacher explains: (a) |G| = pⁿ. The class equation reads |G| = |Z(G)| +
    ∑[G : C(xᵢ)], summing over non-central class representatives. Each
    index [G : C(xᵢ)] divides pⁿ and is > 1, so it is divisible by p;
    also p | |G|. Hence p | |Z(G)|, so Z(G) ≠ 1. Now induct on n: G/Z(G)
    is a smaller p-group, hence nilpotent, so its upper central series
    reaches G/Z(G); pulling back, Z_{i+1}(G) is the preimage of Zᵢ(G/Z(G)),
    and the upper central series of G reaches G. (b) In D₈, r² commutes
    with r and with s (s r² s = r⁻² = r²), and nothing else outside 1 does,
    so Z = {1, r²}. D₈/Z has order 4, hence is abelian, so Z₂ = G: class 2.
    Lower series: [r, s] = r⁻¹s⁻¹rs = r⁻¹ · r⁻¹ = r⁻², so γ₂ = ⟨r²⟩ (it
    is normal and D₈/⟨r²⟩ ≅ V₄ is abelian), and γ₃ = [⟨r²⟩, G] = 1 since
    r² is central. Q₈ = {±1, ±i, ±j, ±k}: Z = {±1}, [i, j] = i⁻¹j⁻¹ij =
    (−i)(−j)ij = k · k = −1; since ij = k ≠ −k = ji, Q₈ is not abelian; γ₂ =
    {±1} = Z, class 2. For D₁₆ (r⁸ = 1): Z = ⟨r⁴⟩, D₁₆/Z ≅ D₈ of class 2,
    so D₁₆ has class 3; in general D_{2^(k+1)} has class k. (c) S₃ ▷ A₃ ▷
    1 has abelian factors, so S₃ is solvable. But Z(S₃) = 1, so Z₁ = Z₀
    and the upper series is stuck at 1. Lower: γ₂ = A₃ and γ₃ = [A₃, S₃]
    contains [(1 2 3), (1 2)], a 3-cycle, so γ₃ = A₃ = γ₄ = ...
    Common mistakes: using [γᵢ, γᵢ] (the derived series) instead of
    [γᵢ, G]; thinking G/Z(G) cyclic is possible for non-abelian G (it
    forces G abelian). Now you try: the nilpotency class of the
    Heisenberg group UT₃(F_p) of upper unitriangular 3 × 3 matrices, and
    its centre. (class 2; Z = γ₂ = matrices with only the corner entry,
    order p)

D3. Prove that for a finite group G the following are equivalent:
    (i) G is nilpotent; (ii) every proper subgroup H satisfies H < N_G(H);
    (iii) every Sylow subgroup is normal; (iv) G is the direct product of
    its Sylow subgroups. Decide which groups of order 12 are nilpotent.
    Answer: proof below; of the five groups of order 12 exactly Z/12 and
    Z/2 × Z/6 are nilpotent.
    Teacher explains: (i) ⇒ (ii): let i be maximal with Zᵢ ≤ H (Z₀ = 1 ≤
    H and Z_c = G ⊄ H). Then Zᵢ₊₁ ⊄ H and [Zᵢ₊₁, H] ≤ [Zᵢ₊₁, G] ≤ Zᵢ ≤ H,
    so for z ∈ Zᵢ₊₁, h ∈ H: z⁻¹hz = h[h, z] ∈ H. Thus Zᵢ₊₁ ≤ N_G(H), and
    N_G(H) ⊋ H. (ii) ⇒ (iii): let P be a Sylow p-subgroup and N = N_G(P).
    P is the unique Sylow p-subgroup of N (it is normal there). If g
    normalises N, then gPg⁻¹ is a Sylow p-subgroup of N, so gPg⁻¹ = P and
    g ∈ N. So N_G(N) = N; by (ii) N cannot be proper: N = G, P ◁ G.
    (iii) ⇒ (iv): the Sylow subgroups P₁, ..., P_k for the distinct primes
    are normal with pairwise trivial intersections (coprime orders). For
    x ∈ Pᵢ, y ∈ Pⱼ (i ≠ j), [x, y] ∈ Pᵢ ∩ Pⱼ = 1, so they commute, and the
    map P₁ × ... × P_k → G, (x₁, ..., x_k) ↦ x₁⋯x_k is an injective
    homomorphism; comparing orders it is an isomorphism. (iv) ⇒ (i):
    p-groups are nilpotent (D2) and Zᵢ(A × B) = Zᵢ(A) × Zᵢ(B), so a finite
    product of nilpotent groups is nilpotent. Order 12 = 4 · 3: nilpotent
    ⇔ G ≅ P₂ × P₃ with |P₂| = 4, |P₃| = 3; P₂ ∈ {Z/4, Z/2 × Z/2}, so G ≅
    Z/12 or Z/2 × Z/6. A₄ (n₃ = 4), D₁₂ (n₂ = 3) and Z/3 ⋊ Z/4 (n₂ = 3)
    are not. Common mistakes: "one normal Sylow subgroup suffices" (A₄
    has V₄ normal); "nilpotent ⇒ abelian" (Q₈). Now you try: how many
    nilpotent groups of order 72 = 8 · 9 are there? (5 · 2 = 10)

D4. (a) State the Jordan-Hölder theorem. Find all composition series of
    Z/12 and their factors. (b) Give the composition factors of S₄, S₅ and
    GL₂(F₃). (c) Prove that every group of order 30 has a normal subgroup
    of order 15, hence is solvable, and give its composition factors.
    Answer: (a) 3 series, factors always Z/2, Z/2, Z/3; (b) S₄: Z/2, Z/3,
    Z/2, Z/2; S₅: Z/2, A₅; GL₂(F₃): Z/2 (four times), Z/3; (c) counting
    Sylow elements; factors Z/2, Z/3, Z/5.
    Teacher explains: JORDAN-HÖLDER: any two composition series of a
    finite group have the same length and the same multiset of simple
    factors. (a) Subgroups of Z/12 correspond to divisors; a composition
    series is a maximal chain of divisors 1 | a | b | 12 with prime
    ratios. Orders (top to bottom): 12 > 6 > 3 > 1 (factors Z/2, Z/2,
    Z/3), 12 > 6 > 2 > 1 (Z/2, Z/3, Z/2), 12 > 4 > 2 > 1 (Z/3, Z/2, Z/2).
    Three series, same factors in different orders. In general Z/n has
    (a₁ + ... + a_k)!/(a₁!⋯a_k!) composition series for n = ∏pᵢ^aᵢ.
    (b) S₄ ▷ A₄ ▷ V₄ ▷ Z/2 ▷ 1 refines the derived series. S₅ ▷ A₅ ▷ 1 with
    A₅ simple. GL₂(F₃) ▷ SL₂(F₃) ▷ Q₈ ▷ {±I} ▷ 1 has factors Z/2, Z/3,
    Z/2 × Z/2, Z/2, and V₄ is refined into two Z/2's. (c) n₅ | 6, n₅ ≡ 1
    (mod 5) ⇒ n₅ ∈ {1, 6}; n₃ | 10, n₃ ≡ 1 (mod 3) ⇒ n₃ ∈ {1, 10}. Distinct
    subgroups of prime order meet trivially, so n₅ = 6 would give 24
    elements of order 5 and n₃ = 10 would give 20 elements of order 3;
    24 + 20 > 30, so one of P₅, P₃ is normal. Then H = P₃P₅ is a subgroup
    of order 15, cyclic (3 ∤ 5 − 1), and of index 2, hence normal. The
    series G ▷ H ▷ P₅ ▷ 1 has factors Z/2, Z/3, Z/5: G is solvable. Common
    mistakes: counting elements of Sylow subgroups of non-prime order
    (they may overlap); concluding G ≅ H × Z/2 (there are 4 groups of
    order 30: Z/30, D₃₀, S₃ × Z/5, D₁₀ × Z/3). Now you try: show that a
    group of order 20 is not simple. (n₅ | 4 and n₅ ≡ 1 mod 5 ⇒ n₅ = 1)

D5. (a) Given φ : H → Aut(N), define N ⋊_φ H and show N is normal in it.
    (b) Classify the groups of order pq, p < q primes. (c) Write down the
    non-abelian group of order 21 by generators and relations.
    Answer: (a) (n, h)(n′, h′) = (n φ_h(n′), hh′); (b) Z/pq, and one more
    group iff p | q − 1; (c) ⟨x, y | x⁷ = y³ = 1, yxy⁻¹ = x²⟩.
    Teacher explains: (a) On N × H put (n, h)(n′, h′) = (n·φ_h(n′), hh′).
    Associativity uses φ_{hh′} = φ_h ∘ φ_{h′}; the identity is (1, 1) and
    (n, h)⁻¹ = (φ_{h⁻¹}(n⁻¹), h⁻¹). The projection (n, h) ↦ h is a
    homomorphism with kernel N × 1, so N ◁ G, and inside G we have h n h⁻¹
    = φ_h(n): the action becomes conjugation. Conversely, if N ◁ G, H ≤ G,
    N ∩ H = 1 and NH = G, then G ≅ N ⋊ H with φ = conjugation. (b) n_q ≡ 1
    (mod q) and n_q | p < q force n_q = 1: Q ≅ Z/q is normal. Let P ≅ Z/p
    be a Sylow p-subgroup; P ∩ Q = 1 and |PQ| = pq, so G ≅ Z/q ⋊_φ Z/p
    with φ : Z/p → Aut(Z/q) ≅ (Z/q)^× ≅ Z/(q − 1). If p ∤ q − 1, φ is
    trivial and G ≅ Z/q × Z/p ≅ Z/pq. If p | q − 1, the cyclic group
    (Z/q)^× has a unique subgroup of order p, generated by some a; the
    non-trivial φ send the generator y of P to aᵏ (1 ≤ k < p), and
    replacing y by a suitable power yʲ shows all these give isomorphic
    groups. So there are exactly two groups. (c) p = 3, q = 7: 3 | 6, and
    a = 2 has order 3 mod 7 (2³ = 8 ≡ 1). So G = ⟨x, y | x⁷ = y³ = 1, yxy⁻¹
    = x²⟩, of order 21, with trivial centre and n₃ = 7. Common mistakes:
    counting different homomorphisms φ as different groups; forgetting
    to check that the proposed "a" really has order p modulo q; applying
    the argument when p = q. Now you try: how many groups have order 35,
    55, 57, 91? (1, 2, 2, 1)

D6. (a) State the structure theorem for finitely generated modules over a
    PID in invariant-factor and elementary-divisor form. (b) Identify the
    abelian group M = Z³/K, where K is spanned by the rows of
        A = [  2   4   4 ]
            [ −6   6  12 ]
            [ 10   4  16 ].
    (c) List all abelian groups of order 360 in both forms.
    Answer: (b) M ≅ Z/2 ⊕ Z/2 ⊕ Z/156 (order 624); (c) six groups.
    Teacher explains: (a) M ≅ Rʳ ⊕ R/(d₁) ⊕ ... ⊕ R/(d_k), d₁ | ... | d_k
    non-zero non-units, r and the ideals (dᵢ) unique; equivalently M ≅ Rʳ
    ⊕ ⊕ᵢ R/(pᵢ^eᵢ) with the prime powers unique up to order. Proof idea:
    M ≅ Rⁿ/K with K ≤ Rⁿ free (R a PID), and a presentation matrix can be
    brought by invertible row and column operations to the SMITH NORMAL
    FORM diag(d₁, ..., d_k, 0, ...). (b) The quantities Δᵢ = gcd of all
    i × i minors are unchanged by invertible operations, and Δᵢ = d₁⋯dᵢ.
    Here Δ₁ = gcd of the entries = 2; the 2 × 2 minors have gcd Δ₂ = 4 (e.g.
    2·6 − 4·(−6) = 36, 2·12 − 4·(−6) = 48, 4·12 − 4·6 = 24, 2·4 − 4·10 =
    −32, ... all divisible by 4, and the gcd is exactly 4); Δ₃ = |det A| =
    624 = 2⁴ · 3 · 13. So d₁ = 2, d₂ = 4/2 = 2, d₃ = 624/4 = 156 and
       M ≅ Z/2 ⊕ Z/2 ⊕ Z/156,  elementary divisors 2, 2, 4, 3, 13.
    (c) 360 = 2³ · 3² · 5, so the count is p(3)p(2)p(1) = 3 · 2 · 1 = 6:
       elementary divisors        invariant factors
       8, 9, 5                    Z/360
       2, 4, 9, 5                 Z/2 ⊕ Z/180
       2, 2, 2, 9, 5              Z/2 ⊕ Z/2 ⊕ Z/90
       8, 3, 3, 5                 Z/3 ⊕ Z/120
       2, 4, 3, 3, 5              Z/6 ⊕ Z/60
       2, 2, 2, 3, 3, 5           Z/2 ⊕ Z/6 ⊕ Z/30
    To pass from elementary divisors to invariant factors, multiply the
    largest power of each prime to get d_k, then the next largest, etc.
    Common mistakes: reading the answer off the diagonal after row
    operations only (you need divisibility d₁ | d₂ | d₃); forgetting
    "finitely generated" (Q is torsion-free but not free). Now you try:
    Z²/⟨(4, 6), (6, 4)⟩. (Δ₁ = 2, Δ₂ = |16 − 36| = 20: Z/2 ⊕ Z/10)

D7. A linear map T on a finite-dimensional k-vector space V makes V a
    k[x]-module (x·v = Tv). (a) Explain how the structure theorem gives
    the rational canonical and Jordan forms. (b) Find all Jordan forms and
    invariant factors of 6 × 6 complex matrices with characteristic
    polynomial (x − 1)⁴(x − 2)² and minimal polynomial (x − 1)²(x − 2).
    (c) How many similarity classes have characteristic polynomial
    (x − 1)⁴(x − 2)²? (d) Count similarity classes of 2 × 2 matrices over
    F_q.
    Answer: (b) two classes; (c) p(4)p(2) = 10; (d) q² + q.
    Teacher explains: (a) V is f.g. and torsion over the PID k[x], so V ≅
    k[x]/(f₁) ⊕ ... ⊕ k[x]/(f_m), f₁ | ... | f_m monic. On k[x]/(f), x acts
    by the COMPANION MATRIX of f in the basis 1, x, ..., x^(deg f − 1). So
    T is similar to a block diagonal of companion matrices (RATIONAL
    CANONICAL FORM); f_m = minimal polynomial, f₁⋯f_m = characteristic
    polynomial. Over C, the elementary divisors are (x − λ)ᵉ and on
    k[x]/((x − λ)ᵉ) the basis (x − λ)^(e−1), ..., (x − λ), 1 gives a Jordan
    block J_e(λ). Two matrices are similar iff their modules are
    isomorphic iff they have the same invariant factors. (b) Eigenvalue 1:
    block sizes form a partition of 4 with largest part 2: (2, 2) or
    (2, 1, 1). Eigenvalue 2: a partition of 2 with largest part 1: (1, 1).
       J₂(1) ⊕ J₂(1) ⊕ [2] ⊕ [2]:     f₁ = f₂ = (x − 1)²(x − 2)
       J₂(1) ⊕ [1] ⊕ [1] ⊕ [2] ⊕ [2]: f₁ = x − 1, f₂ = (x − 1)(x − 2),
                                       f₃ = (x − 1)²(x − 2)
    (group the elementary divisors: f_m takes the largest power of each
    prime, f_{m−1} the next largest, ...). Degrees add up to 6 in both.
    (c) Any partition of 4 for λ = 1 and of 2 for λ = 2: 5 · 2 = 10.
    (d) Either T is scalar aI (invariant factors x − a, x − a: q classes)
    or V is cyclic with the single invariant factor any monic quadratic
    (q² classes): q² + q in all (6 over F₂). Common mistakes: confusing
    the number of Jordan blocks for λ (= dim ker(T − λ)) with the algebraic
    multiplicity; thinking the minimal polynomial alone determines the
    class. Now you try: how many conjugacy classes of nilpotent 5 × 5
    matrices are there? (p(5) = 7)

D8. (a) State the universal property of M ⊗_R N and sketch why it exists.
    Prove (b) Z/m ⊗_Z Z/n ≅ Z/d, d = gcd(m, n); (c) Q ⊗_Z Z/n = 0;
    (d) R/I ⊗_R M ≅ M/IM; (e) C ⊗_R C ≅ C × C as rings.
    Answer: (b) (a, b) ↦ ab mod d and d(1 ⊗ 1) = 0; (c) q ⊗ a = (q/n) ⊗
    na; (d) right exactness; (e) C[x]/(x² + 1) and the CRT.
    Teacher explains: (a) M ⊗_R N (R commutative) is a module with a
    bilinear map ⊗ : M × N → M ⊗ N such that every bilinear β : M × N → P
    factors uniquely as β = β̃ ∘ ⊗ with β̃ linear. Uniqueness up to unique
    isomorphism is formal. Existence: take the free module on the SET
    M × N and divide by the submodule generated by (m + m′, n) − (m, n) −
    (m′, n), (m, n + n′) − (m, n) − (m, n′), (rm, n) − r(m, n), (m, rn) −
    r(m, n). (b) β(a, b) = ab mod d is well defined (d | m, d | n) and
    bilinear, so β̃ : Z/m ⊗ Z/n → Z/d is onto (β̃(1 ⊗ 1) = 1). Every a ⊗ b =
    ab(1 ⊗ 1), so the tensor is cyclic, generated by 1 ⊗ 1; and m(1 ⊗ 1) =
    m ⊗ 1 = 0, n(1 ⊗ 1) = 1 ⊗ n = 0, so d(1 ⊗ 1) = 0 (d = um + vn). A cyclic
    group of order ≤ d mapping onto Z/d is ≅ Z/d. (c) For q ∈ Q, a ∈ Z/n:
    q ⊗ a = (q/n)·n ⊗ a = (q/n) ⊗ na = (q/n) ⊗ 0 = 0; pure tensors generate,
    so Q ⊗ Z/n = 0. (Divisible ⊗ torsion = 0 by the same trick.)
    (d) Tensor 0 → I → R → R/I → 0 with M; right exactness (D10) gives
    I ⊗ M → M → R/I ⊗ M → 0 exact, and the image of I ⊗ M → M, i ⊗ m ↦
    im, is IM. (e) C ≅ R[x]/(x² + 1), so C ⊗_R C ≅ C[x]/(x² + 1) =
    C[x]/((x − i)(x + i)) ≅ C × C by the Chinese remainder theorem.
    Explicitly (1 ⊗ 1 + i ⊗ i)(1 ⊗ 1 − i ⊗ i) = 1 ⊗ 1 − i² ⊗ i² = 0: zero
    divisors, so it is not a field. Common mistakes: believing every
    element is a pure tensor (e₁ ⊗ e₁ + e₂ ⊗ e₂ in R² ⊗ R² is not);
    ignoring the ring: 2 ⊗ 1 = 1 ⊗ 2 = 0 in Z ⊗ Z/2, but 2 ⊗ 1 ≠ 0 in
    2Z ⊗ Z/2 ≅ Z/2 (2 is not a multiple of 2 inside 2Z). Now you try:
    Z/12 ⊗ Z/18 and Q ⊗_Z Q. (Z/6; Q: a/b ⊗ c/d = ac/(bd) ⊗ 1)

D9. (a) Prove the splitting lemma: for a short exact sequence 0 → A →f B
    →g C → 0 of R-modules, a retraction r (rf = 1_A) exists iff a section
    s (gs = 1_C) exists iff B ≅ A ⊕ C compatibly with f and g. (b) Show
    that 0 → Z/2 → Z/4 → Z/2 → 0 and 0 → Z →n Z → Z/n → 0 (n ≥ 2) do not
    split, but every short exact sequence ending in a free module does.
    (c) State the snake lemma and apply it to multiplication by n on a
    short exact sequence of abelian groups.
    Answer: (a) B = f(A) ⊕ ker r = f(A) ⊕ s(C); (b) Z/4 ≇ Z/2 ⊕ Z/2, Z ≇
    Z ⊕ Z/n; lift a basis; (c) 0 → A[n] → B[n] → C[n] → A/nA → B/nB →
    C/nC → 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) Given r, write b = fr(b) + (b − fr(b)); the
    second term lies in ker r since rf = 1. And f(A) ∩ ker r = 0: if
    r(f(a)) = 0 then a = rf(a) = 0. So B = f(A) ⊕ ker r, and g maps ker r
    isomorphically onto C (g is onto and kills f(A)); its inverse gives a
    section. Given s, b − sg(b) ∈ ker g = f(A), so B = f(A) ⊕ s(C), and r
    := f⁻¹ ∘ (projection to f(A)) is a retraction. The direct sum form
    gives both maps at once. (b) If 0 → Z/2 → Z/4 → Z/2 → 0 split, Z/4 ≅
    Z/2 ⊕ Z/2, but Z/4 has an element of order 4. If 0 → Z → Z → Z/n → 0
    split, Z ≅ Z ⊕ Z/n would have torsion. If C is free on a basis {cᵢ},
    choose bᵢ with g(bᵢ) = cᵢ and define s(cᵢ) = bᵢ; then gs = 1. (The same
    works for projective C, by definition.) (c) SNAKE LEMMA: given a
    commutative diagram with exact rows A → B → C → 0 and 0 → A′ → B′ → C′
    and vertical maps α, β, γ, there is an exact sequence
       ker α → ker β → ker γ →δ coker α → coker β → coker γ.
    The connecting map: for c ∈ ker γ lift to b ∈ B; then β(b) maps to
    γ(c) = 0 in C′, so β(b) = f′(a′) for a unique a′ ∈ A′; put δ(c) = [a′].
    Another lift b + f(a) changes a′ by α(a), so δ is well defined. With
    α, β, γ all = multiplication by n: ker = n-torsion A[n], coker =
    A/nA. For 0 → Z →p Z → Z/p → 0 and n = p the six terms are 0, 0, Z/p,
    Z/p, Z/p, Z/p with maps δ = iso, 0, iso. FIVE LEMMA: in a commutative
    ladder of two exact rows of five terms, if the outer four vertical maps
    are isomorphisms, so is the middle one. Common mistakes: thinking any
    B ≅ A ⊕ C forces splitting (see M12 p)); forgetting to check that δ
    is well defined. Now you try: the snake sequence for n = 2 on 0 → Z/2
    → Z/4 → Z/2 → 0. (all six terms Z/2; maps iso, 0, iso, 0, iso)

D10. (a) Show that Hom_R(M, −) is left exact but Hom_Z(Z/2, −) is not
     exact. (b) Define projective, injective and flat modules; prove P is
     projective iff it is a direct summand of a free module, and show Z/2
     is a projective, non-free Z/6-module. (c) Using Baer's criterion show
     that over Z injective = divisible; deduce Q and Q/Z are injective and
     Z is not. (d) Show Q is flat but not projective over Z, and that
     − ⊗ N is right exact.
     Answer: (a) 0 → 0 → 0 → Z/2 is not onto at the end; (b) Z/6 ≅ Z/2 ⊕
     Z/3; (c) extend nZ → I iff x = ny is solvable; (d) torsion-free but
     not free; ⊗ is a left adjoint.
     Teacher explains: (a) For 0 → A →f B →g C exact, 0 → Hom(M, A) →
     Hom(M, B) → Hom(M, C) is exact: if f ∘ φ = 0 then φ = 0 (f injective);
     if g ∘ ψ = 0 then ψ(M) ⊆ ker g = f(A), so ψ = f ∘ φ. Apply Hom(Z/2, −)
     to 0 → Z →2 Z → Z/2 → 0: Hom(Z/2, Z) = 0, so we get 0 → 0 → 0 → Z/2,
     not onto: the identity Z/2 → Z/2 does not lift to Z. (b) P is
     PROJECTIVE if every map P → C lifts along every surjection B → C
     (⇔ Hom(P, −) exact); I is INJECTIVE if every map A → I extends along
     every injection A → B (⇔ Hom(−, I) exact); M is FLAT if − ⊗ M
     preserves injections. Free modules are projective (define the lift
     on a basis). If P is projective, choose a surjection F → P from a
     free F; lifting 1_P gives a section, so F ≅ P ⊕ ker by D9. A summand
     of a projective is projective (extend by zero). Over Z/6, Z/6 ≅ Z/2 ⊕
     Z/3 (CRT), so Z/2 is projective, yet not free: free Z/6-modules have
     order 6ᵏ. (c) BAER: I is injective iff every map from an ideal J → I
     extends to R. Ideals of Z are nZ; a map nZ → I is n ↦ x and extends to
     Z iff x = n·y for some y ∈ I. So injective ⇔ divisible. Q and Q/Z are
     divisible; Z is not (2y = 1 has no solution). (d) Over a PID flat ⇔
     torsion-free, or directly: Q = ⋃(1/n!)Z is a directed union of free
     modules and ⊗ commutes with directed unions. Over a PID submodules of
     free modules are free, so projective ⇒ free; but any two elements of
     Q are Z-dependent (for a/b, c/d: bc·(a/b) − ad·(c/d) = 0), so a free Q
     would be Z, which is not divisible. Right exactness: Hom(M ⊗ N, P) ≅
     Hom(M, Hom(N, P)) and Hom(N, P) is left exact, so the contravariant
     functor Hom(− ⊗ N, P) is left exact for every P, which means − ⊗ N
     sends A → B → C → 0 to an exact sequence. Common mistakes:
     "projective = free" (false over Z/6); "Z/n is never injective"
     (it is injective over Z/n, though not over Z). Now you try: is Z/4 a
     flat Z-module? (no: Z →2 Z ⊗ Z/4 gives Z/4 →2 Z/4, not injective)

D11. (a) Define categories, functors and natural transformations; show
     that ev : V → V**, v ↦ (φ ↦ φ(v)) is natural but that no natural
     isomorphism V → V* exists. (b) Compute products and coproducts in Set,
     Grp, Ab, CRing and the poset (N, |). (c) State the Yoneda lemma and
     the definition of an adjunction, give three examples, and prove that
     a left adjoint preserves coproducts.
     Answer: (a) T** ∘ ev_V = ev_W ∘ T; the zero map kills naturality of
     V → V*; (b) (×, ⊔), (×, *), (⊕, ⊕), (×, ⊗_Z), (gcd, lcm); (c) Hom(FX,
     Y) ≅ Hom(X, GY).
     Teacher explains: (a) A CATEGORY has objects, sets Hom(X, Y),
     associative composition and identities. A FUNCTOR F sends objects to
     objects and morphisms to morphisms preserving composition and
     identities (contravariant ones reverse arrows). A NATURAL
     TRANSFORMATION η : F ⇒ G is a family η_X : FX → GX with G(f) ∘ η_X =
     η_Y ∘ F(f) for every f : X → Y. For T : V → W linear and ψ ∈ W*:
        T**(ev_V(v))(ψ) = ev_V(v)(ψ ∘ T) = ψ(Tv) = ev_W(Tv)(ψ),
     so ev is natural (an isomorphism for finite dimension). V ↦ V* is
     contravariant, so naturality of η_V : V → V* would demand η_V = T* ∘
     η_W ∘ T for every T : V → W; with T = 0 this forces η_V = 0, not an
     isomorphism when V ≠ 0. (b) The PRODUCT X × Y has projections such
     that maps Z → X, Z → Y correspond to one map Z → X × Y; the COPRODUCT
     dually. Set: cartesian product and disjoint union. Grp: direct
     product and FREE PRODUCT (Z/2 * Z/2 is the infinite dihedral group).
     Ab and R-Mod: ⊕ is both (finitely many factors). CRing: R × S and R
     ⊗_Z S (maps R → T and S → T combine to r ⊗ s ↦ f(r)g(s)). In (N, |),
     a morphism a → b means a | b: product = gcd, coproduct = lcm, initial
     object 1, terminal object 0. (c) YONEDA: for F : C → Set,
     Nat(Hom(A, −), F) ≅ F(A) via η ↦ η_A(1_A); so Hom(A, −) ≅ Hom(B, −)
     implies A ≅ B. An ADJUNCTION F ⊣ G is a bijection Hom_D(FX, Y) ≅
     Hom_C(X, GY) natural in X and Y. Examples: free group ⊣ forgetful
     Grp → Set; abelianisation ⊣ inclusion Ab → Grp; − ⊗_R N ⊣ Hom_R(N,
     −). Proof for coproducts: for every Y,
        Hom(F(X₁ ⊔ X₂), Y) ≅ Hom(X₁ ⊔ X₂, GY) ≅ Hom(X₁, GY) × Hom(X₂, GY)
                          ≅ Hom(FX₁, Y) × Hom(FX₂, Y) ≅ Hom(FX₁ ⊔ FX₂, Y),
     naturally in Y, so F(X₁ ⊔ X₂) ≅ FX₁ ⊔ FX₂ by Yoneda. The same argument
     works for all colimits (cokernels included): hence ⊗ distributes over
     ⊕ and is right exact, and the free group on S ⊔ T is F(S) * F(T).
     Common mistakes: taking the direct product as the coproduct of
     groups; expecting products to exist everywhere (fields: there is no
     field mapping to both F₂ and F₃). Now you try: the coproduct of Z[x]
     and Z[y] in CRing. (Z[x] ⊗_Z Z[y] ≅ Z[x, y])

D12. (a) Compute the homology of the complex 0 → Z →d₂ Z² →d₁ Z → 0 with
     d₂(a) = (2a, 2a), d₁(x, y) = 3x − 3y. (b) Explain how a short exact
     sequence of complexes gives a long exact sequence in homology.
     (c) Define Tor and Ext via projective resolutions and compute
     Torᵢ^Z(Z/m, Z/n), Extⁱ_Z(Z/m, Z/n) and Ext¹(Z/n, Z). (d) Interpret
     Ext¹(Z/p, Z/p). (e) Compute Torₙ^R(k, k) for R = k[x]/(x²).
     Answer: (a) H₂ = 0, H₁ = Z/2, H₀ = Z/3; (b) snake lemma; (c) Tor₀ =
     Tor₁ = Z/d, Ext⁰ = Ext¹ = Z/d, d = gcd(m, n), higher ones 0;
     Ext¹(Z/n, Z) = Z/n; (d) p extension classes; (e) k for every n.
     Teacher explains: (a) d₁d₂(a) = 6a − 6a = 0 ✓. H₂ = ker d₂ = 0. ker d₁
     = {(x, x)} ≅ Z and im d₂ = {(2a, 2a)}, so H₁ ≅ Z/2. im d₁ = 3Z, so H₀
     = Z/3. (Check: 1 − 2 + 1 = 0 = rank H₂ − rank H₁ + rank H₀.) (b) Given
     0 → A_• → B_• → C_• → 0 exact in each degree, the snake lemma applied
     to the maps dₙ between the cokernel and kernel rows gives
        ... → Hₙ(A) → Hₙ(B) → Hₙ(C) →∂ Hₙ₋₁(A) → Hₙ₋₁(B) → ...
     where ∂[c] = [a] with a the element whose image is d(b), b a lift of
     c: "lift, apply d, pull back". (c) A PROJECTIVE RESOLUTION ... → P₁ →
     P₀ → A → 0 is exact with Pᵢ projective; delete A, apply the functor,
     take homology: Torₙ(A, B) = Hₙ(P_• ⊗ B), Extⁿ(A, B) = Hⁿ(Hom(P_•,
     B)). Any two resolutions are chain homotopy equivalent (comparison
     theorem), so the answer is independent of choices; Tor₀ = A ⊗ B and
     Ext⁰ = Hom(A, B). For A = Z/m: 0 → Z →m Z → Z/m → 0. Tensoring with
     Z/n: 0 → Z/n →m Z/n → 0, so Tor₀ = Z/n / m(Z/n) ≅ Z/d and Tor₁ =
     ker(m on Z/n) = {x : n | mx} = (n/d)Z/nZ ≅ Z/d. Applying Hom(−, Z/n):
     0 → Z/n →m Z/n → 0, so Hom = Z/d, Ext¹ = coker ≅ Z/d. Resolutions of
     length 1 exist for every abelian group (subgroups of free are free),
     so Torᵢ = Extⁱ = 0 for i ≥ 2. For Ext¹(Z/n, Z): 0 → Z →n Z → 0 has
     cokernel Z/n. (d) Ext¹(C, A) is in bijection with equivalence classes
     of extensions 0 → A → B → C → 0, the zero class being the split one.
     Ext¹(Z/p, Z/p) = Z/p: the split class B = Z/p ⊕ Z/p, and p − 1
     non-split classes, all with B ≅ Z/p² (they differ in the choice of
     the map Z/p → Z/p², 1 ↦ kp). So p classes but only 2 middle groups.
     (e) ... → R →x R →x R → k → 0 is a free resolution (ker x = im x =
     (x)). Tensoring with k turns every x into 0, so Torₙ(k, k) = k for
     all n ≥ 0: k has infinite projective dimension over R. Common
     mistakes: forgetting to delete A before applying the functor;
     confusing extension classes with isomorphism types of B; Tor₀ is the
     tensor product, not Tor₁. Now you try: Tor₁^Z(Z/12, Z/18) and
     Ext¹_Z(Z/4, Z/6). (Z/6; Z/2)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Classify each group: NS (nilpotent, hence solvable), S (solvable, not
    nilpotent) or X (not solvable).
    a) Z/60                 b) S₃                  c) Q₈
    d) D₈ (order 8)         e) A₄                  f) S₄
    g) S₅                   h) A₅ × Z/2            i) D₂₄ (order 24)
    j) D₃₂ (order 32)       k) GL₂(F₃)             l) SL₂(F₃)
    m) SL₂(F₅)              n) UT₃(Z) (Heisenberg) o) invertible upper
                                                      triangular 2 × 2
                                                      real matrices
    p) any group of order 15                       q) any group of order p⁵
    r) Z/3 ⋊ Z/4 (non-abelian)                     s) S₃ × S₃
    t) the free group F₂    u) any group of order 45   v) S₃ × Z/5
    Answers:
      a) NS     b) S      c) NS     d) NS     e) S      f) S
      g) X      h) X      i) S (12 is not a power of 2)             j) NS
      k) S (centre of order 2, GL₂(F₃)/Z ≅ S₄ has trivial centre)
      l) S (n₃ = 4)       m) X (SL₂(F₅)/{±I} ≅ A₅)      n) NS (class 2)
      o) S (commutators are unipotent; the centre is the scalars and
         the quotient ≅ {x ↦ ax + b} has trivial centre)
      p) NS (cyclic)      q) NS     r) S (n₂ = 3)      s) S
      t) X (F₂′ is free of infinite rank, the series never ends)
      u) NS (abelian)     v) S

      r) 1 < Z/2 = Z₁ = Z₂ = ... (S₃ has trivial centre): never reaches G

         leaving 8 elements, which must form the unique Sylow 2-subgroup

M8. Exact sequences.
    a) all abelian G with 0 → Z/3 → G → Z/4 → 0
    b) all groups G with a normal subgroup Z/3 and G/Z/3 ≅ Z/4
    c) all abelian G with 0 → Z/2 → G → Z/2 → 0
    d) all abelian G with 0 → Z/2 → G → Z/4 → 0
    e) all abelian G with 0 → Z → G → Z/n → 0
    f) 0 → Q² → Q⁵ → V → Q³ → 0 exact: dim V
    g) the cokernel of Z/4 → Z/8, 1 ↦ 2
    h) does 0 → Z →(2,1) Z ⊕ Z → C → 0 split? Identify C.
    i) does 0 → 2Z → Z → Z/2 → 0 split?
    j) ker and coker of multiplication by 4 on Z/12
    k) ker and coker of Z/6 → Z/9, 1 ↦ 3
    l) the snake sequence for ×3 on 0 → Z/3 → Z/9 → Z/3 → 0
    m) apply Hom(−, Z) to 0 → Z →n Z → Z/n → 0
    n) apply − ⊗ Z/n to 0 → Z →n Z → Z/n → 0
    o) apply Hom(Z/n, −) to 0 → Z →n Z → Z/n → 0
    p) exact 0 → Z/2 → A → Z → 0: must it split?
    Answers:
      a) Z/12 only           b) Z/12 and Z/3 ⋊ Z/4
      c) Z/4, Z/2 ⊕ Z/2      d) Z/8, Z/2 ⊕ Z/4
      e) Z ⊕ Z/k for each k | n (e.g. 1 ↦ (n/k, 1) in Z ⊕ Z/k)
      f) 6 (2 − 5 + dim V − 3 = 0)          g) Z/2
      h) yes; C ≅ Z via (x, y) ↦ x − 2y (free quotient)
      i) no (Z has no element of order 2)
      j) ker = {0, 3, 6, 9} ≅ Z/4, coker ≅ Z/4
      k) ker = {0, 3} ≅ Z/2, coker ≅ Z/3
      l) all six terms Z/3; maps iso, 0, iso, 0, iso
      m) 0 → 0 → Z →n Z → Ext¹(Z/n, Z) ≅ Z/n → 0
      n) 0 → Tor₁(Z/n, Z/n) ≅ Z/n → Z/n →0 Z/n → Z/n → 0
      o) 0 → 0 → 0 → Z/n → Ext¹(Z/n, Z) ≅ Z/n → 0 (the connecting map is
         an isomorphism)
      p) yes: the quotient Z is free, so A ≅ Z ⊕ Z/2

M9. Projective? Injective? Flat? (answer three Y/N)
    a) Z over Z              b) Q over Z             c) Q/Z over Z
    d) Z/n over Z (n ≥ 2)    e) Z/n over Z/n         f) Z/2 over Z/6
    g) Z/2 over Z/4          h) Z^N over Z           i) k[x] over k[x]
    j) Z[1/2] over Z         k) k over k[x]/(x²)     l) k[x]/(x²) over
                                                        itself
    m) a vector space over a field                   n) Z_(p) over Z
    o) Q[x] over Z[x]        p) Z ⊕ Q over Z
    Answers:
      a) Y N Y    b) N Y Y    c) N Y N    d) N N N    e) Y Y Y
      f) Y Y Y (Z/6 ≅ Z/2 × Z/3)          g) N N N (Baer: 2 ↦ 1 on
         2Z/4 does not extend)
      h) N N Y (Baer-Specker: Z^N is not free)
      i) Y N Y (not divisible by x)
      j) N N Y    k) N N N (over a self-injective ring projective ⇔
         injective)                       l) Y Y Y     m) Y Y Y
      n) N N Y (divisible by primes ≠ p only)
      o) N N Y (a localisation of Z[x])   p) N N Y

M10. Categories, functors, universal properties.
     a) product and coproduct in Set, Grp, Ab, CRing, Top
     b) product and coproduct in (N, |) and in (P(X), ⊆)
     c) initial and terminal objects in Set, Grp, CRing, Fields, Top, (N, |)
     d) Z/2 * Z/2: finite or infinite?
     e) covariant or contravariant: Hom(A, −), Hom(−, B), V ↦ V*,
        (X, x₀) ↦ π₁(X, x₀), X ↦ C(X, R), G ↦ G^ab
     f) left adjoint of the forgetful functor Grp → Set; Ab → Set;
        R-Mod → Set
     g) left and right adjoints of the forgetful functor Top → Set
     h) left adjoint of the inclusion Ab → Grp
     i) right adjoint of − ⊗_R N; right adjoint of the group ring
        functor Grp → Ring, G ↦ Z[G]
     j) natural or not: V → V**; V → V*; G → G^ab; det : GL_n(R) → R^×
     k) Nat(Hom(A, −), Hom(B, −)) by Yoneda
     l) lim Z/pⁿ; colim(Z →2 Z →2 ...); colim(Z/p ⊂ Z/p² ⊂ ...)
     m) equaliser and coequaliser of f, g : A → B in Ab
     n) pullback of f : X → Z, g : Y → Z in Set; of two subsets A, B ⊆ Z
     o) is the forgetful functor Grp → Set faithful? full?
     p) does the product of F₂ and F₃ exist in the category of fields?
     Answers:
       a) Set: ×, ⊔; Grp: ×, free product *; Ab: ⊕, ⊕; CRing: ×, ⊗_Z;
          Top: product topology, disjoint union
       b) gcd, lcm; A ∩ B, A ∪ B
       c) Set: ∅, point; Grp: trivial group (both, a zero object); CRing:
          Z, zero ring; Fields: neither; Top: ∅, point; (N, |): 1, 0
       d) infinite (the infinite dihedral group)
       e) cov, contra, contra, cov, contra, cov
       f) free group F(S); free abelian group Z^(S); free module R^(S)
       g) discrete topology (left), indiscrete topology (right)
       h) abelianisation G ↦ G/G′      i) Hom_R(N, −); units R ↦ R^×
       j) natural; not natural; natural; natural (in R)
       k) Hom(B, A)
       l) the p-adic integers Z_p; Z[1/2]; the Prüfer group Z/p^∞
       m) ker(f − g), coker(f − g)
       n) {(x, y) : f(x) = g(y)}; A ∩ B
       o) faithful, not full (not every map of sets is a homomorphism)
       p) no (no field maps to both: different characteristics)

M11. Homology, Tor and Ext (over Z unless stated).
     a) Tor₁(Z/12, Z/18)      b) Tor₁(Z/5, Z/7)       c) Tor₁(Z/n, Q)

     p) homology of 0 → Z →2 Z →0 Z →3 Z → 0 (degrees 3, 2, 1, 0)
     q) homology of 0 → Z → Z² → Z → 0, a ↦ (a, a), (x, y) ↦ x − y
     r) projective dimension of Z/n over Z; of Z/2 over Z/4; of k over
        k[x, y]

     Answers:

       f) 0        g) Z/2      h) Z/n      i) 0 (Q injective)

       m) k for every n ≥ 0    n) Z/2 for every n ≥ 0
