M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) Every nilpotent group is abelian.
     b) Every solvable group is nilpotent.
     c) Subgroups and quotients of nilpotent groups are nilpotent.
     d) An extension of a nilpotent group by a nilpotent group is
        nilpotent.
     e) A finite group all of whose Sylow subgroups are abelian is abelian.
     f) A finite group whose composition factors are all cyclic is
        solvable.
     g) If N ◁ G with N and G/N abelian, then G is abelian.
     h) Every f.g. torsion-free Z-module is free.
     i) Every torsion-free abelian group is free.
     j) Every projective module is free.
     k) Every flat module is projective.
     l) If f is injective then f ⊗ 1_N is injective.
     m) If M ⊗ N = 0 then M = 0 or N = 0.
     n) Every element of M ⊗ N has the form m ⊗ n.
     o) Hom_R(M, −) is exact for every M.
     p) If 0 → A → B → C → 0 is exact and B ≅ A ⊕ C, then it splits.
     q) The coproduct in Grp is the direct product.
     r) Left adjoints preserve colimits.
     s) Tor₁^Z(A, B) = 0 whenever A is torsion-free.
     t) Every group of order p²q (p, q distinct primes) is solvable.
     Answers:
       a) F: Q₈       b) F: S₃      c) T      d) F: S₃ (Z/3 by Z/2)
       e) F: S₃       f) T          g) F: S₃
       h) T (structure theorem)     i) F: Q
       j) F: Z/2 over Z/6           k) F: Q over Z
       l) F: Z →2 Z becomes Z/2 →0 Z/2 after ⊗ Z/2
       m) F: Z/2 ⊗ Z/3 = 0          n) F: e₁ ⊗ e₁ + e₂ ⊗ e₂ in R² ⊗ R²
       o) F: Hom(Z/2, −) on 0 → Z → Z → Z/2 → 0
       p) F: B = ⊕_N (Z/2 ⊕ Z/4), A = Z/2 = 2·(first Z/4); then B/A ≅ B ≅
          A ⊕ B/A, but A ⊆ 2B, while a direct summand Z/2 cannot lie in 2B
       q) F: Z/2 * Z/2 is infinite   r) T
       s) T (torsion-free = flat over Z)
       t) T (Sylow counting; also Burnside's pᵃqᵇ theorem)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: keep a small zoo on the board and test every statement on
  it: S₃ (solvable, not nilpotent), Q₈ and D₈ (nilpotent, not abelian),
  A₄ (a normal Sylow 2 but not nilpotent), A₅ (simple), Z/2 over Z/6
  (projective, not free), Q over Z (flat and injective, not projective),
  0 → Z →2 Z → Z/2 → 0 (the sequence that breaks Hom(Z/2, −) and − ⊗
  Z/2). For modules over a PID always compute Δᵢ = gcd of i × i minors
  first; for tensor products always ask "what does 1 ⊗ 1 generate and
  what kills it?"; for Tor and Ext write the resolution 0 → Z →m Z →
  Z/m → 0, DELETE Z/m, apply the functor and take homology. In category
  theory, draw the universal-property triangle before naming the object.
  Harder: prove the Schreier refinement theorem and deduce Jordan-Hölder;
  prove that a finite group is nilpotent iff all maximal subgroups are
  normal (via the Frattini argument); prove Burnside's pᵃqᵇ theorem with
  characters; prove the structure theorem via Smith normal form over a
  Euclidean ring and then a PID; prove the snake lemma and the long exact
  sequence of Tor and Ext; show Ext¹ classifies extensions (Baer sum);
  prove that every module embeds in an injective one (Q/Z-duality); and
  prove the Yoneda lemma and the adjoint functor characterisation of
  limits.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.6 — HOMOLOGICAL ALGEBRA: EXT, TOR, DERIVED FUNCTORS, DERIVED
CATEGORIES (INTRODUCTION)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - CHAIN COMPLEXES over a ring R (or in an abelian category): C• with
    dₙ : Cₙ → Cₙ₋₁, d ∘ d = 0, HOMOLOGY Hₙ = ker dₙ / im dₙ₊₁ (cohomological
    indexing Hⁿ with dⁿ : Cⁿ → Cⁿ⁺¹). Chain maps induce maps on Hₙ;
    CHAIN HOMOTOPIC maps (f − g = dh + hd) induce the SAME map. A
    QUASI-ISOMORPHISM is a chain map inducing isomorphisms on all Hₙ.
  - EXACTNESS TOOLS: the SNAKE LEMMA (a map of short exact sequences gives
    0 → ker a → ker b → ker c →δ coker a → coker b → coker c → 0), the
    FIVE LEMMA, and the LONG EXACT HOMOLOGY SEQUENCE of a short exact
    sequence of complexes 0 → C′ → C → C″ → 0:
    ... → Hₙ(C′) → Hₙ(C) → Hₙ(C″) →δ Hₙ₋₁(C′) → ...
  - PROJECTIVE modules (Hom(P, −) exact ⇔ direct summand of a free
    module), INJECTIVE modules (Hom(−, I) exact; BAER'S CRITERION: enough
    to extend maps from ideals), FLAT modules (− ⊗ M exact). Free ⇒
    projective ⇒ flat. Over a PID: projective = free, flat = torsion-free,
    injective = DIVISIBLE (Q, Q/Z, Z/p^∞). Every module has a projective
    and an injective RESOLUTION ("enough projectives/injectives").
  - COMPARISON THEOREM: a map M → N lifts to a chain map between
    projective resolutions P• → Q•, unique up to homotopy. HORSESHOE
    LEMMA: resolutions of M′ and M″ combine into one of M for
    0 → M′ → M → M″ → 0. Hence derived functors are well defined.
  - DERIVED FUNCTORS: for F right exact (e.g. − ⊗ N), LₙF(M) = Hₙ(F(P•));
    for F left exact (Hom(M, −), Hom(−, N), fixed points), RⁿF(M) =
    Hⁿ(F(I•)) (covariant) or Hⁿ(F(P•)) (contravariant in M). L₀F = F,
    R⁰F = F, and a short exact sequence gives a LONG EXACT SEQUENCE.
    They form UNIVERSAL δ-FUNCTORS (Grothendieck: effaceable ⇒ universal).
    DIMENSION SHIFTING: if 0 → K → P → M → 0 with P projective then
    Extⁿ⁺¹(M, N) ≅ Extⁿ(K, N) for n ≥ 1.
  - TOR: Torₙᴿ(M, N) = Lₙ(− ⊗ N)(M), BALANCED (may resolve either
    variable), symmetric. Tor₁ measures failure of left exactness of ⊗.
    Over Z: Tor₁(Z/m, Z/n) ≅ Z/gcd(m, n), Tor₁(Z/n, A) ≅ A[n] = {a : na =
    0}, Tor₁(Q/Z, A) ≅ torsion of A, Torₙ = 0 for n ≥ 2. General:
    Tor₁ᴿ(R/I, M) = ker(I ⊗ M → M), Tor₁ᴿ(R/I, R/J) ≅ (I ∩ J)/IJ.
  - FLATNESS via Tor: M flat ⇔ Tor₁(M, −) = 0 ⇔ Tor₁(R/I, M) = 0 for all
    (finitely generated) ideals I ⇔ I ⊗ M → M injective. Q is flat not
    projective over Z; Z/n is not flat; f.p. flat ⇒ projective.
  - EXT: Extⁿᴿ(M, N) = Rⁿ Hom(M, −)(N), computable from a projective
    resolution of M OR an injective resolution of N. Over Z: Ext¹(Z/n, A)
    ≅ A/nA, Ext¹(Z/m, Z/n) ≅ Z/gcd(m, n), Ext¹(Z/n, Z) ≅ Z/n, Ext¹(Z, A) = 0,
    Extⁿ = 0 for n ≥ 2, Ext¹(Q/Z, Z) ≅ Ẑ, Ext¹(Q, Z) ≅ Ẑ/Z ≠ 0 (although
    Hom(Q, Z) = 0).
  - EXT¹ CLASSIFIES EXTENSIONS: equivalence classes of 0 → N → E → M → 0
    ↔ Ext¹(M, N), split extension ↔ 0, group law = BAER SUM. Yoneda:
    Extⁿ ↔ n-fold exact sequences, composition = Yoneda product. E.g.
    Ext¹(Z/2, Z/4) = Z/2: extensions Z/4 ⊕ Z/2 (split) and Z/8.
  - HOMOLOGICAL DIMENSIONS: pd M = shortest projective resolution; id N;
    gl.dim R = sup pd M = sup{n : Extⁿ ≠ 0}. Semisimple ⇔ gl.dim 0;
    HEREDITARY (PID, Dedekind) ⇔ gl.dim ≤ 1; HILBERT SYZYGY THEOREM:
    gl.dim k[x₁, ..., xₙ] = n; gl.dim Z/4 = gl.dim k[x]/(x²) = ∞ (periodic
    resolutions ... → R →x R →x R → k → 0).
  - KOSZUL COMPLEX: for x₁, ..., xₙ in R = k[x₁, ..., xₙ] it is a free
    resolution of k with ranks C(n, i); hence Torᵢᴿ(k, k) ≅ k^C(n, i)
    and Extⁱᴿ(k, k) ≅ k^C(n, i). For a REGULAR SEQUENCE it resolves R/I.
  - APPLICATIONS: UNIVERSAL COEFFICIENT THEOREMS Hₙ(X; G) ≅ Hₙ(X) ⊗ G ⊕
    Tor(Hₙ₋₁(X), G), Hⁿ(X; G) ≅ Hom(Hₙ(X), G) ⊕ Ext(Hₙ₋₁(X), G) (split,
    not naturally); KÜNNETH Hₙ(X × Y) ≅ ⊕ Hᵢ(X) ⊗ Hⱼ(Y) ⊕ ⊕ Tor(Hᵢ, Hⱼ)
    (i + j = n, resp. n − 1). GROUP COHOMOLOGY Hⁿ(G, M) = Extⁿ_ZG(Z, M),
    Hₙ(G, M) = Torₙ^ZG(Z, M); for cyclic G periodic of period 2.
  - HOMOTOPY CATEGORY K(A): complexes, maps modulo homotopy; TRIANGULATED
    with shift X[1] (X[1]ⁿ = Xⁿ⁺¹, d ↦ −d) and MAPPING CONE Cone(f)ⁿ =
    Xⁿ⁺¹ ⊕ Yⁿ; DISTINGUISHED TRIANGLES X → Y → Cone(f) → X[1] give long
    exact cohomology sequences; f is a quasi-iso ⇔ Cone(f) is acyclic.
  - DERIVED CATEGORY D(A) = K(A)[quasi-isos⁻¹] (Verdier localisation,
    roofs X ← X′ → Y). A ↦ A[0] is fully faithful; Hom_D(M, N[n]) ≅
    Extⁿ(M, N) (0 for n < 0); D(A) is triangulated but NOT abelian. Total
    derived functors RF, LF (RHom, ⊗ᴸ) with Hⁱ(RHom(M, N)) = Extⁱ and
    H⁻ⁱ(M ⊗ᴸ N) = Torᵢ. Over a hereditary ring every complex is ≅ ⊕
    Hⁿ(X)[−n]. Quasi-iso ≠ homotopy equivalence: [Z →2 Z] ≅ Z/2 in D(Z)
    but not in K(Z). Grothendieck spectral sequence R(G∘F) ⇐ RG ∘ RF.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) State and prove the snake lemma (construction of δ). (b) Apply
    it to the map from 0 → Z →2 Z → Z/2 → 0 to itself given by
    multiplication by 2 on all three terms; list all six groups and find
    δ. (c) Deduce the long exact homology sequence.
    Answer: (b) kernels 0, 0, Z/2; cokernels Z/2, Z/2, Z/2; δ is an
    isomorphism, coker a → coker b is 0, coker b → coker c is an iso.
    Teacher explains: Rows 0 → A →i B →p C → 0 and 0 → A′ →i′ B′ →p′ C′
    → 0, verticals a, b, c. For z ∈ ker c: choose y ∈ B with p(y) = z
    (p onto). Then p′(b(y)) = c(p(y)) = 0, so b(y) = i′(x′) for a unique
    x′ ∈ A′ (exactness, i′ injective). Put δ(z) = [x′] ∈ coker a. Well
    defined: another lift is y + i(x), which changes x′ by a(x), zero in
    coker a. Exactness at each of the six spots is a diagram chase of the
    same kind (e.g. δ(z) = 0 ⇔ x′ = a(x) ⇔ y − i(x) ∈ ker b maps to z).
    (b) All verticals are "×2". On Z, ×2 is injective with cokernel Z/2;
    on Z/2 it is 0, kernel = cokernel = Z/2. The snake reads
       0 → 0 → 0 → Z/2 →δ Z/2 → Z/2 → Z/2 → 0.
    Since ker b = 0, δ is injective, hence an iso; so coker a → coker b
    is 0, so coker b → coker c is injective and onto. Explicitly: z = 1,
    lift y = 1 ∈ Z, b(y) = 2 = i′(1), δ(1) = [1] ≠ 0 ✓. (c) For 0 → C′ →
    C → C″ → 0 of complexes, apply the snake to the rows
    Cₙ/im d → Z_{n−1} (cycles); the kernels are Hₙ and the cokernels
    Hₙ₋₁, giving ... → Hₙ(C′) → Hₙ(C) → Hₙ(C″) →δ Hₙ₋₁(C′) → ... .
    Common mistakes: defining δ without checking independence of the lift;
    forgetting that the snake starts with 0 only if i is injective and
    ends with 0 only if p′ is onto. Now you try: for the same rows with
    verticals "×3", find the six groups. (0, 0, 0, Z/3, Z/3, 0)

D2. (a) Prove that a Z-module is injective iff it is divisible, using
    Baer's criterion. (b) Embed Z/n into an injective module and write a
    minimal injective resolution of Z and of Z/n. (c) Show Z/2 is not
    injective over Z/4 and not projective over Z/4, but is projective
    over Z/6.
    Answer: (b) 0 → Z → Q → Q/Z → 0; 0 → Z/n → Q/Z →·n Q/Z → 0 (1 ↦ 1/n).
    (c) 0 → Z/2 → Z/4 → Z/2 → 0 does not split; Z/6 ≅ Z/2 × Z/3.
    Teacher explains: BAER: I is injective iff every R-map f : J → I from
    an ideal J extends to R. (a) Over Z an ideal is nZ. If I is divisible
    and f : nZ → I, pick y with ny = f(n); then g(k) = ky extends f.
    Conversely, if I is injective, a ∈ I and n ≠ 0, the map nZ → I,
    n ↦ a extends to g : Z → I and a = g(n) = n g(1): divisible. (b) Q
    and Q/Z are divisible, hence injective. 0 → Z → Q → Q/Z → 0 shows
    id Z = 1 (Z itself is not divisible). For Z/n: 1 ↦ 1/n embeds Z/n in
    Q/Z; the cokernel (Q/Z)/(⟨1/n⟩) ≅ Q/Z via x ↦ nx. Over a PID quotients
    of divisible groups are divisible, so id ≤ 1 for every module:
    gl.dim Z = 1. (c) Over Z/4 the sequence 0 → Z/2 →·2 Z/4 → Z/2 → 0 is
    exact; if Z/2 were injective the injection would split, if projective
    the surjection would split, and either would give Z/4 ≅ Z/2 ⊕ Z/2,
    false. Over Z/6 = Z/2 × Z/3 the module Z/2 = (Z/6)·3 is a direct
    summand of the free module, hence projective (and flat). Common
    mistakes: "torsion-free ⇒ injective" (Z is torsion-free, not
    injective); using divisible ⇔ injective over non-PIDs. Now you try: is
    Q ⊕ Z/5 injective over Z? (no: Z/5 is not divisible)

D3. Define left derived functors of a right exact functor F. Prove
    (a) independence of the projective resolution, (b) L₀F ≅ F,
    (c) LₙF(P) = 0 for P projective and n ≥ 1, and (d) dimension shifting:
    if 0 → K → P → M → 0 with P projective then Lₙ₊₁F(M) ≅ LₙF(K), n ≥ 1.
    Answer: comparison theorem + homotopy invariance; right exactness;
    P is its own resolution; long exact sequence.
    Teacher explains: Choose ... → P₁ → P₀ → M → 0 projective and set
    LₙF(M) = Hₙ(F(P•)), deleting M. (a) If Q• is another resolution, the
    comparison theorem gives chain maps f : P• → Q•, g : Q• → P• over
    id_M, and gf, id both lift id_M, so gf ≃ id; likewise fg ≃ id. An
    additive F preserves homotopies (F(dh + hd) = F(d)F(h) + F(h)F(d)), so
    F(P•) ≃ F(Q•) and the homology agrees, canonically. (b) F right exact
    applied to P₁ → P₀ → M → 0 gives F(P₁) → F(P₀) → F(M) → 0 exact, so
    L₀F(M) = coker(F(P₁) → F(P₀)) ≅ F(M). (c) 0 → P → P → 0 is a
    resolution. (d) The horseshoe lemma gives a short exact sequence of
    resolutions and hence the LONG EXACT SEQUENCE
       ... → Lₙ₊₁F(P) → Lₙ₊₁F(M) → LₙF(K) → LₙF(P) → ... → F(M) → 0.
    For n ≥ 1 both outer terms vanish by (c), so Lₙ₊₁F(M) ≅ LₙF(K); for
    n = 0: L₁F(M) = ker(F(K) → F(P)). The same story, with injective
    resolutions, gives RⁿF for left exact F. The family (LₙF, δ) is a
    UNIVERSAL δ-functor: any natural map F → G₀ of δ-functors extends
    uniquely, because LₙF vanishes on projectives ("effaceable").
    Common mistakes: resolving with non-projective modules; forgetting to
    delete M before applying F; applying F to M itself in degree 0.
    Now you try: for F = − ⊗ Z/2 and 0 → 2Z → Z → Z/2 → 0 compute
    L₁F(Z/2). (ker(2Z ⊗ Z/2 → Z/2) = Z/2, the map being 0)

D4. (a) Compute Torₙ^Z(Z/m, A) for all n and all abelian groups A.
    (b) Deduce Tor₁(Z/m, Z/n) ≅ Z/gcd(m, n), and compute Tor₁(Z/12, Z/18),
    Tor₁(Q/Z, A) and Tor₁(Q, A). (c) Verify balance: compute Tor₁(Z/4, Z/6)
    by resolving the second variable.
    Answer: (a) Tor₀ = A/mA, Tor₁ = A[m], Torₙ = 0 (n ≥ 2). (b) Z/6;
    torsion subgroup of A; 0. (c) Z/2 both ways.
    Teacher explains: (a) Resolve Z/m: 0 → Z →·m Z → Z/m → 0 (length 1,
    so Torₙ = 0 for n ≥ 2). Delete Z/m, tensor with A: 0 → A →·m A → 0 in
    degrees 1, 0. Hence Tor₀ = A/mA = Z/m ⊗ A and Tor₁ = ker(·m) = A[m].
    (b) A = Z/n: the solutions of mx ≡ 0 (mod n) form the subgroup
    generated by n/g, g = gcd(m, n), of order g, so Tor₁ ≅ Z/g; for
    (12, 18) that is Z/6. Tor commutes with direct limits and Q/Z = lim
    Z/m (divisibility order), so Tor₁(Q/Z, A) = lim A[m] = ⋃A[m] = A_tors.
    Q is flat (a localisation of Z) so Tor₁(Q, A) = 0. Alternatively
    apply − ⊗ A to 0 → Z → Q → Q/Z → 0: 0 → Tor₁(Q/Z, A) → A → Q ⊗ A,
    and ker(A → Q ⊗ A) is exactly the torsion. (c) Resolve Z/6 instead:
    0 → Z →·6 Z → Z/6 → 0; tensor with Z/4: Z/4 →·6 Z/4, kernel {0, 2} ≅
    Z/2; resolving Z/4 instead gives Z/6[4] = {0, 3} ≅ Z/2: they agree.
    Balance holds in general because both computations equal the
    homology of the double complex P• ⊗ Q•. Common mistakes: writing
    Tor₁(Z/m, Z/n) = Z/lcm; thinking Tor₁(Q/Z, Q/Z) = 0 (it is Q/Z).
    Now you try: Tor₁(Z/4 ⊕ Z/9, Z/6). (Z/2 ⊕ Z/3 ≅ Z/6)

D5. (a) Show Tor₁ᴿ(R/I, M) ≅ ker(I ⊗ M → M) and Tor₁ᴿ(R/I, R/J) ≅
    (I ∩ J)/IJ. (b) Prove: over a PID, M is flat iff torsion-free.
    (c) Compute Tor₁ for (R, I, J) = (Z, (4), (6)), (k[x, y], (x), (y)),
    (k[x, y], (x, y), (x, y)); deduce that 𝔪 = (x, y) is not flat over
    k[x, y] although it is torsion-free.
    Answer: (c) Z/2; 0; 𝔪/𝔪² ≅ k² ≠ 0, i.e. Tor₂(k, k) = Tor₁(𝔪, k) ≠ 0.
    Teacher explains: (a) Tensor 0 → I → R → R/I → 0 with M: since R is
    flat, Tor₁(R, M) = 0 and the long exact sequence starts
       0 → Tor₁(R/I, M) → I ⊗ M → M → M/IM → 0.
    Take M = R/J: I ⊗ R/J = I/IJ and the map I/IJ → R/J has kernel
    (I ∩ J)/IJ. (b) If M is flat, the injection R →·a R (a ≠ 0) stays
    injective after ⊗M, so a·m = 0 ⇒ m = 0. Conversely a torsion-free
    module is a direct limit of f.g. torsion-free = free modules, Tor
    commutes with direct limits, so Tor₁(−, M) = 0. (Or: ideals are (a),
    and (a) ⊗ M ≅ M →·a M is injective.) (c) Z: (4) ∩ (6) = (12), IJ =
    (24): (12)/(24) ≅ Z/2 ✓ = Z/gcd. (x) ∩ (y) = (xy) = IJ: Tor₁ = 0 —
    "transversal" intersections have no higher Tor (Serre's intersection
    multiplicity starts from this). I = J = 𝔪: 𝔪/𝔪² has basis x̄, ȳ, so
    Tor₁(k, k) = k². For 𝔪: from 0 → 𝔪 → R → k → 0, Tor₁(𝔪, k) ≅
    Tor₂(k, k) = k (Koszul, D9) ≠ 0, so 𝔪 is not flat. Over a domain
    flat ⇒ torsion-free, but the converse needs a Prüfer/PID ring.
    Common mistakes: writing Tor₁(R/I, R/J) = I ∩ J; confusing
    torsion-free with flat over non-PIDs. Now you try: Tor₁^Z(Z/8, Z/12)
    via the formula. ((24)/(96) ≅ Z/4)

D6. (a) Compute Extⁿ_Z(Z/m, A) for all n and A. (b) Find Ext¹(Z/12,
    Z/18), Ext¹(Z/n, Z) — the latter both by a projective resolution of
    Z/n and by an injective resolution of Z. (c) Compute Ext¹(Q/Z, Z) and
    show Ext¹(Q, Z) ≠ 0 although Hom(Q, Z) = 0.
    Answer: (a) Ext⁰ = A[m], Ext¹ = A/mA, Extⁿ = 0 (n ≥ 2). (b) Z/6; Z/n.
    (c) Ẑ = lim Z/n; Ext¹(Q, Z) ≅ Ẑ/Z, uncountable.
    Teacher explains: (a) Hom(−, A) on 0 → Z →·m Z gives 0 → A →·m A → 0
    in degrees 0, 1: Ext⁰ = ker = A[m] = Hom(Z/m, A), Ext¹ = coker = A/mA.
    (b) (Z/18)/12(Z/18): 12 generates the subgroup of order 18/6 = 3, so
    the quotient has order 6: Z/6. Ext¹(Z/n, Z) = Z/nZ. Via 0 → Z → Q →
    Q/Z → 0 (injective resolution of Z): Hom(Z/n, Q) = 0 → Hom(Z/n, Q/Z)
    ≅ Z/n → 0, and Ext¹ is the H¹ = Z/n ✓ (BALANCE of Ext). (c) Apply
    Hom(Q/Z, −) to 0 → Z → Q → Q/Z → 0: Hom(Q/Z, Q) = 0 and Ext¹(Q/Z, Q)
    = 0 (Q injective), so Ext¹(Q/Z, Z) ≅ Hom(Q/Z, Q/Z) = End(Q/Z) =
    lim Z/n = Ẑ ≅ ∏_p Z_p. Now apply Hom(−, Z) to the same sequence:
       0 → Hom(Q, Z) = 0 → Hom(Z, Z) = Z → Ext¹(Q/Z, Z) → Ext¹(Q, Z)
         → Ext¹(Z, Z) = 0,
    so Ext¹(Q, Z) ≅ Ẑ/Z, uncountable (Ẑ is). Moral: Ext¹(Q, Z) ≠ 0 means
    there is a non-split extension 0 → Z → E → Q → 0 — e.g. a suitable
    subgroup of Q ⊕ Ẑ. Common mistakes: Ext¹(Z/n, Z) = 0 "because Z has no
    torsion"; confusing A[m] (Ext⁰) with A/mA (Ext¹). Now you try:
    Ext¹(Z/6, Z ⊕ Z/4). (Z/6 ⊕ Z/2)

D7. (a) Describe the bijection Ext¹(M, N) ↔ {extensions 0 → N → E → M
    → 0}/≅ via a projective presentation. (b) Classify extensions of Z/2
    by Z/4 and of Z/p by Z/p. (c) Explain the Baer sum on the latter.
    Answer: (b) Ext¹(Z/2, Z/4) = Z/2: E = Z/4 ⊕ Z/2 or Z/8. Ext¹(Z/p, Z/p)
    = Z/p: p classes, one split, p − 1 with E ≅ Z/p². (c) addition in Z/p.
    Teacher explains: (a) Take 0 → K →ι P → M → 0, P projective. The long
    exact sequence gives Hom(P, N) →ι* Hom(K, N) → Ext¹(M, N) → 0. Given
    φ : K → N, form the PUSHOUT E = (N ⊕ P)/{(φ(k), −ι(k))}; then 0 → N →
    E → M → 0 is exact. Changing φ by ψ∘ι (ψ : P → N) gives an equivalent
    extension, and φ extends to P iff E splits. Conversely an extension
    gives φ by lifting P → M to P → E. (b) M = Z/2: K = 2Z ≅ Z, P = Z,
    φ is determined by a = φ(2) ∈ Z/4, and ι* has image {2c} = 2Z/4. So
    Ext¹ = (Z/4)/(2) ≅ Z/2. a = 0: split, E = Z/4 ⊕ Z/2. a = 1: E =
    (Z/4 ⊕ Z)/⟨(1, −2)⟩; e = (0, 1) satisfies 2e = (1, 0), which has order
    4, so e has order 8 and E ≅ Z/8 (0 → Z/4 →·2 Z/8 → Z/2 → 0). For Z/p
    by Z/p: a ∈ Z/p, ι* has image p·(Z/p) = 0, so Ext¹ ≅ Z/p. For a ≠ 0,
    e = (0, 1) has pe = (a, 0) ≠ 0 so E ≅ Z/p². The p − 1 non-split
    classes have ISOMORPHIC middle terms but are inequivalent extensions:
    equivalence demands commuting with the given maps. (c) BAER SUM of
    E₁, E₂: take the pullback over the diagonal M → M ⊕ M of E₁ ⊕ E₂, then
    push out along the sum N ⊕ N → N. In the φ-description it is φ₁ + φ₂,
    i.e. a₁ + a₂ mod p; for p = 2 the sum of Z/4 with itself is split.
    Common mistakes: counting isomorphism types of E instead of classes;
    believing E ≅ N ⊕ M forces the split class (true for f.g. modules by
    Miyata's theorem, false in general). Now you try: how many classes
    and which middle groups for extensions of Z/3 by Z/2? (Ext¹ = 0: only
    Z/6)

D8. Let R = Z/4 and S = k[x]/(x²). (a) Write minimal free resolutions of
    Z/2 over R and k over S. (b) Compute Torₙᴿ(Z/2, Z/2), Extⁿᴿ(Z/2, Z/2),
    Extⁿᴿ(Z/2, R), and the analogues over S. (c) Deduce the global
    dimensions and that R, S are self-injective.
    Answer: (a) ... → R →·2 R →·2 R → Z/2 → 0; ... → S →x S →x S → k → 0.
    (b) Z/2 (resp. k) for every n ≥ 0; Ext⁰(Z/2, R) = Z/2, Extⁿ = 0 for
    n ≥ 1 (same over S). (c) gl.dim = ∞; Extⁿ(−, R) = 0 for n ≥ 1.
    Teacher explains: (a) R → Z/2 has kernel 2R ≅ Z/2, which is again the
    image of R →·2 R, whose kernel is again 2R: the resolution is
    PERIODIC of period 1 and never stops. (b) Tensoring with Z/2 turns ·2
    into 0: every term Z/2, every map 0, so Torₙ = Z/2 for all n. Hom(−,
    Z/2) likewise gives Z/2 →0 Z/2 →0 ..., Extⁿ = Z/2. Hom(−, R) gives R
    →·2 R →·2 R ...: Ext⁰ = ker = 2R ≅ Z/2 = Hom(Z/2, R) ✓, and Extⁿ =
    ker/im = 2R/2R = 0. Over S replace 2 by x: identical results with k.
    (c) pd Z/2 = ∞ since Torₙ ≠ 0 for all n, so gl.dim R = ∞, although R
    is Artinian and "small". Since Extⁿ(Z/2, R) = 0 for n ≥ 1 and Z/2 is
    the only simple module, Baer/induction on length gives Ext¹(−, R) = 0:
    R is SELF-INJECTIVE (Frobenius). Contrast Z/6 ≅ Z/2 × Z/3: semisimple,
    gl.dim 0. Common mistakes: stopping the
    resolution after one step as over Z; thinking finite rings have finite
    global dimension. Now you try: Torₙ^Z/9(Z/3, Z/9) for n ≥ 1. (0: Z/9
    is free over itself)

D9. Let R = k[x, y], 𝔪 = (x, y). (a) Show that the Koszul complex
    0 → R →(−y, x)ᵀ R² →(x y) R → k → 0 is exact. (b) Compute Torᵢᴿ(k, k),
    Extⁱᴿ(k, k) and Extⁱᴿ(k, R). (c) State Hilbert's syzygy theorem and
    find gl.dim R, pd k, pd 𝔪, pd R/(x), pd R/(x², y³).
    Answer: (b) k, k², k; k, k², k; 0, 0, k. (c) gl.dim k[x₁..xₙ] = n;
    here 2; pd k = 2, pd 𝔪 = 1, pd R/(x) = 1, pd R/(x², y³) = 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) (x y)∘(−y, x)ᵀ = −xy + yx = 0. The right map is
    onto 𝔪 with cokernel k. If xf + yg = 0 then x | yg, so x | g (UFD):
    g = xh, and then f = −yh, i.e. (f, g) = h(−y, x): exact in the middle.
    h ↦ (−yh, xh) is injective (domain). (b) Tensor with k = R/𝔪: every
    matrix entry lies in 𝔪, so all differentials become 0 and Torᵢ is the
    i-th term: k, k², k (ranks C(2, i)). Hom(−, k) likewise: k, k², k.
    Hom(−, R) gives R →(x, y)ᵀ R² →(−y x) R, which is again a Koszul
    complex ("self-duality"): exact except at the end, H² = R/(x, y) = k,
    H⁰ = H¹ = 0. This is the GORENSTEIN property (depth 2 = dim 2).
    (c) SYZYGY THEOREM: every f.g. module over k[x₁, ..., xₙ] has a free
    resolution of length ≤ n; gl.dim = n, attained by k because Torₙ(k, k)
    = k ≠ 0 (Koszul in n variables has ranks C(n, i)). pd 𝔪 = pd k − 1 = 1
    by dimension shifting (0 → 𝔪 → R → k → 0). R/(x): 0 → R →x R, pd 1.
    x², y³ is a REGULAR SEQUENCE, so its Koszul complex 0 → R → R² → R →
    R/(x², y³) → 0 is exact: pd 2, with Tor(R/(x², y³), k) = k, k², k.
    Common mistakes: forgetting the sign in (−y, x); thinking the
    Koszul complex is exact for any sequence (x, x fails: H₁ ≠ 0).
    Now you try: Torᵢ(k, k) over k[x, y, z]. (k, k³, k³, k)

D10. (a) Derive the universal coefficient theorem for cohomology from
     a free chain complex C over Z. (b) Compute H*(RP²; Z), H*(RP²; Z/2)
     and H*(K; Z), H_*(K; Z/2) for the Klein bottle K. (c) Use Künneth to
     compute H_*(RP² × RP²; Z) and check with Z/2-coefficients.
     Answer: (b) Z, 0, Z/2; Z/2, Z/2, Z/2; Z, Z, Z/2; Z/2, (Z/2)², Z/2.
     (c) Z, (Z/2)², Z/2, Z/2, 0.
     Teacher explains: (a) Let Zₙ, Bₙ be cycles, boundaries. Bₙ₋₁ ⊆ Cₙ₋₁
     is free (subgroup of free over a PID), so 0 → Zₙ → Cₙ → Bₙ₋₁ → 0
     splits. Applying Hom(−, G) and the long exact sequence of the
     resulting short exact sequence of cochain complexes, one finds
        0 → Ext¹(Hₙ₋₁, G) → Hⁿ(C; G) → Hom(Hₙ, G) → 0,
     split (not naturally), using the free resolution 0 → Bₙ₋₁ → Zₙ₋₁ →
     Hₙ₋₁ → 0. Rule of thumb for G = Z: FREE parts stay in degree n,
     TORSION moves up one degree. (b) RP²: H₀ = Z, H₁ = Z/2, H₂ = 0. H⁰ =
     Z; H¹ = Hom(Z/2, Z) ⊕ Ext(Z, Z) = 0; H² = Hom(0, Z) ⊕ Ext(Z/2, Z) =
     Z/2. With Z/2: H⁰ = Z/2, H¹ = Hom(Z/2, Z/2) = Z/2, H² = Ext(Z/2, Z/2)
     = Z/2. Klein bottle: H₀ = Z, H₁ = Z ⊕ Z/2, H₂ = 0, so H⁰ = Z, H¹ =
     Hom(Z ⊕ Z/2, Z) = Z, H² = Ext(Z ⊕ Z/2, Z) = Z/2; homology UCT Hₙ(K;
     Z/2) = Hₙ ⊗ Z/2 ⊕ Tor(Hₙ₋₁, Z/2) gives Z/2, (Z/2)², Tor(Z/2, Z/2) =
     Z/2. (c) KÜNNETH: Hₙ(X × Y) = ⊕_{i+j=n} Hᵢ ⊗ Hⱼ ⊕ ⊕_{i+j=n−1}
     Tor(Hᵢ, Hⱼ). With H(RP²) = (Z, Z/2, 0): H₀ = Z; H₁ = Z/2 ⊕ Z/2;
     H₂ = H₁ ⊗ H₁ = Z/2; H₃ = Tor(H₁, H₁) = Z/2; H₄ = 0. Check: with Z/2
     coefficients (a field) the ranks must be (1, 1, 1) * (1, 1, 1) =
     (1, 2, 3, 2, 1); UCT from our answer gives 1, 2, 1 + 2, 1 + 1, 0 + 1 ✓.
     Euler characteristic 1 = χ(RP²)² ✓. Common mistakes: putting
     Ext(Hₙ, G) instead of Ext(Hₙ₋₁, G); forgetting the Tor term in
     Künneth (it produces H₃ here!). Now you try: H*(RP³; Z) given H₀ =
     Z, H₁ = Z/2, H₂ = 0, H₃ = Z. (Z, 0, Z/2, Z)

D11. (a) Show Hⁿ(G, M) := Extⁿ_ZG(Z, M) can be computed for G = ⟨t⟩ ≅
     Z/m from the periodic resolution ... → ZG →N ZG →(t−1) ZG → Z → 0,
     N = 1 + t + ... + t^(m−1). (b) Compute Hⁿ(Z/m, Z), Hₙ(Z/m, Z) and
     Hⁿ(Z/2, Z₋) (sign action). (c) Interpret H²(Z/2, Z) = Z/2 via group
     extensions.
     Answer: (b) Z, 0, Z/m, 0, Z/m, ...; Z, Z/m, 0, Z/m, 0, ...; 0, Z/2,
     0, Z/2, ... (c) the central extensions Z × Z/2 and Z (0 → Z →2 Z).
     Teacher explains: (a) Exactness: ker ε = augmentation ideal =
     im(t − 1); ker(t − 1) = fixed elements of ZG = Z·N = im N; ker N =
     {∑aᵢtⁱ : ∑aᵢ = 0} = im(t − 1). Applying Hom_ZG(−, M) (Hom(ZG, M) = M):
        M →(t−1) M →N M →(t−1) M →N ... ,
     so H⁰ = M^G, H^odd = ker N/(t − 1)M, H^even>0 = M^G/NM ("Tate
     periodicity"). (b) M = Z trivial: t − 1 = 0, N = m. H⁰ = Z, H^odd =
     ker(·m) = 0, H^even = Z/m. Homology uses ⊗: Z ← Z ←0 Z ←m Z ←0 ...,
     H₀ = Z, H₁ = Z/m (= G^ab ✓), H₂ = ker(m) = 0, H₃ = Z/m, ... Sign
     action on Z for m = 2: t − 1 = −2, N = 1 + t = 0: H⁰ = M^G = 0, H^odd
     = Z/2Z, H^even = 0/0 = 0. (c) H²(G, A) classifies extensions 0 → A →
     E → G → 0 inducing the given action. For A = Z trivial and G = Z/2
     the classes are E = Z × Z/2 (split) and E = Z with A = 2Z (non-split:
     twice a generator of E generates A). Also
     H¹(G, M) = crossed homomorphisms / principal ones; = Hom(G, M) for
     trivial action. Common mistakes: using the trivial-action formulas
     for a non-trivial module; mixing up homology and cohomology degrees.
     Now you try: Hⁿ(Z/3, Z/2) with trivial action. (Z/2 for n = 0, 0 for
     n ≥ 1: N = 3 acts invertibly)

D12. (a) Define K(A), D(A) and the mapping cone; show that f : X → Y is
     a quasi-isomorphism iff Cone(f) is acyclic. (b) Show that
     P = [Z →2 Z] (degrees −1, 0) is isomorphic to Z/2 in D(Z) but not in
     K(Z). (c) Prove Hom_D(Z)(Z/2, Z[1]) ≅ Z/2 although every chain map
     Z/2 → Z[1] is 0; identify the triangle Z →2 Z → Z/2 → Z[1].
     Answer: (b) P → Z/2 is a quasi-iso; no homotopy inverse exists since
     Hom(Z/2, Z) = 0. (c) Hom_D(Z/2, Z[1]) = Hom_K(P, Z[1]) = Ext¹(Z/2, Z).
     Teacher explains: (a) K(A): complexes with chain maps modulo homotopy.
     D(A): formally invert the quasi-isomorphisms; morphisms are roofs
     X ← X′ → Y with X′ → X a quasi-iso. Cone(f)ⁿ = Xⁿ⁺¹ ⊕ Yⁿ, d(x, y) =
     (−dx, f(x) + dy). There is a short exact sequence 0 → Y → Cone(f) →
     X[1] → 0 whose connecting map is Hⁿ(f), so the long exact sequence
     ... → Hⁿ(X) →f Hⁿ(Y) → Hⁿ(Cone f) → Hⁿ⁺¹(X) →f ... shows H(Cone f)
     = 0 ⇔ all Hⁿ(f) are isomorphisms. (b) The map P → Z/2 (reduction in
     degree 0) induces H⁻¹: 0 → 0 and H⁰: Z/2 → Z/2 iso: a quasi-iso,
     hence invertible in D. A homotopy inverse would contain a chain map
     g : Z/2 → P, i.e. Z/2 → Z in degree 0, which is 0; then the
     composite Z/2 → P → Z/2 is 0 ≠ id on H⁰. (c) Chain maps between
     Z/2 (degree 0) and Z[1] (degree −1) vanish for degree reasons. But
     in D replace Z/2 by P: Hom_K(P, Z[1]) = maps Z (deg −1) → Z with
     nothing to check, i.e. Z, modulo homotopies h : Z (deg 0) → Z (deg
     −1 of Z[1]), which change the map by ·2: Z/2Z = Ext¹(Z/2, Z). This is
     the general principle Hom_D(M, N[n]) = Extⁿ(M, N), with composition
     = Yoneda product. The nonzero element is the connecting morphism of
     the DISTINGUISHED TRIANGLE Z →2 Z → Z/2 → Z[1], which encodes the
     non-split extension 0 → Z → Z → Z/2 → 0. D(A) is additive and
     triangulated, but not abelian: in a triangulated category every
     monomorphism splits, so an abelian one is semisimple; D(Z) is not.
     Common mistakes: thinking D(A) morphisms are just chain maps;
     believing quasi-isomorphic complexes are homotopy equivalent (true
     for bounded-above complexes of PROJECTIVES only). Now you try:
     compute H*(Cone(Z/4 → Z/2)). (Z/2 in degree −1 only: cone ≅ Z/2[1])

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Complexes, homology and exactness. Compute the requested groups.
    a) H₁, H₀ of 0 → Z →·2 Z → 0 (degrees 1, 0)
    b) H₁, H₀ of 0 → Z →·0 Z → 0
    c) middle homology of Z/4 →·2 Z/4 →·2 Z/4
    d) middle homology of Z/8 →·2 Z/8 →·4 Z/8
    e) middle homology of Z/8 →·4 Z/8 →·4 Z/8
    f) coker of Z² → Z² given by the matrix [[2, 0], [0, 3]]
    g) coker of [[2, 4], [6, 8]]         h) coker of [[6, 4], [4, 6]]
    i) coker and kernel of Z³ → Z², [[2, 4, 6], [0, 6, 12]]
    j) coker and kernel of Z³ → Z², [[3, 6, 9], [6, 12, 18]]
    k) all homology of 0 → Z →(1, 1)ᵀ Z² →(1 −1) Z → 0
    l) snake lemma for 0 → Z →·3 Z → Z/3 → 0 mapped to itself by ·4: the
       six groups
    m) dim V if 0 → k² → k⁵ → V → k⁴ → k³ → 0 is exact
    n) all A with 0 → Z/2 → A → Z/3 → 0 exact
    o) all A with 0 → Z/3 → A → Z/3 → 0 exact
    p) 0 → C′ → C → C″ → 0 exact, C′ and C″ acyclic: is C acyclic?
    q) f ≃ g (homotopic) and f a quasi-iso: is g a quasi-iso?
    r) is the zero map Z/2[0] → Z/2[0] homotopic to the identity?
    Answers:
      a) 0, Z/2     b) Z, Z      c) 0 (ker = im = {0, 2})     d) 0
      e) Z/2 (ker 4 = {0,2,4,6}, im 4 = {0,4})   f) Z/6 (Smith 1, 6)
      g) Z/2 ⊕ Z/4 (Smith 2, 4)   h) Z/2 ⊕ Z/10 (Smith 2, 10)
      i) Z/2 ⊕ Z/6; kernel ≅ Z     j) Z ⊕ Z/3; kernel ≅ Z²
      k) all 0 (exact)
      l) kernels 0, 0, 0; cokernels Z/4, Z/4, 0 (·4 is bijective on Z/3)
      m) 4 (alternating sum of dimensions is 0)   n) Z/6 only
      o) Z/9 or Z/3 ⊕ Z/3          p) yes (long exact sequence)
      q) yes (same map on homology)  r) no (homotopic maps agree on H₀)

M2. Projective (P), injective (I), flat (F): list which hold (ring Z
    unless stated).
    a) Z               b) Q               c) Q/Z            d) Z/6
    e) Z[1/2]          f) Z_(p) (localisation)             g) Z/p^∞
    h) ∏_{n∈N} Z (Baer-Specker)          i) Q ⊕ Q/Z        j) ⊕_N Z
    k) Z/4 over Z/4    l) Z/2 over Z/4    m) Z/2 over Z/6   n) Z/3 over Z/6
    o) k(x) over k[x]  p) 𝔪 = (x, y) over k[x, y]          q) any module
       over a field k   r) k[x]/(x²) over itself
    s) Hom_Z(Z/n, Q/Z) (as a Z-module)   t) Mₙ(k)-module kⁿ (column vectors)
    Answers:
      a) P, F      b) F, I       c) I       d) none      e) F
      f) F         g) I          h) F (torsion-free, not free: Specker)
      i) I         j) P, F       k) P, I, F  l) none      m) P, I, F
      n) P, I, F   o) F, I (divisible over a PID)  p) none (pd 𝔪 = 1)
      q) P, I, F   r) P, I, F (self-injective)
      s) none (≅ Z/n)            t) P, I, F (Mₙ(k) semisimple)

      l) isomorphism                m) 0 (Q/Z injective)
      n) 0 (M flat)                 o) 0 (Q flat)
      p) Ext¹(Q, Z) ≅ Ẑ/Z ≠ 0       q) Tor₂(k, k) = k

M5. Hom and Ext over Z.

M6. Extensions. Give |Ext¹(M, N)| and the possible middle terms E of
    0 → N → E → M → 0 (up to isomorphism of groups).
    a) M = Z/2, N = Z/4        b) M = Z/4, N = Z/2
    c) M = Z/3, N = Z/2        d) M = Z/p, N = Z/p (number of classes
       giving E ≅ Z/p²)        e) M = Z/2, N = Z
    f) M = Z, N arbitrary      g) M = Z/2 ⊕ Z/2, N = Z/2 (number of
       classes for each E)     h) M = Z/4, N = Z/4
    i) M = Z/2, N = Z/2 ⊕ Z/2   j) M = k, N = k over k[x] (the
       non-split E)            k) Baer sum in Ext¹(Z/5, Z/5) of the
       classes a = 3 and a = 4 (notation of D7)
    l) Baer sum of the non-split class of Ext¹(Z/2, Z/4) with itself
    m) M = Q, N = Z: does a non-split extension exist?
    Answers:
      a) 2: Z/4 ⊕ Z/2, Z/8          b) 2: Z/2 ⊕ Z/4, Z/8
      c) 1: Z/6                     d) p classes; p − 1 give Z/p²
      e) 2: Z ⊕ Z/2, Z (0 → Z →2 Z → Z/2 → 0)      f) 1: N ⊕ Z only
      g) 4: (Z/2)³ for 1 class, Z/4 ⊕ Z/2 for 3 classes
      h) 4: Z/4 ⊕ Z/4 (a = 0), Z/16 (a = 1, 3), Z/8 ⊕ Z/2 (a = 2)
      i) 4: (Z/2)³ for 1 class, Z/4 ⊕ Z/2 for 3 classes
      j) Ext¹ = k; E = k[x]/(x²)    k) a = 2 (3 + 4 = 7 ≡ 2)
      l) the split class            m) yes, Ext¹(Q, Z) ≠ 0

M7. Resolutions over quotient rings. Compute (all n ≥ 0 unless stated).
    a) Torₙ^Z/4(Z/2, Z/2)            b) Extⁿ_Z/4(Z/2, Z/2)

M8. Koszul complexes, syzygies, dimensions (k a field, R = k[x, y]

M11. Cones, triangles and the derived category (cohomological indexing;
     a module M means M[0]; Cone(f) of f : X → Y in degree 0 has X in
     degree −1 and Y in degree 0).
    a) H*(Cone(Z →·3 Z))                 b) H*(Cone(Z →0 Z/2))

       Hom_D(k, R[2])

    o) Q ⊗ᴸ Z/5                          p) H*(k ⊗ᴸ_S k), S = k[x]/(x²)
    q) H*(RHom_Z(Q, Z))
    r) X = [Z →·2 Z →0 Z/3] in degrees −1, 0, 1: X ≅ ? in D(Z)
    s) is [Z →·2 Z] ≅ Z/2 in K(Z)? in D(Z)?
    t) Hom_K(Z)(Z/2, [Z →·2 Z])
    u) complete the triangle Z/2 →·2 Z/4 → ? → Z/2[1]
    v) Yoneda square of the non-zero class of Ext¹_S(k, k), S = k[x]/(x²):
       zero or non-zero in Ext²?

      e) 0 (acyclic)               f) Ext¹(Z/2, Z) = Z/2
      g) Z/2       h) 0 (Ext² over Z vanishes)   i) 0 (negative Ext)
      j) Ext¹(Q, Z) ≅ Ẑ/Z          k) Ext⁷_Z/4(Z/2, Z/2) = Z/2
      l) k², k, 0, k               m) H⁰ = Z/2, H⁻¹ = Tor₁ = Z/2
      n) H⁰ = Z/2, H¹ = Z/2        o) 0 (Q flat, Q ⊗ Z/5 = 0)
      p) H⁻ⁿ = k for every n ≥ 0 (unbounded)    q) H⁰ = 0, H¹ = Ẑ/Z
      r) Z/2 ⊕ Z/3[−1] (hereditary ring: X ≅ ⊕ Hⁿ(X)[−n])
      s) no; yes                   t) 0 (Hom(Z/2, Z) = 0 in degree 0)
      u) Z/2 (the quotient Z/4/(2)), connecting map = the class of Z/4
      v) non-zero (Ext*_S(k, k) ≅ k[t], t in degree 1)

M12. True or false? Give a proof sketch or a counterexample.
    a) Every flat Z-module is projective.
    b) Every projective module is flat.
    c) Every injective Z-module is flat.
    d) A ⊗ − is left exact.
    e) Hom(M, −) is exact iff M is projective.
    f) Submodules of free Z-modules are free.
    g) Submodules of free modules are projective over every ring.
    h) Over every integral domain, divisible ⇒ injective.
    i) Extⁿ_Z(A, B) = 0 for all n ≥ 2.
    j) Hom(A, B) = 0 ⇒ Ext¹(A, B) = 0.
    k) Tor₁(A, B) ≅ Tor₁(B, A).
    l) If 0 → A → B → C → 0 is exact and B ≅ A ⊕ C, it splits.
    m) The global dimension of a finite ring is finite.
    n) Quasi-isomorphic complexes are homotopy equivalent.
    o) D(A) is an abelian category.
    p) In D(Z) every complex X is isomorphic to ⊕ Hⁿ(X)[−n].
    q) In D(Z/4) every complex X is isomorphic to ⊕ Hⁿ(X)[−n].
    r) Hom_D(M, N[n]) ≅ Extⁿ(M, N) for modules M, N and n ≥ 0.
    s) If Tor₁(M, Z/p) = 0 for every prime p, then M is flat over Z.
    t) Every f.g. module over k[x, y] has a free resolution of length ≤ 2.
    u) Tor₁(Z/m, Z/n) ≅ Z/lcm(m, n).
    v) H¹(G, M) ≅ Hom(G, M) for every G-module M.
    w) Tor^R commutes with direct limits (filtered colimits).
    Answers:
      a) F: Q            b) T: summand of free, and free is flat
      c) F: Q/Z (torsion)                  d) F: Z →·2 Z, ⊗ Z/2 gives 0
      e) T               f) T (PID)
      g) F: 2Z/4 ≅ Z/2 ⊆ Z/4 is not projective over Z/4
      h) F: K/R for R = k[x, y] (true exactly for Dedekind domains)
      i) T (gl.dim Z = 1)                  j) F: Hom(Z/2, Z) = 0,
         Ext¹(Z/2, Z) = Z/2
      k) T (balance + symmetry of ⊗)
      l) F: 0 → Z → Z ⊕ (Z/2)^(∞) → (Z/2)^(∞) → 0, n ↦ (2n, 0) (T for f.g.
         modules, Miyata)
      m) F: Z/4           n) F: [Z →·2 Z] and Z/2
      o) F: triangulated + abelian forces semisimple; D(Z) is not
      p) T (hereditary)
      q) F: [Z/4 →·2 Z/4] has H⁻¹ = H⁰ = Z/2 but represents the non-zero
         class in Ext²_Z/4(Z/2, Z/2)
      r) T                s) T (M[p] = 0 for all p ⇒ torsion-free)
      t) T (Hilbert syzygy theorem)        u) F: gcd, e.g. m = 2, n = 4
      v) F: G = Z/2 on Z₋, Hom(G, Z) = 0 but H¹ = Z/2
      w) T

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: drill the two "master computations" until automatic — for
  M = Z/m resolve by 0 → Z →m Z → Z/m → 0 and read off Tor₁ = A[m], Ext¹ =
  A/mA — and insist every answer comes from an explicit resolution, not
  from memory ("resolve, delete, apply F, take homology"). Always write
  the long exact sequence with all six or nine terms and label every map
  before chasing; check answers by balance (resolve the other variable)
  and by Euler characteristics. For extensions, draw the pushout and
  compute the order of a lifted generator. For derived categories start
  from the single example [Z →2 Z] ≅ Z/2 and the triangle Z → Z → Z/2 →
  Z[1] before any abstraction. Harder: prove the horseshoe lemma and the
  comparison theorem in full; prove Baer's criterion via Zorn; show that
  Ext¹ classes form a group under the Baer sum and that Yoneda Ext agrees
  with derived-functor Ext; prove Tor commutes with filtered colimits and
  deduce flat = torsion-free over a PID; compute Ext*_k[x]/(x²)(k, k) as
  a ring; build the Grothendieck spectral sequence and derive the Lyndon-
  Hochschild-Serre and base-change spectral sequences; verify the
  octahedral axiom for cones in K(A); prove Hom_D(M, N[n]) = Extⁿ(M, N)
  via K-projective resolutions; and show that every triangulated category
  that is abelian is semisimple.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.7 — SHEAF THEORY: PRESHEAVES AND SHEAVES, STALKS, SHEAFIFICATION,
MORPHISMS, DIRECT AND INVERSE IMAGES, EXACTNESS, FLASQUE/SOFT/FINE SHEAVES,
SHEAF COHOMOLOGY (DERIVED FUNCTOR AND ČECH), LOCALLY CONSTANT SHEAVES
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PRESHEAF of abelian groups on a space X: a group ℱ(U) for each open U
    and restriction maps ρ_UV : ℱ(U) → ℱ(V) (written s ↦ s|_V) for V ⊆ U,
    with ρ_UU = id and ρ_VW ∘ ρ_UV = ρ_UW; i.e. a functor Open(X)ᵒᵖ → Ab.
    Elements of ℱ(U) are SECTIONS; Γ(U, ℱ) = ℱ(U), Γ(X, ℱ) = global
    sections. A MORPHISM φ : ℱ → 𝒢 is a family φ_U commuting with the
    restrictions (a natural transformation).
  - SHEAF: a presheaf with (LOCALITY) if U = ⋃Uᵢ and s|_Uᵢ = 0 for all i
    then s = 0, and (GLUING) if sᵢ ∈ ℱ(Uᵢ) agree on overlaps Uᵢ ∩ Uⱼ then
    some s ∈ ℱ(U) has s|_Uᵢ = sᵢ. Equivalently ℱ(U) → ∏ℱ(Uᵢ) ⇉
    ∏ℱ(Uᵢ ∩ Uⱼ) is an equalizer; in particular ℱ(∅) = 0. Sheaves:
    continuous, C^∞, holomorphic (𝒪), harmonic functions, sections of a
    bundle, locally constant functions (the CONSTANT SHEAF A_X, with
    A_X(U) = A^{π₀(U)} for U locally connected). Non-sheaves: bounded
    functions, constant functions, polynomials (gluing fails), the image
    presheaf of exp : 𝒪 → 𝒪*.
  - STALK ℱₓ = colim_{U ∋ x} ℱ(U): GERMS [U, s], two sections identified
    if they agree on a smaller neighbourhood. 𝒪_{C,0} = C{z} (convergent
    power series). For SHEAVES: a section is zero iff all its germs are
    zero, and φ is injective / surjective / an isomorphism iff every φₓ
    is. (False for presheaves: P(X) = Z, P(U) = 0 for U ≠ X has all
    stalks 0 but P ≠ 0.)
  - SHEAFIFICATION ℱ⁺: ℱ⁺(U) = functions s : U → ⊔ℱₓ, s(x) ∈ ℱₓ, locally
    given by the germs of one section of ℱ. Universal: every morphism
    from ℱ to a sheaf factors uniquely through ℱ → ℱ⁺, and (ℱ⁺)ₓ = ℱₓ.
    Constant presheaf ↦ locally constant functions; bounded continuous ↦
    all continuous; exp-image ↦ 𝒪*; U ↦ H^q(U; Z) (q ≥ 1, X a manifold)
    ↦ 0.
  - MORPHISMS AND EXACTNESS: the kernel presheaf of a morphism of sheaves
    IS a sheaf; image and cokernel must be sheafified. Sh(X) is an
    ABELIAN category and 0 → ℱ′ → ℱ → ℱ″ → 0 is EXACT iff it is exact on
    every stalk. Γ(X, −) is LEFT exact, not right exact: the EXPONENTIAL
    SEQUENCE 0 → 2πiZ → 𝒪 → 𝒪* → 0 is exact on C*, yet z ∈ 𝒪*(C*) has no
    global logarithm (cokernel on global sections ≅ Z, winding number).
  - DIRECT IMAGE (f_*ℱ)(V) = ℱ(f⁻¹V), left exact; f : X → pt gives
    f_*ℱ = Γ(X, ℱ). INVERSE IMAGE f⁻¹𝒢 = sheafification of V ↦ colim_{W ⊇
    f(V)} 𝒢(W); it is EXACT with (f⁻¹𝒢)ₓ = 𝒢_{f(x)}; f⁻¹A_Y = A_X.
    ADJUNCTION Hom(f⁻¹𝒢, ℱ) ≅ Hom(𝒢, f_*ℱ). Stalks of f_* are NOT stalks
    of ℱ in general: for j : R ∖ {0} → R, (j_*Z)₀ = Z²; for proper f,
    (f_*ℱ)_y = Γ(f⁻¹(y), ℱ|) (proper base change for H⁰).
  - OPEN/CLOSED DÉVISSAGE: for j : U → X open, i : Z = X ∖ U → X closed,
    EXTENSION BY ZERO j_!ℱ (sections of ℱ on V ∩ U with support closed in
    V; stalks ℱₓ on U, 0 on Z) and i_* (exact; stalks 0 off Z) give the
    exact sequence 0 → j_!j⁻¹ℱ → ℱ → i_*i⁻¹ℱ → 0. SKYSCRAPER x_*A.
  - FLASQUE (flabby): every restriction ℱ(X) → ℱ(U) is surjective.
    Examples: skyscrapers, every sheaf on a discrete space, the constant
    sheaf on an IRREDUCIBLE space, the GODEMENT sheaf U ↦ ∏_{x∈U} ℱₓ of
    discontinuous sections, injective sheaves, f_* of a flasque sheaf.
    Not flasque: Z_R, C⁰, C^∞, 𝒪. KEY LEMMA: if ℱ′ is flasque and 0 → ℱ′ →
    ℱ → ℱ″ → 0 is exact then Γ(ℱ) → Γ(ℱ″) is onto; if also ℱ is flasque
    so is ℱ″. Every sheaf embeds in a flasque one (Godement).
  - SOFT: X paracompact Hausdorff and every section over a CLOSED set
    (a germ of sections on a neighbourhood) extends to X. FINE: the sheaf
    ℰnd(ℱ) admits partitions of unity. Fine ⇒ soft ⇒ Γ-acyclic; flasque
    ⇒ soft. C⁰, C^∞, Ω^p, any C^∞-module (vector-bundle sections,
    distributions) are fine; Z_R and 𝒪 are not soft.
  - SHEAF COHOMOLOGY H^i(X, ℱ) = R^iΓ(X, ℱ) (Sh(X) has enough injectives):
    H⁰ = Γ; a short exact sequence of sheaves gives a LONG exact sequence;
    H^i can be computed by ANY Γ-acyclic resolution (flasque, soft on
    paracompact, fine). DE RHAM: 0 → R_M → Ω⁰ → Ω¹ → ... is a fine
    resolution, so H^i(M, R) ≅ H^i_dR(M). On a paracompact, locally
    contractible X, H^i(X, A_X) ≅ singular cohomology H^i(X; A).
    VANISHING: H^i = 0 for i > n on an n-manifold; H^i = 0 for i > 0 for
    flasque/soft sheaves and for skyscrapers; Grothendieck: i > dim X on
    a noetherian space.
  - ČECH COHOMOLOGY of a cover 𝔘 = {Uᵢ}: Č^p = ∏_{i₀<...<i_p}
    ℱ(U_{i₀...i_p}), (δc)_{i₀...i_{p+1}} = ∑_k (−1)^k c_{i₀..î_k..i_{p+1}}|.
    Ȟ⁰(𝔘, ℱ) = Γ(X, ℱ). Ȟ^p(X, ℱ) = colim over refinements. Ȟ¹ = H¹
    ALWAYS (torsors, e.g. Pic X = H¹(X, 𝒪_X*)); Ȟ^p = H^p on paracompact
    Hausdorff X. LERAY: if H^q(U_{i₀...i_p}, ℱ) = 0 for q > 0 then Ȟ(𝔘, ℱ)
    = H(X, ℱ). For A_X and a GOOD cover, Ȟ(𝔘) = simplicial cohomology of
    the NERVE. On P¹: h⁰(𝒪(n)) = max(n + 1, 0), h¹(𝒪(n)) = max(−n − 1, 0).
  - LOCALLY CONSTANT SHEAVES (LOCAL SYSTEMS): ℒ with X covered by opens on
    which ℒ ≅ M_U. On X connected, locally path-connected, semilocally
    simply connected: local systems with fibre M ↔ representations
    ρ : π₁(X, x) → Aut(M) (MONODROMY), up to conjugacy. On a simply
    connected space every local system is constant. Examples: the
    orientation sheaf of the Möbius band (monodromy −1), f_*Z for a
    covering (permutation monodromy), solutions of zf′ = αf on C*
    (monodromy e^{2πiα}). On S¹ with monodromy T: H⁰ = ker(T − 1) =
    M^T (invariants), H¹ = coker(T − 1) = M_T (coinvariants).

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Define presheaf and sheaf. Decide which are sheaves on R, and
    which axiom fails: (i) bounded continuous functions; (ii) constant
    functions with values in Z; (iii) C^∞ functions; (iv) locally
    constant Z-valued functions. (b) For X locally connected, show
    Γ(U, Z_X) ≅ Z^{π₀(U)}, and compute it for U = R ∖ {0, 1} and U = R²
    minus the unit circle.
    Answer: (i), (ii) not sheaves (gluing fails); (iii), (iv) sheaves.
    (b) Z³ and Z².
    Teacher explains: The presheaf axioms only say restriction is
    functorial. A sheaf is a presheaf where "local data determine and
    produce global data". (i) Locality holds (a function that vanishes
    locally vanishes), but gluing fails: on Uₙ = (n − 1, n + 1) the
    functions sₙ(x) = x are bounded and agree on overlaps, yet the glued
    function x on R is unbounded. Boundedness is a GLOBAL condition.
    (ii) Take U = (0, 1) ∪ (2, 3) with s₁ = 0 on (0, 1), s₂ = 1 on (2, 3).
    The overlap is empty, so they agree there vacuously, but no constant
    function on U restricts to both. (iii) Smoothness is local: if f is
    smooth on each Uᵢ, the glued function is smooth at each point, and it
    is unique. (iv) Same argument; "locally constant" is a local
    condition. (b) A locally constant function is constant on each
    connected component (the set where it takes a given value is open
    and closed). In a locally connected space the components of U are
    open, so any assignment of an integer to each component is locally
    constant: Γ(U, Z_X) = ∏_{π₀(U)} Z. R ∖ {0, 1} has 3 components: Z³.
    R² minus the circle has 2 (inside and outside): Z². Common mistakes:
    calling the constant PRESHEAF U ↦ Z the constant sheaf (it has
    Γ(U) = Z for every non-empty U, and even Γ(∅) = Z); forgetting that
    ℱ(∅) = 0 for a sheaf (glue the empty family). Now you try: Γ(U, Z_R)
    for U = R ∖ Z. (∏_{n∈Z} Z, uncountable)

D2. (a) Define the stalk ℱₓ and describe 𝒪_{C,0} and the stalk of C⁰_R
    at 0. (b) Prove: for a sheaf ℱ, a section s ∈ ℱ(U) with sₓ = 0 for
    all x ∈ U is 0; and a morphism of sheaves φ : ℱ → 𝒢 is an
    isomorphism iff every φₓ is an isomorphism. (c) Show that (b) fails
    for presheaves.
    Answer: (a) 𝒪_{C,0} = C{z}, power series with positive radius;
    C⁰-germs at 0. (b) locality, then gluing. (c) P(X) = Z, P(U) = 0 for
    U ≠ X.
    Teacher explains: (a) ℱₓ = colim_{U∋x} ℱ(U): an element is a pair
    (U, s), with (U, s) ~ (V, t) if s|_W = t|_W for some x ∈ W ⊆ U ∩ V.
    A holomorphic germ at 0 is its Taylor series, which converges on some
    disc and determines f there (identity theorem): 𝒪_{C,0} ≅ C{z}.
    Continuous germs have no such description: |x| and max(x, 0) differ
    on every neighbourhood of 0, yet agree at 0. (b) If sₓ = 0 there is
    an open Wₓ ∋ x with s|_Wₓ = 0; the Wₓ cover U, so s = 0 by locality.
    Now let every φₓ be bijective. INJECTIVE: if φ_U(s) = 0 then φₓ(sₓ)
    = 0, so sₓ = 0 for all x and s = 0. SURJECTIVE: given t ∈ 𝒢(U), for
    each x pick (Wₓ, sˣ) with φ(sˣ) = t on a (smaller) Wₓ. On Wₓ ∩ W_y
    the sections sˣ and sʸ have the same image under the injective φ, so
    they agree; glue them to s ∈ ℱ(U). Then φ(s) and t agree locally, so
    φ(s) = t. The converse (iso ⇒ stalk iso) is clear. Surjectivity used
    GLUING in ℱ, injectivity used LOCALITY. (c) For X = R, the presheaf
    P with P(R) = Z and P(U) = 0 otherwise has Pₓ = 0 (every x has a
    neighbourhood U ≠ R), so P → 0 is an iso on stalks but not on
    global sections. Common mistakes: believing "surjective on stalks ⇒
    surjective on sections" (see D4). Now you try: the stalk at 0 of the
    subsheaf ℐ ⊆ 𝒪_C of functions vanishing at 0. (zC{z})

D3. (a) Construct the sheafification ℱ⁺ of a presheaf ℱ and state its
    universal property. (b) Identify the sheafification of: the constant
    presheaf A; the presheaf of bounded continuous functions on R; the
    image presheaf U ↦ exp(𝒪(U)) ⊆ 𝒪*(U) on C*; the presheaf U ↦
    H¹_dR(U) on a manifold.
    Answer: (a) compatible families of germs. (b) the locally constant
    sheaf A_X; C⁰_R; 𝒪*; the zero sheaf.
    Teacher explains: (a) Put ℱ⁺(U) = {s : U → ⊔ₓℱₓ with s(x) ∈ ℱₓ, such
    that every x ∈ U has a neighbourhood W and t ∈ ℱ(W) with s(y) = t_y
    for all y ∈ W}. This is a sheaf because its sections are functions
    defined by a LOCAL condition. The map θ : ℱ → ℱ⁺, t ↦ (y ↦ t_y), is
    an iso on stalks, so (ℱ⁺)ₓ = ℱₓ. UNIVERSAL PROPERTY: every morphism
    ℱ → 𝒢 with 𝒢 a sheaf factors uniquely as ℱ → ℱ⁺ → 𝒢 (define it on
    germs; it glues because 𝒢 is a sheaf). θ is an iso iff ℱ is a sheaf.
    (b) Constant presheaf: a section of ℱ⁺ is locally the germ of a
    constant, i.e. a locally constant function: this is A_X. Bounded
    functions: every continuous germ is the germ of a bounded function
    (restrict to a small interval), so the stalks are all continuous
    germs and ℱ⁺ = C⁰_R. exp-image: every non-vanishing f has a
    logarithm on a small disc, so the stalks are 𝒪*ₓ and ℱ⁺ = 𝒪*: but
    ℱ(C*) ≠ 𝒪*(C*) since z ∉ exp(𝒪(C*)). H¹_dR(U): by the Poincaré lemma
    every closed form is exact on a small ball, so every stalk is 0 and
    ℱ⁺ = 0 although H¹_dR(R² ∖ 0) = R. Common mistakes: sheafifying by
    only adding gluings once (the Čech-H⁰ "plus construction" must in
    general be applied TWICE; the germ construction works in one step).
    Now you try: the sheafification of the presheaf of polynomial
    functions on R, and its sections over (0, 1) ∪ (2, 3). (locally
    polynomial functions; pairs of polynomials, R[x]²)

D4. (a) Show that the kernel presheaf of a morphism of sheaves is a sheaf
    and that exactness of sheaves is stalkwise. (b) Show that 0 → Z →
    𝒪 → 𝒪* → 0 (f ↦ e^{2πif}) is exact on X = C*, but that 𝒪(X) → 𝒪*(X)
    is not onto. Compute its cokernel, and the cokernel for X = C ∖ {0,
    1}. (c) Deduce that Γ is not right exact.
    Answer: (b) cokernel ≅ Z via f ↦ (1/2πi)∮_{|z|=1} f′/f; for C ∖ {0, 1}
    it is Z². (c) exactly this example.
    Teacher explains: (a) If φ : ℱ → 𝒢 and sᵢ ∈ ker φ(Uᵢ) are compatible,
    glue them in ℱ to s; φ(s)|_Uᵢ = φ(sᵢ) = 0 so φ(s) = 0 by locality in
    𝒢. So ker φ is a sheaf. The image presheaf need not be: im φ and
    coker φ are the SHEAFIFICATIONS of U ↦ im φ_U and U ↦ coker φ_U.
    Stalks commute with these constructions (filtered colimits are exact),
    so 0 → ℱ′ → ℱ → ℱ″ → 0 is exact iff it is exact at every stalk.
    (b) Stalk at a ∈ C*: a germ f with e^{2πif} = 1 is locally constant
    with values in Z, so the kernel is Z. Surjective: a non-vanishing g
    near a has a logarithm on a small disc, g = e^{2πif} with f =
    (1/2πi)log g. So the sequence is exact. Globally, g = z would need
    f′ = (1/2πi)g′/g = 1/(2πiz), but ∮_{|z|=1} dz/z = 2πi ≠ 0, so 1/z has
    no primitive on C*. In general g ∈ 𝒪*(X) is e^{2πif} iff g′/g has a
    primitive iff all its periods ∮g′/g vanish. On C* the only period is
    over |z| = 1 and n(g) = (1/2πi)∮g′/g ∈ Z (winding number); n(zᵏ) = k.
    So coker ≅ Z. On C ∖ {0, 1} there are two independent loops (around 0
    and around 1): g = zᵃ(z − 1)ᵇ realises (a, b), so coker ≅ Z². The
    cokernel is H¹(X, Z) (since H¹(X, 𝒪) = 0 on open subsets of C).
    (c) The surjection of sheaves 𝒪 → 𝒪* is not surjective on Γ(X, −).
    Common mistakes: checking surjectivity of a sheaf map on global
    sections; forgetting that the kernel is the constant sheaf Z, not
    the group Z. Now you try: is d/dz : 𝒪 → 𝒪 surjective on C*? on
    global sections? (yes as sheaves; no: 1/z, cokernel ≅ C by residues)

D5. (a) Define f_*ℱ and f⁻¹𝒢, state the adjunction, and prove
    (f⁻¹𝒢)ₓ = 𝒢_{f(x)}. (b) For j : R ∖ {0} → R compute the stalks of
    j_*Z. (c) For p : S¹ → S¹, z ↦ z², compute the stalks and global
    sections of p_*Z and describe its monodromy.
    Answer: (b) (j_*Z)ₓ = Z for x ≠ 0, (j_*Z)₀ = Z². (c) stalks Z²,
    Γ = Z, monodromy swaps the two factors.
    Teacher explains: (a) (f_*ℱ)(V) = ℱ(f⁻¹V): a sheaf on Y, since f⁻¹
    preserves covers. f⁻¹𝒢 is the sheafification of U ↦ colim_{W ⊇ f(U)}
    𝒢(W). The stalk at x of that presheaf is colim_{U∋x} colim_{W⊇f(U)}
    𝒢(W) = colim_{W∋f(x)} 𝒢(W) = 𝒢_{f(x)} (every W ∋ f(x) contains f(U)
    for U = f⁻¹W, and conversely), and sheafification keeps stalks. Hence
    f⁻¹ is EXACT. ADJUNCTION: Hom_X(f⁻¹𝒢, ℱ) ≅ Hom_Y(𝒢, f_*ℱ), both sides
    being compatible maps 𝒢(W) → ℱ(U) for f(U) ⊆ W; so f_* is left exact
    (a right adjoint). (b) For x ≠ 0, small intervals around x avoid 0 and
    are connected: stalk Z. At 0, (j_*Z)(−ε, ε) = Z((−ε, 0) ∪ (0, ε)) =
    Z², independent of ε: stalk Z². So (j_*ℱ)₀ is not a stalk of ℱ.
    (c) For a small arc A ⊂ S¹, p⁻¹(A) is two disjoint arcs, so
    (p_*Z)(A) = Z² and every stalk is Z². Globally Γ(S¹, p_*Z) =
    Γ(S¹, Z) = Z (connected). Going once around the base, a lift of a
    point moves to the OTHER preimage: the two sheets swap, so the
    monodromy is T = (0 1; 1 0). p_*Z is a rank-2 local system that is not
    constant (a constant one would have Γ = Z²). Its invariants
    ker(T − 1) = Z(1, 1) = Z match Γ. Common mistakes: thinking
    (f_*ℱ)_{f(x)} = ℱₓ (true for closed embeddings, false in general);
    writing f⁻¹𝒢(U) = 𝒢(f(U)) (f(U) need not be open). Now you try: stalks
    of j_*Z for j : R² ∖ {x-axis} → R² at (0, 0) and at (0, 1). (Z², Z)

D6. (a) Define flasque sheaves and show that skyscrapers, the Godement
    sheaf and the constant sheaf on an irreducible space are flasque,
    while Z_R and C⁰_R are not. (b) Prove: if 0 → ℱ′ → ℱ → ℱ″ → 0 is
    exact and ℱ′ is flasque, then Γ(X, ℱ) → Γ(X, ℱ″) is surjective.
    (c) Deduce that if ℱ′ and ℱ are flasque, so is ℱ″.
    Answer: (b) Zorn's lemma on partial lifts; (c) apply (b) on each U.
    Teacher explains: (a) Skyscraper x_*A: restriction is id_A or A → 0,
    both onto. Godement 𝒢(U) = ∏_{x∈U} ℱₓ: restriction is a projection of
    products, onto. Irreducible X: every non-empty open is connected
    (and dense), so A_X(U) = A for all U ≠ ∅, restrictions are identities.
    Z_R: Z = Γ(R) → Γ((0, 1) ∪ (2, 3)) = Z² is not onto. C⁰_R: 1/x on
    (0, 1) is not the restriction of a continuous function on R.
    (b) Let t ∈ ℱ″(X). Locally t lifts (stalk surjectivity). Consider
    pairs (U, s), s ∈ ℱ(U) mapping to t|_U, ordered by extension. Chains
    have upper bounds (glue), so by ZORN there is a maximal (U, s).
    Suppose U ≠ X; pick x ∉ U and a lift s′ of t on a neighbourhood V of
    x. On U ∩ V, s − s′ maps to 0, so s − s′ ∈ ℱ′(U ∩ V). FLASQUE: extend
    it to r ∈ ℱ′(X). Then s and s′ + r agree on U ∩ V and glue to a lift
    on U ∪ V ⊋ U: contradiction. So U = X. (c) Given u ∈ ℱ″(U), (b) on U
    (ℱ′|_U is flasque) lifts u to s ∈ ℱ(U); extend s to X (ℱ flasque) and
    push it to ℱ″(X): it restricts to u. Consequence (D7): flasque
    sheaves are Γ-acyclic. Common mistakes: trying to lift t in one step
    (lifts exist only locally); forgetting that the correction r lives in
    ℱ′, which is where flasqueness is used. Now you try: show that a
    sheaf on a discrete space is flasque. (ℱ(U) = ∏_{x∈U} ℱₓ)

D7. (a) Define H^i(X, ℱ) as a derived functor, explain why Sh(X) has
    enough injectives, and state the long exact sequence. (b) Show that
    flasque sheaves are acyclic and that H^i may be computed from any
    flasque resolution. (c) Use the sequence 0 → j_!Z_U → Z_R → i_*Z_K →
    0 with U = (0, 1), K = R ∖ U to compute H^*(R, j_!Z_U).
    Answer: (c) H⁰ = 0, H¹ = Z, H^i = 0 for i ≥ 2: a CONTRACTIBLE space
    with non-zero H¹ for a non-constant sheaf.
    Teacher explains: (a) Embed ℱ into ∏ₓ (iₓ)_*Iₓ with ℱₓ ⊆ Iₓ an
    injective abelian group: this is injective (Hom(𝒢, ∏(iₓ)_*Iₓ) =
    ∏Hom(𝒢ₓ, Iₓ), exact in 𝒢). Take an injective resolution 0 → ℱ → I⁰ →
    I¹ → ... and set H^i(X, ℱ) = H^i(Γ(X, I^•)). H⁰ = Γ (left exactness).
    A short exact sequence 0 → ℱ′ → ℱ → ℱ″ → 0 gives
       0 → H⁰(ℱ′) → H⁰(ℱ) → H⁰(ℱ″) → H¹(ℱ′) → H¹(ℱ) → H¹(ℱ″) → H²(ℱ′) → ...
    (b) Injectives are flasque (Hom(j_!Z_U, I) = I(U) and j_!Z_U ⊆ Z_X,
    so I(X) → I(U) is onto). If ℱ is flasque,
    embed it in an injective I, with quotient 𝒬: flasque by D6(c). The
    long sequence and D6(b) give H¹(ℱ) = coker(Γ(I) → Γ(𝒬)) = 0 and
    H^{i+1}(ℱ) = H^i(𝒬) for i ≥ 1; induction (𝒬 is flasque too) gives
    H^i(ℱ) = 0 for all i > 0. The standard lemma then says that ANY
    resolution by Γ-acyclics computes R^iΓ. (c) Stalks: on U the sequence
    is 0 → Z → Z → 0 → 0, on K it is 0 → 0 → Z → Z → 0: exact. The
    long exact sequence, with H^i(R, Z) = 0 (i > 0) and H^i(R, i_*Z_K) =
    H^i(K, Z) = 0 (i > 0; K = (−∞, 0] ∪ [1, ∞) is two contractible
    pieces):
       0 → H⁰(j_!Z_U) → Z → Z² → H¹(j_!Z_U) → 0.
    The middle map is the diagonal n ↦ (n, n). So H⁰ = 0 and H¹ ≅ Z²/Z ≅
    Z. (This is H¹_c((0, 1)) = Z.) Common mistakes: "contractible ⇒
    acyclic" — true only for constant (or locally constant) coefficients;
    confusing i_*Z_K with a constant sheaf on R. Now you try: H^*([0, 1],
    j_!Z_U) for U = [0, 1) (open in [0, 1]). (all 0: Z → Z(pt) is iso)

D8. (a) Define soft and fine sheaves and prove that on a paracompact
    Hausdorff space every fine sheaf is soft; show Z_R is not soft.
    (b) Prove de Rham's theorem H^i(M, R) ≅ H^i_dR(M) and compute
    H^*(R² ∖ {0}, R). (c) Using the fine sheaf C⁰_X of real functions,
    show H¹(X, Z) ≅ C(X, S¹)/exp(2πi C(X, R)) for X paracompact, and get
    H¹(S¹, Z) ≅ Z.
    Answer: (a) extend with a partition of unity; the closed set {0, 1}.
    (b) R, R, 0. (c) the class of g : X → S¹ is its "degree".
    Teacher explains: (a) SOFT: every section over a closed set K
    (= a section on some open V ⊇ K) extends to X, after shrinking. FINE:
    for every locally finite open cover {Uᵢ} there are endomorphisms ηᵢ
    of ℱ with supp ηᵢ ⊆ Uᵢ and ∑ηᵢ = id; e.g. multiplication by a
    partition of unity on any C⁰- or C^∞-module. Fine ⇒ soft: given s on
    V ⊇ K, cover X by V and X ∖ K, take η₁ + η₂ = id with supp η₁ ⊆ V,
    supp η₂ ⊆ X ∖ K; then η₁(s) (extended by 0 outside supp η₁) is a global
    section equal to s near K. Z_R: the section equal to 0 near 0 and 1
    near 1 over K = {0, 1} does not extend to the connected R. Soft
    sheaves on paracompact spaces are acyclic (same proof as D6/D7 with
    closed sets). (b) The sequence 0 → R_M → Ω⁰ → Ω¹ → Ω² → ... is exact
    by the POINCARÉ LEMMA (closed forms are locally exact; locally closed
    0-forms are constants), and each Ω^p is fine. So H^i(M, R) =
    H^i(Γ(Ω^•)) = H^i_dR(M). On R² ∖ 0, dθ is closed not exact, and
    H¹_dR = R·[dθ], H² = 0 (non-compact surface): R, R, 0. (c) 0 → Z → C⁰
    → C⁰_{S¹} → 0 (f ↦ e^{2πif}) is exact (local logarithms). H¹(X, C⁰) =
    0 as C⁰ is fine, so H¹(X, Z) = coker(C(X, R) → C(X, S¹)). For X = S¹,
    g ↦ deg g gives C(S¹, S¹)/exp ≅ Z (a map lifts iff degree 0).
    Common mistakes: calling Z_R fine because R is paracompact — fineness
    is about the sheaf, not the space; forgetting Poincaré's lemma is what
    makes the de Rham complex a RESOLUTION. Now you try: H¹(T², Z) via (c).
    (Z²: homotopy classes [T², S¹] ↔ pairs of degrees)

D9. (a) Define the Čech complex of a cover 𝔘 and show Ȟ⁰(𝔘, ℱ) = Γ(X, ℱ).
    (b) Compute Ȟ^*(𝔘, Z) for S¹ with 𝔘 = {U, V} two open arcs whose
    intersection has two components, and for three arcs U₁, U₂, U₃ with
    connected pairwise overlaps and empty triple overlap. (c) State
    Leray's theorem and explain why the answers equal H^*(S¹, Z).
    Answer: (b) both give Ȟ⁰ = Z, Ȟ¹ = Z, Ȟ^p = 0 for p ≥ 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) Č^p(𝔘, ℱ) = ∏_{i₀<...<i_p} ℱ(U_{i₀} ∩ ... ∩
    U_{i_p}) with (δc)_{i₀...i_{p+1}} = ∑_{k=0}^{p+1} (−1)^k
    c_{i₀...î_k...i_{p+1}}|. In degree 0, (δc)_{ij} = c_j − c_i, so a
    0-cocycle is a family agreeing on overlaps, i.e. (sheaf axioms)
    exactly a global section. (b) Two arcs: Č⁰ = Z(U) × Z(V) = Z², Č¹ =
    Z(U ∩ V) = Z² (two components W₁, W₂); δ(a, b) = (b − a, b − a). The
    kernel is {a = b} ≅ Z = Ȟ⁰; the image is the diagonal, and Z²/diagonal
    ≅ Z via (x, y) ↦ y − x: Ȟ¹ = Z. Three arcs: Č⁰ = Z³, Č¹ = Z³ (U₁₂,
    U₁₃, U₂₃), Č² = 0; δ(a₁, a₂, a₃) = (a₂ − a₁, a₃ − a₁, a₃ − a₂) has
    kernel the constants (Ȟ⁰ = Z) and rank 2 image, which is a SATURATED
    sublattice (it contains (1, 1, 0) and (0, 1, 1)); so Ȟ¹ = Z³/Z² ≅ Z,
    detected by x₁₂ − x₁₃ + x₂₃. The second cover is a GOOD cover: its
    nerve is the boundary of a triangle, and Čech cohomology of Z on a
    good cover is simplicial cohomology of the nerve. (c) LERAY: if ℱ has
    no higher cohomology on every finite intersection of the cover, then
    Ȟ^p(𝔘, ℱ) ≅ H^p(X, ℱ). Arcs and their intersections are disjoint
    unions of intervals, acyclic for Z. Common mistakes: using ordered
    tuples inconsistently (with alternating cochains only i₀ < ... < i_p
    are needed); taking Č¹ = Z instead of Z² when the overlap is
    disconnected. Now you try: Ȟ^*(𝔘, Z/2) for the two-arc cover. (Z/2,
    Z/2)

D10. (a) For S² covered by two open hemispheres (overlap an open band),
     compute Ȟ^*(𝔘, Z) and explain why it is NOT H^*(S², Z). (b) Cover S²
     by the four open sets obtained by thickening the faces of an
     inscribed tetrahedron (all intersections contractible or empty,
     quadruple overlap empty) and compute Ȟ^*(𝔘, Z). (c) Explain why
     Ȟ¹(X, ℱ) = H¹(X, ℱ) always, and interpret H¹(X, 𝒪*) as line bundles.
     Answer: (a) Z, 0, 0 (wrong in degree 2: the band has H¹ ≠ 0).
     (b) ranks 4, 6, 4 and Ȟ⁰ = Z, Ȟ¹ = 0, Ȟ² = Z.
     Teacher explains: (a) Č⁰ = Z², Č¹ = Z(band) = Z (connected), δ(a, b)
     = b − a is onto: Ȟ⁰ = Z, Ȟ¹ = 0, and there is no Č². But H²(S², Z) =
     Z. Leray fails: the band ≃ S¹ has H¹(band, Z) = Z ≠ 0. (In fact the
     Mayer–Vietoris sequence shows H²(S²) ≅ H¹(band) — the missing class
     lives there.) (b) The nerve is the boundary of a 3-simplex: 4
     vertices, 6 edges, 4 triangles, so Č⁰ = Z⁴, Č¹ = Z⁶, Č² = Z⁴, Č³ = 0.
     δ⁰ has kernel the constants, rank 3. δ¹ has rank 3 (its kernel
     contains im δ⁰, and a direct Smith-form computation gives invariant
     factors 1, 1, 1). Hence Ȟ⁰ = Z, Ȟ¹ = (6 − 3 − 3 = 0), and Ȟ² = Z⁴/im
     δ¹ ≅ Z (no torsion, all invariant factors 1). Check: χ = 4 − 6 + 4 =
     2 = χ(S²). (c) A Čech 1-cocycle (g_ij) with g_ij + g_jk = g_ik
     describes gluing data; refining the cover never loses a class
     (Ȟ¹(𝔘) → Ȟ¹(X) is injective), and every derived H¹ class is killed
     on some cover, whence Ȟ¹ = H¹ on any space. For 𝒪*, cocycles g_ij ∈
     𝒪*(U_ij) are TRANSITION FUNCTIONS of line bundles, coboundaries
     g_ij = h_j/h_i change of trivialisations: H¹(X, 𝒪_X*) = Pic(X).
     Common mistakes: trusting any cover; Euler characteristic alone does
     not give the individual groups. Now you try: Ȟ^*(𝔘, Z/2) for the
     tetrahedral cover. (Z/2, 0, Z/2)

D11. Using the standard cover U₀ = {x₀ ≠ 0}, U₁ = {x₁ ≠ 0} of P¹ over a
     field k, compute H⁰ and H¹ of 𝒪(n) by Čech cohomology for every
     n ∈ Z; give h⁰, h¹ for n = 3, −1, −4, and verify χ = n + 1.
     Answer: h⁰ = max(n + 1, 0), h¹ = max(−n − 1, 0); n = 3: (4, 0);
     n = −1: (0, 0); n = −4: (0, 3).
     Teacher explains: With t = x₁/x₀, 𝒪(n)(U₀) = k[t], 𝒪(n)(U₁) =
     k[t⁻¹]·tⁿ (trivialise by x₀ⁿ), and 𝒪(n)(U₀₁) = k[t, t⁻¹]. The Čech
     complex is
        0 → k[t] ⊕ tⁿk[t⁻¹] → k[t, t⁻¹] → 0,   (a, b) ↦ b − a.
     U₀, U₁, U₀₁ are affine, where quasi-coherent sheaves are acyclic, so
     Leray applies. H⁰ = k[t] ∩ tⁿk[t⁻¹] = span{tᵐ : 0 ≤ m ≤ n}, of
     dimension n + 1 if n ≥ 0, else 0. H¹ = k[t, t⁻¹]/(k[t] + tⁿk[t⁻¹]):
     the monomials tᵐ NOT in either summand satisfy m < 0 and m > n, i.e.
     n < m < 0, which is −n − 1 monomials when n ≤ −2, else none. n = 3:
     1, t, t², t³: h⁰ = 4, h¹ = 0. n = −1: 0, 0. n = −4: t⁻¹, t⁻², t⁻³:
     h¹ = 3. χ = h⁰ − h¹ = n + 1 in all cases (Riemann–Roch on P¹). Note
     H¹(P¹, 𝒪(−2)) = k is dual to H⁰(𝒪) (Serre duality, ω = 𝒪(−2)).
     Common mistakes: counting t⁰ or tⁿ in H¹ (they lie in k[t] or
     tⁿk[t⁻¹]); forgetting that ČECH on this cover is exact because the
     pieces are affine. Now you try: h⁰, h¹ of 𝒪(−2) ⊕ 𝒪(5). (6, 1)

D12. (a) Define locally constant sheaves and explain the correspondence
     with representations of π₁. (b) For a local system ℒ on S¹ with fibre
     M and monodromy T, compute H⁰ and H¹ with the two-arc cover.
     (c) Apply it to: the orientation sheaf of the Möbius band (M = Z,
     T = −1); p_*Z for p(z) = z² (T = swap); and the sheaf of solutions of
     zf′ = αf on C* (α ∈ C).
     Answer: (b) H⁰ = ker(T − 1), H¹ = coker(T − 1). (c) (0, Z/2);
     (Z, Z); (C, C) if α ∈ Z, (0, 0) otherwise.
     Teacher explains: (a) ℒ is LOCALLY CONSTANT if every point has a
     neighbourhood U with ℒ|_U ≅ M_U. Along a path γ from x to y, cover γ
     by such U's and continue sections: this gives isomorphisms ℒₓ → ℒ_y
     depending only on the homotopy class of γ. Loops give ρ : π₁(X, x) →
     Aut(ℒₓ). Conversely, from ρ build ℒ = (X̃ × M)/π₁ (sections: locally
     constant functions s on the universal cover with s(γx̃) = ρ(γ)s(x̃)).
     These are inverse equivalences (X connected, locally path-connected,
     semilocally simply connected). (b) Trivialise ℒ on U and V; on the
     two components W₁, W₂ of U ∩ V glue by id and by T. Then Č⁰ = M², Č¹
     = M² and δ(a, b) = (b − a, b − Ta). ker δ: b = a and a = Ta, so H⁰ =
     ker(T − 1). For H¹, the map (x, y) ↦ y − x from M² to M/(T − 1)M is
     onto and kills δ(a, b) since (b − Ta) − (b − a) = (1 − T)a; its
     kernel is exactly im δ. So H¹ = M/(T − 1)M. (c) Möbius: T − 1 = −2 on
     Z: H⁰ = 0 (no global orientation), H¹ = Z/2. p_*Z: T − 1 = (−1 1;
     1 −1), kernel Z(1, 1), image Z(1, −1) saturated: (Z, Z) = H^*(S¹, Z),
     as it must be since p is finite (p_* exact, H(S¹, p_*Z) = H(S¹, Z)).
     Solutions of zf′ = αf are c·z^α locally; continuing around 0
     multiplies by λ = e^{2πiα}: T = λ on C. If α ∈ Z, λ = 1: (C, C);
     otherwise λ − 1 is invertible: (0, 0). Common mistakes: "stalks
     isomorphic ⇒ sheaves isomorphic" (Möbius vs Z_{S¹}); computing H¹ as
     ker instead of coker. Now you try: M = Z², T = (0 −1; 1 0). (H⁰ = 0,
     H¹ = Z/2: det(T − 1) = 2)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Sections. Compute Γ(U, ℱ) (X locally connected; Z_X constant sheaf).
    a) Γ(R, Z_R)                        b) Γ(R ∖ {0}, Z_R)
    c) Γ(R ∖ {0, 1, 2}, Z_R)            d) Γ(R² ∖ {0}, Z)
    e) Γ(R² ∖ x-axis, Z)                f) Γ(R² ∖ (both axes), Z)
    g) Γ(R³ ∖ unit circle, Z)           h) Γ(S¹ ∖ {3 points}, Z)
    i) Γ(R² ∖ unit circle, Z/2)         j) Γ(R ∖ Z, Z) (describe)
    k) the constant PRESHEAF Z on (0, 1) ∪ (2, 3)
    l) Γ(S¹, orientation sheaf of the Möbius band)
    m) Γ(S¹, p_*Z), p(z) = z³; and over a small arc
    n) Γ(R, j_!Z_U), U = (0, 1)         o) Γ(R, j_*Z_U), U = R ∖ {0}
    p) Γ(C*, solutions of zf′ = f/2)     q) Γ(C*, solutions of zf′ = 3f)
    r) Γ(R, x_*A) (skyscraper at x)      s) Γ(C, 𝒪*)/exp(Γ(C, 𝒪))
    Answers:
      a) Z        b) Z²       c) Z⁴       d) Z        e) Z²
      f) Z⁴       g) Z (the complement of a circle in R³ is connected)
      h) Z³       i) (Z/2)²   j) ∏_{n∈Z} Z (one integer per interval)
      k) Z (vs Z² for the sheaf)           l) 0
      m) Z; Z³    n) 0 (support of a section must be closed in R)
      o) Z²       p) 0 (√z: monodromy −1)  q) C (c·z³)
      r) A        s) 0 (C simply connected: every g ∈ 𝒪*(C) has a log)

M2. Sheaf (S), presheaf but not sheaf (P), or not even a presheaf (N)
    under restriction? For P name the failing axiom.
    a) continuous functions on X      b) bounded continuous functions
    c) constant real functions        d) U ↦ Z for all U, incl. ∅
    e) L¹(U) (integrable functions)   f) L¹_loc(U)
    g) C^∞ functions with compact support in U
    h) polynomial functions on U ⊆ R  i) harmonic functions on U ⊆ R²
    j) U ↦ H¹(U; Z) (U ⊆ R²)          k) U ↦ C⁰(U)/{constants}
    l) sections of a vector bundle     m) real-analytic functions
    n) holomorphic f on U with f ≠ 0 and a logarithm on U
    o) continuous maps U → S¹ (group) p) holomorphic 1-forms that are exact
    q) U ↦ {f ∈ 𝒪(U) : f′ = f}        r) meromorphic functions on U ⊆ C
    Answers:
      a) S      b) P (gluing)       c) P (gluing on disjoint opens)
      d) P (locality/gluing: a sheaf has ℱ(∅) = 0)
      e) P (gluing: 1 on R)          f) S
      g) N (extension by zero, not restriction, is natural; restriction
         of a compactly supported function need not be compactly
         supported)
      h) P (gluing on disconnected U)          i) S
      j) P (locality: dθ ≠ 0 on R² ∖ 0 but 0 on small discs)
      k) P (locality: on (0, 1) ∪ (2, 3) the class of 𝟙_{(2,3)} is
         locally constant, hence locally 0, but it is not 0)
      l) S      m) S      n) P (gluing: z on C*)       o) S
      p) P (gluing: dz/z)            q) S (a local system: c·e^z)   r) S

M3. Stalks. Compute ℱₓ.
    a) (Z_R)₅                        b) (x_*A)_y, y ≠ x, X Hausdorff
    c) (x_*A)ₓ                       d) 𝒪_{C,0}
    e) ideal sheaf of {0} in 𝒪_C, at 0 and at 1
    f) (j_!Z_U)₀ and (j_!Z_U)_{1/2}, U = (0, 1) ⊂ R
    g) (j_*Z_U)₀ and (j_*Z_U)₂, U = (0, 1) ⊂ R
    h) (j_*Z_U)₀, U = R ∖ {0}         i) (j_*Z_U)₀, U = R² ∖ {0}
    j) (j_*Z_U)_{(0,0)}, U = R² ∖ x-axis
    k) (j_*Z_U) at (0, 0) and at (1, 0), U = R² ∖ ([0, ∞) × {0})
    l) (p_*Z)_y, p : S¹ → S¹, z ↦ z⁵
    m) (p_*Z)_y, p : R → S¹, t ↦ e^{2πit}
    n) (f_*Z_R)₀ and (f_*Z_R)₁, f : R → R constant 0
    o) presheaf P(R) = Z, P(U) = 0 (U ≠ R), at any x
    p) (C^∞_R / x·C^∞_R) at 0 and at 1
    q) (f⁻¹𝒢)ₓ for f : X → Y, and (f⁻¹A_Y)ₓ
    r) (i⁻¹𝒪_C)ₓ, i : R ↪ C, x ∈ R
    Answers:
      a) Z       b) 0       c) A       d) C{z}
      e) zC{z}; C{z − 1} (all germs: ℐ = 𝒪 away from 0)
      f) 0; Z    g) Z ((0, ε) connected); 0     h) Z²
      i) Z (punctured disc connected)          j) Z²
      k) Z (disc minus a radius is connected); Z² (disc cut in two)
      l) Z⁵      m) ∏_{n∈Z} Z (preimage of an arc: Z copies)
      n) Z (= Γ(R, Z)); 0 (small nbhd of 1 has empty preimage)
      o) 0       p) R (f ↦ f(0); Hadamard: f(0) = 0 ⇒ f = xg); 0 (x is
         invertible near 1)
      q) 𝒢_{f(x)}; A                          r) C{z − x}

M4. Sheafification. Identify ℱ⁺ (a known sheaf, or its stalks).
    a) the constant presheaf Z on R     b) bounded continuous functions
    c) polynomial functions on R; sections of ℱ⁺ over (0, 1) ∪ (2, 3)
    d) U ↦ exp(𝒪(U)) ⊆ 𝒪*(U) on C*     e) U ↦ H¹_dR(U) on R² ∖ {0}
    f) U ↦ Z^{π₀(U)} on R               g) P(R) = Z, P(U) = 0 (U ≠ R)
    h) U ↦ Z if 0 ∈ U, 0 otherwise (identity restrictions)
    i) X = {a, b} discrete, P(X) = P({a}) = P({b}) = Z, diagonal
       restrictions: ℱ⁺(X)
    j) U ↦ C^∞(U)/{constants} on R
    k) U ↦ d(C^∞(U)) ⊆ Ω¹(U) (exact 1-forms) on R² ∖ {0}
    l) U ↦ Z-valued locally constant functions with FINITE image, on R;
       compare sections over R ∖ Z
    m) U ↦ 𝒪(U)/z𝒪(U) on C            n) U ↦ H¹(U; Z) on S¹
    o) U ↦ ℱ(U)/𝒢(U) for sheaves 𝒢 ⊆ ℱ
    p) U ↦ ℱ(U) ⊗ Q for ℱ = Z_X
    Answers:
      a) Z_R (locally constant functions)   b) C⁰_R
      c) locally polynomial functions (stalk R[x]); R[x]²
      d) 𝒪*       e) 0 (Poincaré lemma)      f) already a sheaf, Z_R
      g) 0 (all stalks 0)                   h) already a sheaf: 0_*Z
      i) Z² (P was not a sheaf: gluing fails on {a} ∪ {b})
      j) ≅ Ω¹_R via f ↦ df (kernel of d on germs = constants)
      k) the sheaf 𝒵¹ of CLOSED 1-forms (dθ is locally exact)
      l) Z_R; the presheaf gives finitely-valued sequences, the sheaf
         gives all of ∏_{n∈Z} Z
      m) the skyscraper C at 0 (stalk C{z}/zC{z} = C; z invertible off 0)
      n) 0 (arcs are contractible)          o) the quotient sheaf ℱ/𝒢
      p) Q_X

M5. Exactness. For each map φ of sheaves say whether it is injective (I)
    and surjective (S) as a sheaf map, and give the cokernel of φ on
    GLOBAL sections.
    a) ·2 : Z_X → Z_X, X = S¹         b) exp : 𝒪 → 𝒪* on C
    c) exp : 𝒪 → 𝒪* on C*             d) exp on C ∖ {0, 1, 2}
    e) exp on the annulus 1 < |z| < 2  f) d/dz : 𝒪 → 𝒪 on C ∖ {0, 1}
    g) d/dx : C^∞ → C^∞ on R ∖ {0}     h) d : Ω⁰ → 𝒵¹ on R² ∖ {0}
    i) d : Ω⁰ → 𝒵¹ on R² ∖ {2 points}  j) d : Ω⁰ → 𝒵¹ on T²
    k) d : Ω⁰ → 𝒵¹ on S²              l) ·x : C^∞_R → C^∞_R
    m) f ↦ f(0) : C⁰_R → 0_*R (skyscraper)
    n) restriction Z_R → i_*Z_K, K = {0, 1}
    o) R_X → (R/Z)_X (constant sheaves), X = S¹
    p) C⁰ → C⁰_{S¹}, f ↦ e^{2πif}, on S¹, T² and S²
    Answers:
      a) I, not S (coker sheaf Z/2); Γ-coker Z/2
      b) not I (kernel Z), S; 0                 c) S; Z (winding number)
      d) S; Z³                 e) S; Z           f) S; C² (two residues)
      g) S; 0 (antiderivative on each half-line; kernel R²)
      h) S (Poincaré lemma); R (class of dθ)    i) S; R²
      j) S; R²                 k) S; 0
      l) I (xf = 0 ⇒ f = 0 off 0 ⇒ f = 0), not S; coker sheaf 0_*R
      m) S; 0                  n) S; Z (Z → Z² diagonal)
      o) S; 0 (Γ = R → R/Z onto)
      p) S; Z, Z², 0 (H¹(X; Z), since C⁰ is fine)

M7. For each sheaf decide: flasque (Fl), soft (So), fine (Fi) — answer
    Y/N three times (spaces paracompact Hausdorff).

      h) Y Y Y     i) N Y Y (e^{1/x} on (0, ∞) does not extend)
      j) N N N (Z → Z² for (1, 2) ∪ (3, 4))

M9. Sheaf cohomology H^i(X, A_X) of constant sheaves (list the non-zero
    groups; X locally contractible unless stated).
    a) a point        b) Rⁿ          c) S¹         d) Sⁿ (n ≥ 1)

         (singular H⁰ would be Z^C: the theories differ here)

M10. Long exact sequences. Compute, using 0 → j_!Z_U → Z_X → i_*Z_K → 0
     (K = X ∖ U), Mayer–Vietoris, or the exponential sequence.
     a) H^*(S¹, j_!Z_U), U = S¹ ∖ {pt}
     b) H^*(S¹, j_!Z_U), U = S¹ ∖ {2 points}
     c) H^*([0, 1], j_!Z_U), U = (0, 1)
     d) H^*([0, 1], j_!Z_U), U = [0, 1)
     e) H^*(R, j_!Z_U), U = (0, 1)     f) H^*(R, j_!Z_U), U = (0, ∞)
     g) H^*(R, j_!Z_U), U = R ∖ {0}
     h) H^*(S², j_!Z_U), U = S² ∖ {pt}
     i) H^*(S², Z) by Mayer–Vietoris on two hemispheres
     j) H¹(S¹, (R/Z)_X) from 0 → Z → R → R/Z → 0 (constant sheaves)
     k) H^*(S¹, Z/n) from 0 → Z →(·n) Z → Z/n → 0
     l) Pic(P¹) = H¹(P¹, 𝒪*) (use H¹(𝒪) = H²(𝒪) = 0)
     m) Pic(U) for U ⊆ C open (H¹(U, 𝒪) = 0)
     n) Pic⁰ and Pic/Pic⁰ of a compact Riemann surface of genus g
     o) H^i(X, x_*A), i > 0
     p) H^*(T², j_!Z_U), U = T² ∖ {pt}

       a) 0, Z (H⁰: Z → Z iso)          b) 0, Z² (0 → Z → Z² → H¹ → Z → 0)
       c) 0, Z                          d) 0, 0

       g) 0, 0 (Z → Z iso; note ≠ H_c(U) since R is not compact)
       h) 0, 0, Z                       i) Z, 0, Z (H²(S²) ≅ H¹(band))
       j) R/Z (H¹(Z) = Z ↪ H¹(R) = R, cokernel R/Z)
       k) Z/n, Z/n                      l) Z (degree; c₁ : Pic ≅ H²(P¹, Z))
       m) 0 (H²(U, Z) = 0 too)
       n) Pic⁰ ≅ H¹(X, 𝒪)/H¹(X, Z) ≅ Cᵍ/Z^{2g} (the Jacobian); Z (degree)
       o) 0 (skyscrapers are flasque)
