M11. Locally constant sheaves. On S¹ with fibre M and monodromy T give
     (H⁰, H¹) = (ker(T − 1), coker(T − 1)); then answer the other parts.
     a) M = Z, T = 1                  b) M = Z, T = −1
     c) M = Z², T = (0 1; 1 0)        d) M = Z², T = (1 1; 0 1)
     e) M = Z², T = (1 2; 0 1)        f) M = Z², T = (2 1; 1 1)
     g) M = Z², T = (0 −1; 1 0)       h) M = Z², T = −I
     i) M = Z², T = (0 1; −1 −1)      j) M = Z³, T = cyclic permutation
     k) M = Z², T = diag(3, 1)        l) M = Z/6, T = −1
     m) M = Q, T = 2                  n) M = C, T = e^{2πi/3}
     o) number of rank-1 Z-local systems (up to iso) on S¹, T², RP²,
        K (Klein), Σ_g, S², S¹ ∨ S¹
     p) rank-1 local systems with fibre F_p on S¹ (up to iso)
     q) rank-1 C-local systems on C ∖ {3 points} (parameter space)
     r) T², M = Z, monodromies (−1, 1): H⁰, H¹, H² (complex M → M² → M)
     s) local systems on a simply connected space
     Answers:
       a) Z, Z          b) 0, Z/2        c) Z, Z          d) Z, Z
       e) Z, Z ⊕ Z/2    f) 0, 0 (det(T − 1) = −1)       g) 0, Z/2
       h) 0, (Z/2)²     i) 0, Z/3        j) Z, Z          k) Z, Z ⊕ Z/2
       l) Z/2, Z/2      m) 0, 0          n) 0, 0
       o) |Hom(π₁, {±1})| = |H¹(X; Z/2)|: 2, 4, 2, 4, 2^{2g}, 1, 4
       p) p − 1 (Aut(F_p) = F_p* abelian: iso classes = Hom(Z, F_p*))
       q) (C*)³ (monodromy around each puncture)
       r) 0, Z/2, Z/2 (d⁰m = (−2m, 0), d¹(x, y) = 2y)
       s) all constant

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) A presheaf all of whose stalks are 0 is the zero presheaf.
     b) A sheaf all of whose stalks are 0 is the zero sheaf.
     c) A morphism of sheaves that is surjective on every stalk is
        surjective on global sections.
     d) The kernel presheaf of a morphism of sheaves is a sheaf.
     e) The image presheaf of a morphism of sheaves is a sheaf.
     f) f⁻¹ is an exact functor.      g) f_* is an exact functor.
     h) (f_*ℱ)_{f(x)} ≅ ℱₓ for every continuous f.
     i) If ℱ′ and ℱ″ are flasque and 0 → ℱ′ → ℱ → ℱ″ → 0 is exact, then
        ℱ is flasque.
     j) The constant sheaf Z_R is flasque.
     k) Every flasque sheaf is Γ-acyclic.
     l) If X is contractible then H^i(X, ℱ) = 0 for every sheaf ℱ, i > 0.
     m) The Čech cohomology of ANY open cover computes H^*(X, ℱ).
     n) Ȟ¹(X, ℱ) ≅ H¹(X, ℱ) for every space X and sheaf ℱ.
     o) On a simply connected, locally path-connected space every locally
        constant sheaf is constant.
     p) Two locally constant sheaves with isomorphic stalks are
        isomorphic.
     q) 𝒪_C is soft.
     r) Fine sheaves on paracompact Hausdorff spaces are acyclic.
     s) On an irreducible space H^i(X, A_X) = 0 for i > 0.
     t) Γ(X, −) is right exact.
     u) Every sheaf embeds in a flasque sheaf.
     v) H^i(M, ℱ) = 0 for i > n for every sheaf on an n-manifold M.
     w) φ is injective as a sheaf map iff every φ_U is injective.
     x) φ is surjective as a sheaf map iff every φ_U is surjective.
     y) Sheaf cohomology of Z_X equals singular cohomology for every X.
     Answers:
       a) F: P(R) = Z, P(U) = 0 (U ≠ R)       b) T
       c) F: exp : 𝒪 → 𝒪* on C* (z has no log)  d) T
       e) F: the exp-image on C*               f) T
       g) F: f : C* → pt, f_* = Γ, exp sequence
       h) F: j : R ∖ {0} → R, (j_*Z)₀ = Z²
       i) T (lift, extend, correct by a section of ℱ′)
       j) F: Z → Z² for (0, 1) ∪ (2, 3)         k) T
       l) F: H¹(R, j_!Z_{(0,1)}) = Z            m) F: S² with hemispheres
       n) T              o) T
       p) F: Möbius orientation sheaf vs Z_{S¹} (both have stalks Z)
       q) F: 0 near 0, 1 near 1 on K = {0, 1} (identity theorem)
       r) T              s) T (A_X is flasque)  t) F: see c)
       u) T (Godement)   v) T
       w) T              x) F: exp on C* (only "⇐" holds)
       y) F: the Cantor set (M9 r)); true for locally contractible
          paracompact X

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make every abstract statement concrete on three test cases
  — the constant sheaf Z on R ∖ {0} (π₀ matters), the exponential sequence
  on C* (surjective on stalks, not on sections), and j_*Z, j_!Z for j :
  R ∖ {0} → R (stalks Z² and 0 at the origin). Always compute stalks
  first: exactness, injectivity and isomorphism are stalkwise, while
  global sections are where the topology lives. For Čech computations
  draw the nerve, write the ranks of Č⁰, Č¹, Č², check χ, and ask
  whether every intersection is acyclic (Leray) before trusting the
  answer; for local systems on S¹ just remember ker(T − 1), coker(T − 1).
  Harder: prove that injective sheaves are flasque and flasque sheaves
  acyclic in full; prove soft ⇒ acyclic on paracompact spaces and derive
  de Rham and Dolbeault (H^{p,q}(X) ≅ H^q(X, Ω^p)); prove Leray's theorem
  via the Čech-to-derived spectral sequence and that Ȟ = H on paracompact
  Hausdorff spaces; construct the equivalence between local systems and
  π₁-representations and show H^*(X, ℒ_ρ) = H^*(π₁, M) for aspherical X;
  compute H^*(T², ℒ) for any pair of commuting monodromies via the
  Koszul complex; prove proper base change (f_*ℱ)_y = Γ(f⁻¹(y), ℱ) and
  set up the Leray spectral sequence H^p(Y, R^q f_*ℱ) ⇒ H^{p+q}(X, ℱ);
  and prove Grothendieck's vanishing theorem on noetherian spaces.


================================================================================
TOPIC 12.8 — COMMUTATIVE ALGEBRA: NOETHERIAN AND ARTINIAN RINGS, PRIMARY
DECOMPOSITION, INTEGRAL EXTENSIONS, NULLSTELLENSATZ, DIMENSION THEORY,
COMPLETIONS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Rings are commutative with 1. A module M is NOETHERIAN if it satisfies
    the ascending chain condition (ACC) on submodules, equivalently every
    submodule is finitely generated, equivalently every non-empty family of
    submodules has a maximal element. For 0 → M′ → M → M″ → 0 exact, M is
    Noetherian iff M′ and M″ are; a finitely generated module over a
    Noetherian ring is Noetherian. Z, fields, PIDs, k[[x]] are Noetherian;
    k[x₁, x₂, ...], the ring of all algebraic integers and C([0, 1]) are not.
  - HILBERT BASIS THEOREM: A Noetherian ⇒ A[x] Noetherian (and A[[x]]).
    Hence every finitely generated algebra over a field or over Z, and all
    its quotients and localizations S⁻¹A, are Noetherian. A subring of a
    Noetherian ring need not be: k[x, xy, xy², xy³, ...] ⊆ k[x, y].
  - ARTINIAN = descending chain condition (DCC). In an Artinian ring every
    prime is maximal, there are finitely many maximal ideals, and the
    Jacobson radical (= nilradical) is nilpotent. AKIZUKI-HOPKINS: A is
    Artinian ⇔ A is Noetherian of dimension 0. STRUCTURE THEOREM: an
    Artinian ring is uniquely a finite product of Artinian LOCAL rings
    (e.g. Z/360 ≅ Z/8 × Z/9 × Z/5). A module has finite LENGTH ℓ(M) iff it
    is both Noetherian and Artinian; ℓ is additive on short exact sequences.
    For a local k-algebra with residue field k, ℓ(A) = dim_k A.
  - PRIMARY IDEALS: q ≠ A is primary if xy ∈ q, x ∉ q ⇒ yⁿ ∈ q for some n;
    then p = √q is prime (q is p-primary). An ideal whose radical is a
    MAXIMAL ideal is primary (so 𝔪ⁿ is 𝔪-primary), but a power of a prime
    need not be primary, and an ideal with prime radical need not be
    primary: (x², xy) has radical (x) yet is not primary.
  - LASKER-NOETHER: in a Noetherian ring every ideal is a finite
    intersection of primary ideals (via irreducible ideals). A MINIMAL
    (irredundant) decomposition I = q₁ ∩ ... ∩ qᵣ has distinct radicals
    pᵢ = √qᵢ and no qᵢ ⊇ ⋂_{j≠i} qⱼ. FIRST UNIQUENESS: the set Ass(A/I) =
    {pᵢ} is unique; pᵢ are exactly the primes of the form (I : x). SECOND
    UNIQUENESS: the components belonging to MINIMAL primes are unique;
    EMBEDDED components are not: (x², xy) = (x) ∩ (x², y) = (x) ∩ (x², y +
    ax) for every a. The zero-divisors of A/I are ⋃ Ass(A/I).
  - INTEGRAL EXTENSIONS: b ∈ B ⊇ A is integral over A if bⁿ + a₁bⁿ⁻¹ + ...
    + aₙ = 0, equivalently A[b] is a finitely generated A-module
    (determinant trick). Integral elements form a subring (the INTEGRAL
    CLOSURE); integrality is transitive. A domain is NORMAL if it is
    integrally closed in its fraction field; UFDs are normal. The integral
    closure of Z in Q(√d) (d squarefree) is Z[√d] if d ≡ 2, 3 mod 4 and
    Z[(1 + √d)/2] if d ≡ 1 mod 4. k[t², t³] is not normal (closure k[t]).
  - COHEN-SEIDENBERG for A ⊆ B integral: LYING OVER (every prime p of A is
    q ∩ A for some prime q of B), GOING UP, INCOMPARABILITY (q ⊆ q′ over the
    same p ⇒ q = q′), and q maximal ⇔ q ∩ A maximal; hence dim A = dim B.
    GOING DOWN holds if in addition A is a normal domain and B is a domain
    (or B is flat over A).
  - NOETHER NORMALIZATION: a finitely generated k-algebra A contains
    algebraically independent y₁, ..., y_d with A finite (integral) over
    k[y₁, ..., y_d]; d = tr.deg_k Frac(A) for a domain = dim A. ZARISKI'S
    LEMMA: if a field K is a finitely generated k-algebra then K/k is
    finite.
  - NULLSTELLENSATZ (k algebraically closed): WEAK form: the maximal ideals
    of k[x₁, ..., xₙ] are exactly (x₁ − a₁, ..., xₙ − aₙ); a system of
    polynomials f₁ = ... = f_m = 0 has no common zero iff 1 ∈ (f₁, ..., f_m).
    STRONG form: I(V(I)) = √I (proof: RABINOWITSCH trick with an extra
    variable y and 1 − yf). Hence V ↔ I is an inclusion-reversing bijection
    between algebraic sets and radical ideals, irreducible sets ↔ primes,
    points ↔ maximal ideals. Finitely generated k-algebras are JACOBSON:
    the nilradical equals the Jacobson radical. Over R it fails:
    (x² + 1) is maximal in R[x] with V = ∅.
  - KRULL DIMENSION dim A = sup of lengths n of prime chains p₀ ⊊ ... ⊊ pₙ;
    HEIGHT ht p = dim A_p. dim k[x₁, ..., xₙ] = n, dim Z = 1, dim Z[x] = 2,
    dim of a field or Artinian ring = 0, dim A/I = max over the minimal
    primes of I. For a finitely generated k-domain, ht p + dim A/p = dim A.
    A Noetherian ring can have infinite dimension (Nagata's example), but a
    Noetherian local ring has finite dimension.
  - KRULL'S PRINCIPAL IDEAL THEOREM: in a Noetherian ring a minimal prime
    over (f) has height ≤ 1 (= 1 if f is a non-zero-divisor); the HEIGHT
    THEOREM: a minimal prime over (f₁, ..., f_r) has height ≤ r. In a
    Noetherian local ring (A, 𝔪, k): dim A = least number of generators of
    an 𝔪-primary ideal (SYSTEM OF PARAMETERS) = degree of the
    HILBERT-SAMUEL polynomial of n ↦ ℓ(A/𝔪ⁿ), and dim A ≤ dim_k 𝔪/𝔪²
    (embedding dimension). A is REGULAR if equality holds; regular local
    rings are domains (and UFDs).
  - HILBERT-SAMUEL: ℓ(A/𝔪ⁿ) = (e/d!)nᵈ + lower terms for n ≫ 0, d = dim A,
    e = MULTIPLICITY (e = 1 for a regular local ring; e = the order of the
    lowest-degree term for a plane curve singularity, e.g. e = 2 for the
    cusp y² = x³ at the origin).
  - COMPLETIONS: the I-adic completion Â = lim← A/Iⁿ; Z_p = lim← Z/pⁿ,
    k[[x₁, ..., xₙ]] = completion of k[x₁, ..., xₙ] at (x₁, ..., xₙ). For A
    Noetherian: Â is Noetherian, flat over A, M̂ = M ⊗ Â for finitely
    generated M, completion is exact (ARTIN-REES LEMMA), and KRULL'S
    INTERSECTION THEOREM ⋂ Iⁿ = 0 holds when A is a Noetherian domain or
    local with I ⊆ 𝔪 (fails for C^∞ germs: e^(−1/x²) ∈ ⋂ 𝔪ⁿ).
  - HENSEL'S LEMMA: in a complete local ring, a simple root mod 𝔪 of a
    monic f (f(a) ∈ 𝔪, f′(a) a unit) lifts uniquely to a root, via Newton's
    iteration a ↦ a − f(a)/f′(a). Units of k[[x]] are the series with
    non-zero constant term; the node y² = x²(1 + x) is irreducible in
    k[x, y] (localized) but splits into two branches in k[[x, y]].

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. (a) Prove that the following are equivalent for an A-module M: ACC on
    submodules; every submodule is finitely generated. (b) Prove the
    Hilbert basis theorem. (c) Show that k[x₁, x₂, x₃, ...] and the subring
    S = k[x, xy, xy², ...] of k[x, y] are not Noetherian.
    Answer: (a) union of a chain is a submodule; (b) ideals of leading
    coefficients; (c) explicit strictly increasing chains.
    Teacher explains: (a) Suppose ACC and let N ⊆ M be a submodule that is
    not finitely generated. Choose n₁ ∈ N, then n₂ ∈ N ∖ (n₁), n₃ ∈ N ∖
    (n₁, n₂), ... ; the chain (n₁) ⊊ (n₁, n₂) ⊊ ... never stops. Conversely,
    if N₁ ⊆ N₂ ⊆ ... then N = ⋃Nᵢ is a submodule (a chain!), generated by
    finitely many elements, all lying in some N_k, so N_k = N_{k+1} = ... .
    (b) Let I ⊆ A[x] be an ideal. For each d let L_d ⊆ A be the set of
    leading coefficients of elements of I of degree d (together with 0).
    Each L_d is an ideal and L₀ ⊆ L₁ ⊆ ... (multiply by x). By ACC the chain
    stabilises at some N, and each L_d (d ≤ N) is finitely generated. Pick
    for every d ≤ N and every generator of L_d a polynomial in I of degree
    d with that leading coefficient: finitely many f₁, ..., f_s. Claim I =
    (f₁, ..., f_s). If f ∈ I has degree d, its leading coefficient is an
    A-combination of the leading coefficients of the chosen fⱼ of degree
    min(d, N); subtract ∑aⱼ x^(d − deg fⱼ) fⱼ to kill the leading term, and
    induct on the degree. (c) (x₁) ⊊ (x₁, x₂) ⊊ ... in the polynomial ring
    in infinitely many variables. In S: xyⁿ⁺¹ ∉ (x, xy, ..., xyⁿ)S, since
    every element of that ideal is a sum of terms xy^j·s with j ≤ n and s ∈
    S; the monomials of S other than 1 all contain x, so the only way to
    get xyⁿ⁺¹ is j ≤ n and s = yⁿ⁺¹⁻ʲ, which is not in S. So the chain
    of ideals (x) ⊊ (x, xy) ⊊ (x, xy, xy²) ⊊ ... is strictly increasing,
    although S ⊆ k[x, y] and k[x, y] is Noetherian. Common mistakes:
    "subrings of Noetherian rings are Noetherian" (false; QUOTIENTS and
    LOCALIZATIONS are); forgetting that finite generation of every ideal,
    not just of the ring as an algebra, is needed. Now you try: is Z[1/2]
    = Z[x]/(2x − 1) Noetherian? (yes: quotient of Z[x])

D2. (a) Prove that in an Artinian ring every prime ideal is maximal and
    there are only finitely many maximal ideals. (b) Decompose A = Z/360Z
    as a product of local Artinian rings and compute ℓ_A(A). (c) Compute
    the length (= dim over k) of R = k[x, y]/(x², xy, y³).
    Answer: (a) A/p is an Artinian domain, hence a field; (b) Z/8 × Z/9 ×
    Z/5, length 3 + 2 + 1 = 6; (c) basis 1, x, y, y²: length 4.
    Teacher explains: (a) Let p be prime and 0 ≠ x ∈ D = A/p. The chain
    (x) ⊇ (x²) ⊇ ... stops: xⁿ = xⁿ⁺¹y for some y, and cancelling xⁿ in the
    domain gives xy = 1. So D is a field and p is maximal. If 𝔪₁, 𝔪₂, ...
    were infinitely many distinct maximal ideals, 𝔪₁ ⊋ 𝔪₁𝔪₂ ⊋ 𝔪₁𝔪₂𝔪₃ ⊋
    ... would be strictly decreasing: if 𝔪₁⋯𝔪ₙ = 𝔪₁⋯𝔪ₙ₊₁ ⊆ 𝔪ₙ₊₁, then
    some 𝔪ᵢ ⊆ 𝔪ₙ₊₁ (prime avoidance for products), so 𝔪ᵢ = 𝔪ₙ₊₁. (b) 360 =
    2³·3²·5 and the CRT gives Z/360 ≅ Z/8 × Z/9 × Z/5, each factor local
    (maximal ideals (2), (3), (0)). A composition series of Z/8 is Z/8 ⊃
    2Z/8 ⊃ 4Z/8 ⊃ 0, length 3. Length is additive over the product: 3 + 2 +
    1 = 6 = the number of prime factors of 360 with multiplicity. (c) R is
    spanned by the monomials not in (x², xy, y³): 1, x, y, y² (x² = xy = 0
    kills every other monomial). A k-algebra of k-dimension 4 whose only
    maximal ideal is (x, y) with residue field k has length 4; a
    composition series is R ⊃ (x, y) ⊃ (x, y²) ⊃ (y²) ⊃ 0. Common mistakes:
    counting generators instead of basis monomials; forgetting that length
    counts composition factors (so ℓ(Z/8) = 3, not 1 or 8). Now you try:
    the length of Z/1000 and of k[x]/(x⁵). (6; 5)

D3. In A = k[x, y]: (a) show that I = (x², xy) is not primary although √I
    = (x) is prime; (b) find a minimal primary decomposition and the
    associated primes; (c) show that the embedded component is not unique
    by proving I = (x) ∩ (x², y + ax) for every a ∈ k.
    Answer: (a) xy ∈ I, x ∉ I, yⁿ ∉ I; (b) I = (x) ∩ (x², y), Ass = {(x),
    (x, y)}; (c) below.
    Teacher explains: (a) √I: x² ∈ I gives x ∈ √I, and I ⊆ (x) gives √I ⊆
    (x); so √I = (x). But xy ∈ I with x ∉ I and no power yⁿ in I (every
    element of I is divisible by x): not primary. (b) Always try to "split
    off" a variable: I = x·(x, y), so I = (x) ∩ (x², y)? An element of the
    intersection is xg with xg ∈ (x², y), i.e. g(0, 0) = 0 (set y = 0: x
    g(x, 0) ∈ (x²)), so g ∈ (x, y) and xg ∈ (x², xy) ✓. (x) is prime, and
    (x², y) has radical (x, y), which is maximal, so it is primary. The
    radicals are distinct and neither component contains the other, so the
    decomposition is minimal. Ass(A/I) = {(x), (x, y)}; (x) is MINIMAL,
    (x, y) is EMBEDDED. Geometrically V(I) is the y-axis, but I remembers an
    "embedded point" at the origin. Check via (I : f): (I : y) = (x) and
    (I : x) = (x, y). (c) If xg ∈ (x², y + ax), substitute y = −ax: x g(x,
    −ax) ∈ (x²), so g(0, 0) = 0 and xg ∈ I. Conversely x² ∈ both and xy =
    x(y + ax) − ax² ∈ (x²,y + ax). And (x², y + ax) is (x, y)-primary
    (its quotient is k[x]/(x²)). So there are infinitely many embedded
    components, but the SET of radicals and the minimal component (x) are
    unique — exactly the two uniqueness theorems. Common mistakes: thinking
    "radical prime ⇒ primary" (only "radical maximal ⇒ primary" is true);
    writing I = (x) ∩ (y) (that is (xy)). Now you try: decompose (xy, y²)
    in k[x, y]. ((y) ∩ (x, y²); Ass = {(y), (x, y)})

D4. Let R = k[x, y, z]/(xy − z²) and P = (x, z) ⊆ R. (a) Show P is prime.
    (b) Show P² is not primary. (c) Find a primary decomposition of P² and
    the symbolic power P⁽²⁾ = P²R_P ∩ R.
    Answer: (a) R/P ≅ k[y]; (b) xy = z² ∈ P², x ∉ P², y ∉ P; (c) P² = (x) ∩
    𝔪² with 𝔪 = (x, y, z), and P⁽²⁾ = (x).
    Teacher explains: (a) R/P = k[x, y, z]/(xy − z², x, z) = k[y], a domain.
    (b) In R, P² = (x², xz, z²). We have x·y = z² ∈ P². Is x ∈ P²? The
    elements of P² are, modulo xy − z², combinations of monomials of degree
    ≥ 2 in the grading deg x = deg y = deg z = 1 (xy − z² is homogeneous), so
    x ∉ P². And no yⁿ lies in P² ⊆ P since y ∉ P = √(P²). So P² is not
    primary. (c) Localize at P: y ∉ P is a unit in R_P, so x = z²/y ∈ P²R_P.
    Hence P⁽²⁾ = P²R_P ∩ R ⊇ (x). Conversely (x) = (x, z²) since z² = xy,
    and R/(x) = k[y, z]/(z²) has only associated prime (z) = P/(x), so (x)
    is P-primary; it contains P² and is contained in P⁽²⁾, and P⁽²⁾ is the
    smallest P-primary ideal containing P², so P⁽²⁾ = (x). The extra
    component sits at the maximal ideal 𝔪 = (x, y, z): one checks
       P² = (x) ∩ 𝔪²  (= (x) ∩ (x², y², z², xy, xz, yz)),
    an intersection of a P-primary and an 𝔪-primary ideal (𝔪-primary since
    √(𝔪²) = 𝔪 is maximal). Indeed an element of (x) ∩ 𝔪² is x·g with xg of
    order ≥ 2, i.e. g ∈ 𝔪; and x·y = z² ∈ P², x·x, x·z ∈ P². So Ass(R/P²) =
    {P, 𝔪}: the vertex of the cone is an embedded prime. Common mistakes:
    believing powers of primes are primary (true for MAXIMAL ideals, and in
    PIDs, false in general). Now you try: in Z, is (p)ⁿ primary? (yes:
    radical (p) is maximal)

D5. (a) Prove: b ∈ B is integral over A ⇔ A[b] is a finitely generated
    A-module ⇔ b lies in a subring C ⊆ B that is a finitely generated
    A-module. Deduce that the integral elements form a ring. (b) Find the
    integral closure of Z in Q(√d), d squarefree. (c) Show that (1 + √5)/2
    is integral over Z but (1 + √3)/2 is not, and that the integral closure
    of k[t², t³] is k[t].
    Answer: (a) determinant trick; (b) Z[√d] for d ≡ 2, 3 (mod 4),
    Z[(1 + √d)/2] for d ≡ 1 (mod 4); (c) x² − x − 1 and x² − x − 1/2.
    Teacher explains: (a) If bⁿ = −(a₁bⁿ⁻¹ + ... + aₙ), then A[b] is spanned
    by 1, b, ..., bⁿ⁻¹. If C = Ac₁ + ... + Acₙ contains 1 and b, write bcᵢ =
    ∑ aᵢⱼcⱼ; then (bI − (aᵢⱼ))c = 0 for the column c, and multiplying by the
    adjugate gives det(bI − (aᵢⱼ))·cⱼ = 0 for all j, hence det(...)·1 = 0:
    a monic equation for b. If b, b′ are integral, A[b, b′] is finitely
    generated (spanned by bⁱb′ʲ), and it contains b ± b′ and bb′. (b) α = u
    + v√d (u, v ∈ Q, v ≠ 0) has minimal polynomial x² − 2ux + (u² − dv²).
    Since Z is a UFD, α is integral iff this has integer coefficients: 2u
    ∈ Z and u² − dv² ∈ Z. If u ∈ Z then dv² ∈ Z, so v ∈ Z (d squarefree).
    If u = m/2, m odd, then 4dv² ≡ m² ≡ 1 (mod 4) forces v = n/2 with n odd
    and dn² ≡ 1 (mod 4), i.e. d ≡ 1 (mod 4). (c) (1 + √5)/2 has minimal
    polynomial x² − x − 1 ∈ Z[x]: integral. (1 + √3)/2 has x² − x − 1/2:
    not integral (3 ≢ 1 mod 4). For k[t², t³] ⊆ k[t]: t = t³/t² lies in the
    fraction field and satisfies T² − t² = 0 with t² in the ring, so t is
    integral; k[t] is a UFD, hence normal, and integral over k[t², t³], so
    it is the integral closure (the normalization of the cusp y² = x³ via
    x = t², y = t³). Common mistakes: claiming Z[√5] is the ring of
    integers of Q(√5); forgetting that "integral" needs a MONIC polynomial
    (every algebraic number satisfies some integer polynomial). Now you
    try: the ring of integers of Q(√−7). (Z[(1 + √−7)/2]: −7 ≡ 1 mod 4)

D6. Let A ⊆ B be an integral extension. (a) Prove: if B is a domain, A is
    a field iff B is a field. (b) Deduce lying over and incomparability,
    and that dim A = dim B. (c) Find all primes of Z[i] lying over 2, 3, 5
    and check lying over and incomparability.
    Answer: (a) invert via the minimal equation; (b) localize at p;
    (c) over 2: (1 + i) only; over 3: (3); over 5: (2 + i), (2 − i).
    Teacher explains: (a) If A is a field and 0 ≠ b ∈ B, take a monic
    equation bⁿ + a₁bⁿ⁻¹ + ... + aₙ = 0 of least degree; aₙ ≠ 0 (else
    cancel b in the domain), so b(bⁿ⁻¹ + ... + aₙ₋₁) = −aₙ and b⁻¹ = −aₙ⁻¹(
    bⁿ⁻¹ + ... ) ∈ B. If B is a field and 0 ≠ a ∈ A, a⁻¹ ∈ B is integral:
    a⁻ᵐ + c₁a⁻ᵐ⁺¹ + ... + c_m = 0; multiply by aᵐ⁻¹ to get a⁻¹ = −(c₁ +
    c₂a + ... + c_m aᵐ⁻¹) ∈ A. Consequence: q ⊆ B prime is maximal iff q ∩ A
    is maximal (apply (a) to A/(q ∩ A) ⊆ B/q). (b) LYING OVER: for p ⊆ A,
    A_p ⊆ B_p is integral; B_p ≠ 0, take a maximal ideal n of B_p; n ∩ A_p
    is maximal by (a), so it is pA_p, and q = n ∩ B lies over p.
    INCOMPARABILITY: if q ⊊ q′ both lie over p, localize at p: then qB_p ⊊
    q′B_p both contract to the maximal ideal pA_p, so both are maximal,
    contradiction. GOING UP follows from lying over for A/p ⊆ B/q. So a
    chain in A lifts to a chain in B (dim B ≥ dim A), and a chain in B
    contracts to a chain with no equalities (dim A ≥ dim B). (c) Z[i] is a
    PID; primes over p divide pZ[i]. 2 = −i(1 + i)²: only (1 + i)
    (RAMIFIED). 3 is prime in Z[i] since 3 ≡ 3 (mod 4) is not a sum of two
    squares: only (3) (INERT). 5 = (2 + i)(2 − i), two distinct primes
    (SPLIT). Over (0) lies only (0). Each nonzero prime of Z[i] lies over
    exactly one (p) and no two comparable primes lie over the same (p):
    incomparability. Both rings have dimension 1. Common mistakes: forgetting
    that lying over needs INTEGRALITY: Z ⊆ Q is not integral and (2) has no
    prime of Q above it. Now you try: the primes of Z[i] over 13. ((3 + 2i),
    (3 − 2i))

D7. (a) Show that A = k[x, y]/(xy − 1) is finite over k[u], u = x + y,
    but not over k[x]. (b) Sketch Noether normalization. (c) Prove Zariski's
    lemma for k = C by a counting argument, and deduce the weak
    Nullstellensatz: every maximal ideal of C[x₁, ..., xₙ] is (x₁ − a₁, ...,
    xₙ − aₙ).
    Answer: (a) x² − ux + 1 = 0; y = 1/x is not integral over k[x];
    (b) change variables xᵢ ↦ xᵢ + xₙ^(rⁱ) to make f monic in xₙ;
    (c) C(t) has uncountable C-dimension.
    Teacher explains: (a) In A, x(u − x) = xy = 1, so x² − ux + 1 = 0 and
    likewise y² − uy + 1 = 0: x and y are integral over k[u], hence A =
    k[u][x] is generated by 1, x as a k[u]-module (u is transcendental,
    since A has dimension 1). Over k[x], A = k[x, 1/x]; if 1/x were integral,
    x⁻ⁿ + a₁x⁻ⁿ⁺¹ + ... + aₙ = 0 with aᵢ ∈ k[x]; multiply by xⁿ: 1 = −x(a₁ +
    ... ) ∈ (x), impossible. Geometrically: projecting the hyperbola onto
    the x-axis misses x = 0 (a finite map is surjective, by lying over),
    while projecting onto the line x + y = u is finite. (b) For one
    polynomial f ∈ k[x₁, ..., xₙ] with k infinite, a linear change xᵢ ↦ xᵢ
    + cᵢxₙ (i < n) makes the top-degree part of f have non-zero xₙᵈ
    coefficient; then f is monic in xₙ up to a constant and k[x]/(f) is
    finite over k[x₁, ..., xₙ₋₁]. In general (any k) use xᵢ ↦ xᵢ + xₙ^(rⁱ)
    with r large, and induct on the number of generators. (c) ZARISKI: if a
    field K is a finitely generated C-algebra, then K = C. K has countable
    C-dimension (spanned by the images of monomials). If t ∈ K ∖ C, t is
    transcendental (C is algebraically closed), so C(t) ⊆ K; but the
    elements 1/(t − a), a ∈ C, are linearly independent (partial fractions:
    a relation ∑cᵢ/(t − aᵢ) = 0 multiplied by t − a₁ and evaluated at a₁
    gives c₁ = 0) — uncountably many, contradiction. WEAK NSS: let 𝔪 be
    maximal. K = C[x]/𝔪 is a field, finitely generated, so K = C and xᵢ ↦
    some aᵢ ∈ C. Then xᵢ − aᵢ ∈ 𝔪, so (x₁ − a₁, ..., xₙ − aₙ) ⊆ 𝔪; the left
    side is already maximal (quotient C), so equality. Common mistakes:
    thinking every maximal ideal of R[x, y] is of this form ((x² + 1, y) is
    maximal, with quotient C); confusing "finitely generated as an algebra"
    with "finitely generated as a module" (Zariski says the first implies
    the second for fields). Now you try: give a Noether normalization of
    k[x, y]/(y² − x³). (k[x]: y is integral, y² = x³)

D8. (a) Prove the strong Nullstellensatz I(V(I)) = √I from the weak one
    (Rabinowitsch trick). (b) Find the least N with (x + y)ᴺ ∈ (x², y³) in
    C[x, y]. (c) Find √(x²y, xy²) and V of it. (d) Show that xy − 1 and y²
    have no common zero by writing 1 = g(xy − 1) + hy².
    Answer: (a) below; (b) N = 4; (c) (xy), the union of the axes;
    (d) 1 = x²y² − (xy + 1)(xy − 1).
    Teacher explains: (a) "⊇" is trivial: if fᵐ ∈ I then fᵐ, hence f,
    vanishes on V(I). "⊆": let I = (f₁, ..., f_m) (Hilbert basis) and f
    vanish on V(I). In k[x₁, ..., xₙ, y] the polynomials f₁, ..., f_m, 1 −
    yf have no common zero (where all fᵢ vanish, f vanishes, so 1 − yf = 1).
    By the weak NSS, 1 = ∑gᵢ(x, y)fᵢ + g₀(x, y)(1 − yf). Substitute y = 1/f
    (in the field k(x)) and clear denominators by fᴺ with N ≥ max deg_y gᵢ:
       fᴺ = ∑ Gᵢ(x) fᵢ(x),   so f ∈ √I.
    (b) (x + y)ᴺ = ∑ C(N, i) xⁱ yᴺ⁻ⁱ; a term lies in (x², y³) iff i ≥ 2 or N −
    i ≥ 3 (monomial ideal!). The only bad terms have i ≤ 1 and N − i ≤ 2,
    i.e. N ≤ 3 (e.g. 3xy² for N = 3). For N = 4 the terms are y⁴, 4xy³,
    and terms with x²: all in the ideal. So N = 4 (in general for (xᵃ, yᵇ)
    it is a + b − 1). (c) (xy)² = y·(x²y) ∈ I, so xy ∈ √I. And I ⊆ (xy),
    which is radical (xy is squarefree in the UFD). So √I = (xy), V = {x =
    0} ∪ {y = 0}. (d) Modulo xy − 1, xy ≡ 1 so x²y² ≡ 1; exactly x²y² − 1 =
    (xy + 1)(xy − 1), so 1 = x²·y² − (xy + 1)(xy − 1). Geometrically y = 0
    would force xy − 1 = −1 ≠ 0. The NSS guarantees such a certificate
    whenever there is no common zero over C. Common mistakes: forgetting
    the NSS needs k algebraically closed (over R, x² + y² + 1 has no zero
    but 1 ∉ (x² + y² + 1)); thinking √I is generated by the square-free
    parts of the generators of I (true for principal or monomial ideals).
    Now you try: least N with (x + y)ᴺ ∈ (x³, y³). (5)

D9. (a) Prove dim k[x₁, ..., xₙ] ≥ n and dim Z[x] ≥ 2 by exhibiting chains,
    and quote the theorem giving equality. (b) Compute dim A for A =
    k[x, y, z]/(xy, xz), find its minimal primes, and show that A has
    maximal ideals of heights 1 and 2.
    Answer: (a) (0) ⊊ (x₁) ⊊ ... ⊊ (x₁, ..., xₙ); (0) ⊊ (2) ⊊ (2, x);
    (b) dim A = 2; minimal primes (x), (y, z); (x − 1, y, z) has height 1,
    (x, y − 1, z) has height 2.
    Teacher explains: (a) Each (x₁, ..., xᵢ) is prime (quotient k[xᵢ₊₁, ...,
    xₙ]) and the inclusions are strict: a chain of length n. Equality: for
    a finitely generated k-domain A, dim A = tr.deg_k Frac(A) (via Noether
    normalization and dim A = dim B for A ⊆ B integral); here tr.deg = n.
    In Z[x]: (2) is prime (Z[x]/(2) = F₂[x]) and (2, x) is maximal (quotient
    F₂). In general dim A[x] = dim A + 1 for A Noetherian, so dim Z[x] = 2.
    The maximal ideals of Z[x] are (p, f) with f irreducible mod p; (x) is
    NOT maximal (quotient Z). (b) xy = xz = 0 means x(y, z) = 0: so (xy, xz)
    = (x) ∩ (y, z) (both prime; x·(y, z) ⊆ intersection and conversely an
    element of (x) ∩ (y, z) is xg with g ∈ (y, z) since x ∉ (y, z)). V(A) =
    the plane x = 0 ∪ the line y = z = 0. dim A = max(dim k[y, z], dim k[x])
    = max(2, 1) = 2. The point (1, 0, 0) lies only on the line: its local
    ring is the local ring of the line, ht(x − 1, y, z) = 1. The point (0,
    1, 0) lies only on the plane: height 2. So "ht 𝔪 = dim A for all
    maximal 𝔪" needs A to be a domain (or equidimensional). Common
    mistakes: adding the dimensions of components; thinking dim A[x] = dim
    A + 1 for all rings (for non-Noetherian A it can be up to 2 dim A + 1).
    Now you try: dim k[x, y, z]/(xz, yz). (2: plane z = 0 ∪ z-axis)

D10. (a) State Krull's principal ideal theorem and the height theorem.
     (b) In R = k[x, y, z, w]/(xw − yz), show P = (x, y) is a prime of
     height 1 that is not principal; deduce R is not a UFD. (c) Decide
     which of the local rings O = k[x, y]_(x,y)/(f) are regular: f = y − x²,
     y² − x³, y² − x² − x³, x³ + y⁴, y − x² − y².
     Answer: (b) R/P ≅ k[z, w] of dim 2 = 3 − 1; P needs 2 generators;
     (c) regular iff f ∉ 𝔪²: y − x² and y − x² − y² regular, others not.
     Teacher explains: (a) PIT: in a Noetherian ring, every prime minimal
     over a principal ideal (f) has height ≤ 1. HEIGHT THEOREM: minimal over
     (f₁, ..., f_r) ⇒ height ≤ r; conversely every prime of height r is
     minimal over some ideal generated by r elements. (b) R/P = k[z, w]
     (x = y = 0 kills xw − yz), a domain of dim 2. R is a 3-dimensional
     domain (xw − yz is irreducible) finitely generated over k, so ht P =
     dim R − dim R/P = 3 − 2 = 1. If P = (f), then since P, R and xw − yz
     are graded with x, y of degree 1, the degree-1 part of P/𝔪P (𝔪 = (x,
     y, z, w)) would be spanned by one element; but x and y are linearly
     independent mod 𝔪P ⊆ 𝔪² + (xw − yz) (the relation has degree 2). So P
     needs two generators. In a Noetherian UFD every height-1 prime is
     principal (take an irreducible factor of a nonzero element), so R is
     not a UFD — as also seen from xw = yz with x, y, z, w irreducible and
     non-associate. (c) O is 1-dimensional, 𝔪/𝔪² is spanned by x, y, and it
     is 1-dimensional exactly when f has a nonzero linear term. f = y − x²:
     𝔪 = (x), regular (a smooth curve). y² − x³: cusp, dim 𝔪/𝔪² = 2 > 1, not
     regular. y² − x² − x³: node, not regular. x³ + y⁴: not regular. y − x²
     − y²: linear term y, regular. REGULAR = SMOOTH POINT (Jacobian
     criterion). Common mistakes: deducing "height 1 ⇒ principal" in a
     non-UFD; thinking PIT bounds the NUMBER of minimal primes. Now you
     try: is k[x, y, z]_(x,y,z)/(x² + y² + z²) regular? (no: f ∈ 𝔪², embedding
     dim 3 > dim 2)

D11. Compute the Hilbert-Samuel function n ↦ ℓ(A/𝔪ⁿ), the dimension and
     the multiplicity e for (a) A = k[x₁, ..., x_d] localized at the
     origin; (b) the cusp A = k[x, y]_(x,y)/(y² − x³); (c) the cone A =
     k[x, y, z]_(x,y,z)/(xy − z²).
     Answer: (a) C(n + d − 1, d), e = 1; (b) 2n − 1, d = 1, e = 2; (c) n²,
     d = 2, e = 2.
     Teacher explains: For a quotient of a polynomial ring by f, localized
     at the origin, A/𝔪ⁿ = k[x]/((f) + 𝔪ⁿ), and its dimension equals that
     of k[x]/((f*) + 𝔪ⁿ), where f* is the LOWEST-degree form of f
     (tangent cone; the associated graded ring is gr A = k[x]/(f*)). (a)
     ℓ(A/𝔪ⁿ) = number of monomials of degree < n in d variables = C(n + d −
     1, d) = nᵈ/d! + ..., so the leading coefficient is 1/d!: e = 1, dim d.
     (b) f* = y². The graded piece of degree j of k[x, y]/(y²) has basis
     xʲ, xʲ⁻¹y: dimension 2 for j ≥ 1 and 1 for j = 0. So ℓ(A/𝔪ⁿ) = 1 + 2(n −
     1) = 2n − 1 (n ≥ 1): values 1, 3, 5, 7, ... . A polynomial of degree 1
     = dim A, with leading coefficient 2 = e/1!: e = 2, the order of the
     singularity. (c) f* = xy − z² itself. In degree j, the monomials of k[x,
     y, z] number C(j + 2, 2) and the multiples of f number C(j, 2), so the
     graded piece has dimension (j + 1)(j + 2)/2 − j(j − 1)/2 = 2j + 1.
     Summing j = 0, ..., n − 1: ℓ = n². Degree 2 = dim A and n² = (e/2!)n²
     gives e = 2 (a quadric cone). Common mistakes: using the highest-degree
     form of f instead of the lowest; reading e as the leading coefficient
     without the factor d!. Now you try: the node y² − x² − x³. (2n − 1 as
     well: f* = y² − x² has order 2, e = 2)

D12. (a) Show that a ∈ Z_p is a unit iff p ∤ a, and compute 1/3 in Z_5
     modulo 125 and its 5-adic digits. (b) Use Hensel's lemma to find √2 in
     Z_7 modulo 7³. (c) State Krull's intersection theorem and show that it
     fails for the ring of C^∞ germs at 0. (d) Show that the node O =
     k[x, y]_(x,y)/(y² − x² − x³) (char k ≠ 2) is a domain whose completion
     is not.
     Answer: (a) 1/3 ≡ 42 = 2 + 3·5 + 1·5² (mod 125); (b) 108 (the other
     root is 235); (c) e^(−1/x²) ∈ ⋂𝔪ⁿ; (d) y² − x²(1 + x) = (y − x√(1 +
     x))(y + x√(1 + x)) in k[[x, y]].
     Teacher explains: (a) Z_p = lim← Z/pⁿ, elements = sequences (aₙ mod pⁿ)
     compatible under reduction, or series ∑dᵢpⁱ with digits 0 ≤ dᵢ < p. If
     p ∤ a, each aₙ is invertible mod pⁿ and the inverses are compatible; if
     p | a, a·b ≡ 1 fails mod p. 3·42 = 126 ≡ 1 (mod 125) and 42 = 2 + 3·5 +
     25. (In fact 1/3 = 2 + 3·5 + 1·5² + 3·5³ + 1·5⁴ + ..., periodic digits
     3, 1.) (b) f(x) = x² − 2. Mod 7: 3² = 9 ≡ 2, and f′(3) = 6 ≢ 0: a simple
     root. Lift x = 3 + 7t: 9 + 42t ≡ 2 (mod 49) ⇔ 7 + 42t ≡ 0 ⇔ 1 + 6t ≡ 0
     (mod 7) ⇔ t = 1: x₂ = 10 (100 = 2·49 + 2 ✓). Lift x = 10 + 49s: 100 +
     980s ≡ 2 (mod 343) ⇔ 98 + 980s ≡ 0 ⇔ 2 + 20s ≡ 0 (mod 7) ⇔ 6s ≡ 5 ⇔
     s = 2: x₃ = 108; check 108² = 11664 = 34·343 + 2 ✓. Newton's step x ↦ x
     − f(x)/f′(x) doubles the precision. (c) KRULL: for A Noetherian, I an
     ideal and M finitely generated, ⋂Iⁿ M = {m : (1 + a)m = 0 for some a ∈
     I}; so ⋂Iⁿ = 0 if A is a domain (I ≠ A) or local with I ⊆ 𝔪. Proof: N =
     ⋂IⁿM satisfies IN = N by ARTIN-REES (Iⁿ M ∩ N = I^(n−c)(I^c M ∩ N)),
     then use Nakayama. Hence A embeds in its I-adic completion. For C^∞
     germs, 𝔪 = germs vanishing at 0, and 𝔪ⁿ = germs with vanishing
     derivatives up to order n − 1 (Taylor/Hadamard). φ = e^(−1/x²) has all
     derivatives 0 at 0, so φ ∈ ⋂𝔪ⁿ, φ ≠ 0: the ring is not Noetherian.
     (d) y² − x² − x³ is irreducible in k[x, y] (as a polynomial in y it
     would factor as (y − g(x))(y + g(x)) with g² = x²(1 + x), impossible in
     k[x]), so O is a domain. In k[[x]], 1 + x has a square root √(1 + x) =
     1 + x/2 − x²/8 + x³/16 − ... (Hensel: T² − (1 + x), root 1 mod x), so
     in k[[x, y]] the equation factors into the two BRANCHES y = ±x√(1 + x)
     and Ô = k[[x, y]]/(y² − x² − x³) has zero-divisors. Completion sees the
     local analytic picture: two crossing branches. Common mistakes:
     applying the basic Hensel lemma when f′(a) is not a unit (at p = 2,
     f′ = 2x never is: x² − 17 needs the stronger form |f(a)| < |f′(a)|²);
     forgetting that completion of a domain need not be a domain.
     Now you try: √−1 in Z_5 modulo 25 starting from 2. (7: 49 = 50 − 1)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Noetherian? Artinian? Answer two letters (Y/N) for each ring.
    a) Z                                b) Q
    c) Z/12                             d) Z[x]
    e) Q[x]/(x³)                        f) Q[x]/(x² − 1)
    g) Z_(p) (localization at (p))      h) Z[1/6]
    i) k[[x]]                           j) Z_p (p-adic integers)
    k) k[x₁, x₂, x₃, ...]
    l) k[x₁, x₂, ...]/(x₁, x₂, ...)² (= k ⊕ V, V² = 0, dim V = ∞)
    m) C([0, 1]), continuous real functions
    n) the ring of all algebraic integers
    o) ∏_{n∈N} F₂                       p) k[x, xy, xy², xy³, ...]
    q) Z[√−5]                           r) Z[x]/(2x, x²)
    s) the ring of germs of holomorphic functions at 0 ∈ C
    t) the ring of entire functions on C
    Answers:
      a) Y N      b) Y Y      c) Y Y      d) Y N      e) Y Y
      f) Y Y (≅ Q × Q)        g) Y N      h) Y N      i) Y N (a DVR)
      j) Y N      k) N N      l) N N (every subspace of V is an ideal)
      m) N N      n) N N ((2) ⊊ (√2) ⊊ (2^(1/4)) ⊊ ...)
      o) N N      p) N N      q) Y N (a Dedekind domain, dim 1)
      r) Y N (contains Z, dim 1)                s) Y N (a DVR)
      t) N N (Iₙ = functions vanishing at n, n + 1, n + 2, ... increase
         strictly)

M2. Artinian rings and length (k a field; dimensions over k).
    a) ℓ_Z(Z/72)                        b) ℓ_Z(Z/pᵏ)
    c) the number of maximal ideals of Z/360
    d) Z/100 as a product of local rings
    e) Q[x]/(x³ − x) as a product; its length
    f) Q[x]/(x⁴ − 1) as a product of fields
    g) Q[x]/((x − 1)²(x² + 1)): factors, length, dim_Q
    h) dim k[x, y]/(x², y²)             i) dim k[x, y]/(x, y)³
    j) dim k[x, y]/(x³, y⁴)             k) dim k[x, y]/(x², xy, y²)
    l) C[x, y]/(y − x², x³): dim and structure
    m) C[x, y]/(x² + y² − 1, x − y): dim, number of maximal ideals
    n) C[x, y]/(y − x², y): dim, number of maximal ideals
    o) C[x, y]/(y² − x³, y − x): dim and local lengths
    p) the least k with 𝔪ᵏ = 0 in k[x, y]/(x², y³), 𝔪 = (x, y)
    q) the Jacobson radical J of Z/360 and the least k with Jᵏ = 0
    r) ℓ(Z/8 × Q[x]/(x²)) (over itself)
    Answers:
      a) 5 (72 = 2³3²)     b) k         c) 3 ((2), (3), (5))
      d) Z/4 × Z/25        e) Q × Q × Q, length 3
      f) Q × Q × Q(i)      g) Q[x]/((x − 1)²) × Q(i); length 3; dim 4
      h) 4 (1, x, y, xy)   i) 6         j) 12        k) 3
      l) 3; ≅ C[x]/(x³), local        m) 2; 2 (the points ±(1, 1)/√2)
      n) 2; 1 (the parabola is tangent to y = 0)
      o) 3 (x³ = x², so C[x]/(x²(x − 1))); length 2 at (0, 0), 1 at (1, 1)
      p) 4 (xy² ≠ 0 has degree 3)
      q) J = (30); J² = (900) = (180) ≠ 0, J³ = (27000) = 0: k = 3
      r) 3 + 2 = 5

M3. Find a minimal primary decomposition (k a field, char k ≠ 2).
    a) (360) in Z                       b) (x² − 3x + 2) in Q[x]
    c) (x³ − x²) in k[x]                d) (xy) in k[x, y]
    e) (x², xy) in k[x, y]              f) (x²y, xy²) in k[x, y]
    g) (xy, xz, yz) in k[x, y, z]       h) (x − y, xy) in k[x, y]
    i) (xy, y² − y) in k[x, y]          j) (x², xy, xz) in k[x, y, z]
    k) (x(y − 1), y(x − 1)) in k[x, y]  l) (6) in Z[x]
    m) (4, 2x) in Z[x]                  n) (x² + 1) in F₅[x]
    o) (x² + 1) in F₃[x]                p) (x² + 1) in F₂[x]
    q) (x³, x²y², y³) in k[x, y]
    Answers:
      a) (8) ∩ (9) ∩ (5)                b) (x − 1) ∩ (x − 2)
      c) (x²) ∩ (x − 1)                 d) (x) ∩ (y)
      e) (x) ∩ (x², y) (embedded (x, y))
      f) (x) ∩ (y) ∩ (x, y)² (or ∩ (x², y²); embedded (x, y))
      g) (x, y) ∩ (x, z) ∩ (y, z) (the three axes)
      h) already primary: = (x − y, x²), radical (x, y)
      i) (y) ∩ (x, y − 1)
      j) (x) ∩ (x², y, z) (embedded (x, y, z))
      k) (x, y) ∩ (x − 1, y − 1) (the ideal is (x − y, x² − x))
      l) (2) ∩ (3)                      m) (2) ∩ (4, x) (embedded (2, x))
      n) (x − 2) ∩ (x − 3)              o) prime (−1 is not a square mod 3)
      p) primary: (x + 1)², radical (x + 1)
      q) already primary (radical (x, y) is maximal)

M4. For I ⊆ A give Ass(A/I), mark embedded primes (E), and say whether I is
    primary.
    a) (4) in Z                         b) (12) in Z
    c) (x²) in k[x, y]                  d) (x², xy) in k[x, y]
    e) (x, y)² in k[x, y]               f) (x²y) in k[x, y]
    g) (x²) in Z[x]                     h) (2x) in Z[x]
    i) (x², 2x) in Z[x]                 j) (9, 3x, x²) in Z[x]
    k) P² in k[x, y, z]/(xy − z²), P = (x, z)
    l) (x³ − x) in k[x]
    m) the zero-divisors of Z/360 and of k[x, y]/(x², xy)
    n) (I : y) and (I : x) for I = (x², xy)
    Answers:
      a) {(2)}; primary         b) {(2), (3)}; not
      c) {(x)}; primary         d) {(x), (x, y) E}; not
      e) {(x, y)}; primary      f) {(x), (y)}; not (no embedded prime)
      g) {(x)}; primary (zero-divisors of Z[x]/(x²) are multiples of x)
      h) {(2), (x)}; not        i) {(x), (2, x) E}; not
      j) {(3, x)}; primary (= (3, x)², radical maximal)
      k) {P, (x, y, z) E}; not (D4)
      l) {(x), (x − 1), (x + 1)}; not
      m) (2) ∪ (3) ∪ (5) (mod 360); (x, y)
      n) (x) and (x, y): exactly the associated primes

M5. Integral over Z? (Y/N) Then integral closures.
    a) √7                                b) 1/2
    c) (1 + √7)/2                        d) (1 + √−3)/2
    e) (1 + √13)/2                       f) ∛2 + √2
    g) (√2 + √6)/2                       h) e^(2πi/5)
    i) π                                 j) (3 + √5)/2
    k) 1/√2                              l) cos(2π/7), 2cos(2π/7)
    m) the integral closure of Z in Q(√−5), Q(√13), Q(√−3), Q(√12)
    n) the normalization of k[x, y]/(y² − x²(x + 1))
    o) the normalization of k[x, y]/(y² − x⁵)
    p) Is k[x, y]/(y² − x) normal?       q) Is Z[√8] normal?
    r) Is Z[x] integral over Z?
    s) Is k[x, y]/(xy − 1) integral over k[x]? over k[x + y]?
    Answers:
      a) Y (x² − 7)       b) N        c) N (x² − x − 3/2)
      d) Y (x² − x + 1)   e) Y (x² − x − 3)   f) Y (sum of integers)
      g) Y (x⁴ − 4x² + 1: α² = 2 + √3)        h) Y (x⁴ + x³ + x² + x + 1)
      i) N (transcendental)                   j) Y (x² − 3x + 1)
      k) N (x² − 1/2)
      l) N (8x³ + 4x² − 4x − 1); Y (x³ + x² − 2x − 1)
      m) Z[√−5]; Z[(1 + √13)/2]; Z[(1 + √−3)/2]; Z[√3] (Q(√12) = Q(√3))
      n) k[t], t = y/x, x = t² − 1, y = t(t² − 1)
      o) k[t], t = y/x², x = t², y = t⁵
      p) Y (≅ k[y])       q) N (√2 = √8/2 is integral, not in Z[√8])
      r) N                s) N; Y

M6. Primes lying over (Cohen-Seidenberg).
    a) primes of Z[i] over 2, 3, 5, 7, 13, 17, 29, 41 (split S, inert I,
       ramified R)
    b) primes of Z[ω], ω = (−1 + √−3)/2, over 2, 3, 7, 13
    c) primes of Z[√−5] over 2, 3, 5, 7, 11
    d) primes of k[x, y]/(y² − x) (char k ≠ 2) over (x), (x − 1)
    e) primes of R[x, y]/(y² − x) and C[x, y]/(y² − x) over (x + 1)
    f) Z ⊆ Z[1/2]: primes over (2)? Why is lying over not violated?
    g) the number of primes of Z[i] over (0); over (p)
    h) A = k[x] ⊆ B = k[x] × k[x]/(x) (integral): primes of B over (x), and
       does going down hold?
    i) dim Z[i], dim Z[∛2], dim k[t] over k[t², t³]
    Answers:
      a) 2: (1 + i) R; 3: I; 5: (2 ± i) S; 7: I; 13: (3 ± 2i) S;
         17: (4 ± i) S; 29: (5 ± 2i) S; 41: (5 ± 4i) S
      b) 2: I (N(a + bω) = a² − ab + b² ≠ 2); 3: (1 − ω), R (3 = −ω²(1 −
         ω)²); 7: (3 + ω), (3 + ω̄), S; 13: (4 + ω), (4 + ω̄), S
      c) 2: (2, 1 + √−5), R; 3: (3, 1 ± √−5), S; 5: (√−5), R;
         7: (7, 3 ± √−5), S; 11: I (−5 is not a square mod 11)
      d) over (x): one, (x, y) (ramified); over (x − 1): (x − 1, y ∓ 1), two
      e) over R: one, (x + 1, y² + 1) with residue field C; over C: two,
         (x + 1, y ∓ i)
      f) none; Z[1/2] is not integral over Z
      g) one ((0)); one or two (at most [Q(i) : Q] = 2)
      h) two: (x) × k[x]/(x) and k[x] × 0; the second is a minimal prime of
         B lying over (x) and no prime inside it lies over (0): going down
         FAILS (B is not a domain)
      i) 1; 1; 1 (integral extensions preserve dimension)

M7. Nullstellensatz, radicals and zero sets (over C unless stated).
    a) the maximal ideals of C[x]      b) is (x² + 1) maximal in R[x]? in C[x]?
    c) is (x² − 2, y) maximal in Q[x, y]? Is it of the form (x − a, y − b)?
    d) √(x³) in k[x]                   e) √(x²y) in k[x, y]
    f) √(x², y²)                       g) √((x − 1)²(x + 2)³) in k[x]
    h) √(xy, y²)                       i) least N: (x + y)ᴺ ∈ (x³, y⁴)
    j) least N: (x + y + z)ᴺ ∈ (x², y², z²)
    k) V(x² + y² − 1, x − y)            l) V(x² + y² − 1, x² + y² − 4) and a
                                           certificate 1 = g f₁ + h f₂
    m) is x ∈ √(x² − y, y²)? least power in the ideal
    n) is x + 1 ∈ √(x² + 1) in C[x]?   o) #V(y − x², y − 1)
    p) V(y − x², y) and √(y − x², y)   q) I(V) for V = the union of the axes
    r) (x² + y²) in R[x, y]: prime? V_R? I(V_R)?
    s) nilradical and Jacobson radical of k[x] and of k[x]_(x)
    t) 1 as a combination of x² − y and xy − 1, x (no common zero?)
    Answers:
      a) (x − a), a ∈ C                b) Y; N ((x − i)(x + i))
      c) Y (quotient Q(√2)); N         d) (x)
      e) (xy)      f) (x, y)           g) ((x − 1)(x + 2))
      h) (y)       i) 6 (= 3 + 4 − 1)  j) 4 (xyz appears for N = 3)
      k) {(1/√2, 1/√2), (−1/√2, −1/√2)}
      l) ∅; 1 = (f₁ − f₂)/3            m) Y; x⁴ = (x² + y)(x² − y) + y²
      n) N ((x² + 1) is radical)       o) 2: (±1, 1)
      p) {(0, 0)}; (x, y) ≠ (y − x², y) = (y, x²)
      q) (xy)
      r) prime (irreducible over R); V_R = {0}; I(V_R) = (x, y) ≠
         √(x² + y²) = (x² + y²): the NSS fails over R
      s) both 0 for k[x] (a Jacobson ring); 0 and (x) for k[x]_(x) (not
         Jacobson — not finitely generated over k)
      t) no common zero (x = 0 forces xy − 1 = −1): 1 = y·x − (xy − 1)

M8. Krull dimension.
    a) Z          b) a field       c) Z/12        d) k[x, y, z]
    e) Z[x]       f) Z[x, y]       g) k[x, y]/(xy)
    h) k[x, y, z]/(xy, xz)         i) k[x, y, z]/(xy, z)
    j) k[[x, y]]  k) Z_p           l) Z_(p)       m) Q[x]/(x²)
    n) Z[i]       o) k[x, y, z, w]/(xw − yz)
    p) k[x, y]_(x,y) and k[x, y]_(x)               q) Z[x]/(2x)
    r) C[x, y, z]/(x² + y² + z² − 1, x)
    s) R[x, y]/(x² + y² + 1)       t) Q[x, y]/(x², y²)
    u) k[x₁, ..., xₙ]/(f), f non-constant
    v) C[x, y, z]/((x, y) ∩ (z))   w) Z_(p)[x]
    Answers:
      a) 1       b) 0       c) 0       d) 3       e) 2       f) 3
      g) 1       h) 2       i) 1       j) 2       k) 1       l) 1
      m) 0       n) 1       o) 3       p) 2 and 1
      q) 1 (Z[x]/(2) = F₂[x] and Z[x]/(x) = Z, both of dim 1)
      r) 1 (the circle y² + z² = 1)
      s) 1 (Krull dimension does not care that there are no real points)
      t) 0       u) n − 1   v) 2 (plane z = 0 ∪ z-axis)
      w) 2 (chain (0) ⊊ (p) ⊊ (p, x))

M9. Heights, principal ideal theorem, regularity. For i)-q) give
    (embedding dimension, dim, regular Y/N).
    a) ht (x, y) in k[x, y, z]          b) ht (x², y³) in k[x, y]
    c) ht (xy) in k[x, y]               d) ht (xy, xz) in k[x, y, z]
    e) ht (2, x) in Z[x]                f) ht (x) and ht (2) in Z[x]
    g) ht (x, y) in k[x, y, z, w]/(xw − yz)
    h) ht (x, y, z, w) in the same ring
    i) k[x, y]_(0)/(y² − x³)            j) k[x, y]_(0)/(y² − x² − x³)
    k) k[x, y]_(0)/(y − x³)             l) k[x, y, z]_(0)/(xy − z²)
    m) k[x, y, z]_(0)/(z − xy)          n) Z_(p)
    o) Z[x]_(p,x)/(x² − p)              p) Z[√−3] at the prime (2, 1 + √−3)
    q) k[x, y]_(0)/(x², y)
    r) the least number of equations f₁, ..., f_r that can cut out a curve
       set-theoretically in C³ (lower bound from the height theorem)
    s) can V(f) ⊆ C³ (one equation) be a single point?
    t) ht (px − 1) in Z_(p)[x], and dim Z_(p)[x]/(px − 1)
    Answers:
      a) 2       b) 2       c) 1
      d) 1 (minimal primes (x) of ht 1 and (y, z) of ht 2)
      e) 2       f) 1, 1    g) 1 (D10)                h) 3
      i) (2, 1, N)          j) (2, 1, N)              k) (1, 1, Y)
      l) (3, 2, N)          m) (2, 2, Y)              n) (1, 1, Y)
      o) (1, 1, Y) (p = x² ∈ 𝔪², so 𝔪 = (x): a DVR)
      p) (2, 1, N) (Z[√−3] is not normal)
      q) (1, 0, N) (Artinian, 𝔪 = (x) ≠ 0)
      r) at least 2 (a component of V(f₁) has dim ≥ 2)
      s) no: every component of V(f) has dimension 2
      t) 1 and 0 (the quotient is Q), although dim Z_(p)[x] = 2: ht p +
         dim A/p = dim A FAILS here

M10. Hilbert-Samuel functions ℓ(A/𝔪ⁿ) (or ℓ(A/Iⁿ)), dimension d and
     multiplicity e; all rings localized at the origin (k a field).
     a) k[x]                            b) k[x, y]
     c) k[x, y, z]                      d) k[x, y]/(y − x²)
     e) k[x, y]/(y² − x³)               f) k[x, y]/(y³ − x⁵)
     g) k[x, y]/(xy(x − y))             h) k[x, y]/(x⁴ + y⁴ + x⁵)
     i) k[x, y, z]/(xy − z²)            j) k[x, y, z]/(x³ + y³ + z³)
     k) k[x, y]/(x², y²)                l) Z_(p)
     m) A = k[x, y]_(0), I = (x², y²): ℓ(A/Iⁿ) and e(I)
     n) A = k[x, y]_(0), I = (x, y²): e(I)
     Answers:
       a) n; d = 1, e = 1               b) n(n + 1)/2; 2, 1
       c) n(n + 1)(n + 2)/6; 3, 1       d) n; 1, 1
       e) 2n − 1 (n ≥ 1); 1, 2          f) 3n − 3 (n ≥ 2); 1, 3
       g) 3n − 3 (n ≥ 2); 1, 3          h) 4n − 6 (n ≥ 3); 1, 4
       i) n²; 2, 2                      j) 1 + 3n(n − 1)/2; 2, 3
       k) 1, 3, 4, 4, ...; d = 0, e = 4 = ℓ(A)
       l) n; 1, 1
       m) 2n(n + 1) (= 4·C(n + 1, 2)); e(I) = 4 = ℓ(A/I)
       n) 2

M11. Completions and Hensel's lemma.
     a) 1/2 in Z₃ modulo 27             b) the 5-adic digits of −1
     c) 1/(1 − x) in k[[x]]             d) 1/(1 + x + x²) in k[[x]] to x⁵
     e) is 2 + x a unit in Q[[x]]? in Z[[x]]? in Z₃[[x]]?
     f) √(1 + x) in Q[[x]] to x³        g) √2 in Z₇ modulo 49
     h) √−1 in Z₅ modulo 125            i) does 3 have a square root in Z₅?
     j) √7 in Z₃ modulo 9               k) ∛2 in Z₅ modulo 25
     l) the 2-adic digits of 1/3        m) the (6)-adic completion of Z
     n) the (x(x − 1))-adic completion of k[x]
     o) the completion of k[x, y]/(xy) at (x, y)
     p) ⋂ₙ(2ⁿ) in Z; ⋂ₙ 2ⁿ(Z/12) in Z/12
     q) the (2)-adic completion of Z/12
     r) is Z_p flat over Z? Q ⊗_Z Z_p = ?
     s) the (x² + 1)-adic completion of Q[x]
     Answers:
       a) 14 (2·14 = 28)                b) 4, 4, 4, ... (−1 = ∑4·5ⁱ)
       c) ∑xⁿ                           d) 1 − x + x³ − x⁴ + ... ((1 − x)/(1 −
                                           x³))
       e) Y; N (2 is not a unit of Z); Y
       f) 1 + x/2 − x²/8 + x³/16        g) 10 or 39
       h) 57 or 68 (2 → 7 → 57)         i) N (3 is not a square mod 5)
       j) 4 or 5                        k) 3 (unique: f′(3) = 27 ≢ 0)
       l) 1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 1, 0, ... (1/3 = 1 + 2 + 2³ + 2⁵ + ...)
       m) Z₂ × Z₃ (CRT at each level)   n) k[[x]] × k[[x − 1]]
       o) k[[x, y]]/(xy)                p) 0; the ideal (4) = 4Z/12 (Krull:
          elements killed by 1 + a, a ∈ (2): 4·(1 + 2) = 12 ≡ 0)
       q) Z/4 (= lim Z/(12, 2ⁿ))
       r) Y (completion of a Noetherian ring is flat); Q_p
       s) Q(i)[[t]], t = x² + 1 (Hensel lifts a root of T² + 1)

M12. True (T) or false (F)? Give a counterexample for F.
     a) A subring of a Noetherian ring is Noetherian.
     b) A quotient or a localization of a Noetherian ring is Noetherian.
     c) Every Artinian ring is Noetherian.
     d) Every Artinian module is Noetherian.
     e) Every Noetherian ring has finite Krull dimension.
     f) A power of a prime ideal is primary.
     g) An ideal whose radical is prime is primary.
     h) An ideal whose radical is maximal is primary.
     i) Minimal primary decompositions are unique.
     j) The associated primes of A/I are uniquely determined by I.
     k) If A ⊆ B is integral, B a domain and A a field, B is a field.
     l) Going down holds for every integral extension A ⊆ B.
     m) Every UFD is integrally closed.
     n) Z[√−3] is integrally closed.
     o) Every maximal ideal of Q[x, y] is (x − a, y − b) with a, b ∈ Q.
     p) If k = k̄ and f vanishes on V(I), then f ∈ I.
     q) dim A[x] = dim A + 1 for every Noetherian ring A.
     r) In a Noetherian domain every height-1 prime is principal.
     s) The completion of a Noetherian local domain is a domain.
     t) ⋂𝔪ⁿ = 0 in every local ring (A, 𝔪).
     u) Every regular local ring is a domain.
     v) ht p + dim A/p = dim A for every prime p of a Noetherian domain A.
     Answers:
       a) F: k[x, xy, xy², ...] ⊆ k[x, y]          b) T
       c) T (Akizuki-Hopkins)
       d) F: the Prüfer group Z[1/p]/Z as a Z-module
       e) F: Nagata's example (local rings have finite dimension)
       f) F: P = (x, z) in k[x, y, z]/(xy − z²)    g) F: (x², xy)
       h) T       i) F: (x) ∩ (x², y) = (x) ∩ (x², y + x)
       j) T (first uniqueness theorem)             k) T
       l) F: k[x] ⊆ k[x] × k[x]/(x) (M6 h)); true if A normal, B a domain
       m) T       n) F: (1 + √−3)/2 is integral
       o) F: (x² − 2, y)
       p) F: I = (x²), f = x (only a power of f lies in I)
       q) T       r) F: (x, y) in k[x, y, z, w]/(xw − yz)
       s) F: the node y² − x² − x³ (D12)
       t) F: C^∞ germs, e^(−1/x²) ∈ ⋂𝔪ⁿ (T for Noetherian local rings)
       u) T       v) F: A = Z_(p)[x], p = (px − 1): 1 + 0 ≠ 2 (M9 t))

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: work first in k[x, y] with MONOMIAL ideals, where everything
  can be drawn as a staircase: the length of k[x, y]/I is the number of
  boxes under the staircase, the radical is read off from the axes, and
  primary decomposition is "one piece per corner"; then move to Z and Z[i]
  (primary decomposition = prime factorization, lying over = factoring p).
  Always translate to geometry: primes ↔ irreducible subvarieties, embedded
  primes ↔ fuzzy points, integral ↔ finite projection, dimension ↔ number
  of parameters, completion ↔ looking through a microscope at one point.
  Before quoting a theorem, check its hypotheses aloud (Noetherian?
  integral? k algebraically closed? A normal?) — every standard
  counterexample above breaks exactly one of them. Harder: prove
  Lasker-Noether via irreducible ideals and the characterization Ass =
  {primes of the form (I : x)}; prove the Artin-Rees lemma via the blow-up
  algebra ⊕Iⁿ and deduce exactness of completion; prove Krull's principal
  ideal theorem with symbolic powers and lengths; prove Noether
  normalization over a finite field; prove that the Hilbert-Samuel
  function is eventually a polynomial of degree dim A (the dimension
  theorem dim = δ = d); show that regular local rings are domains and that
  a Noetherian local ring is regular iff its completion is; and construct
  Nagata's Noetherian ring of infinite dimension.
